Tennessee Cannabis & Hemp — Deep Research, Parts 0–12 (Combined)

Last audited: 28 October 2026. Original research date: 25 October 2026. See Part A (Audit) immediately below for every verdict, every correction applied, and everything still unverified.

PART A — AUDIT (28 October 2026) — read before relying on this file

Last audited: 28 October 2026. This file is an independent audit and corrected rewrite of the Tennessee Cannabis & Hemp deep research report, Parts 0–12, originally compiled 25 October 2026 (521,982 bytes, 42 tables, 196 links). The auditor did not write the original. Every section was checked against primary sources only — the Tennessee Code via the state's official public access site, General Assembly bill and public chapter pages (capitol.tn.gov / wapp.capitol.tn.gov), the Rules and Regulations of the State of Tennessee (Secretary of State), TABC, TDA, the Department of Revenue, the Department of Health, TBI, Medical Cannabis Commission pages, Attorney General opinions, court records, USDA AMS, and official county, metropolitan government and city sites and codes. Nothing was confirmed from news, law-firm blogs, trade press or AI summaries; where only a secondary source exists the claim is marked Unverified.

Federal baseline — used as given, not re-audited. Scheduling is split: under a final rule at 91 FR 22714 (effective 28 April 2026), FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana are in Schedule III; all other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is NOT final. IRS §280E: Treasury has said rescheduling generally removes §280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances (guidance forthcoming); adult-use activity remains fully subject to §280E. Hemp: Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesised; the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA has delayed enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement until 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. Any statement in the original that contradicted this baseline was corrected to match it. Tennessee application — the decisive point, confirmed: Tennessee has no state-licensed medical programme, so marijuana remains Schedule I in Tennessee for federal purposes (and Schedule VI under Tennessee state law, §39-17-415), and the Schedule III medical carve-out creates no Tennessee licensee. Tennessee's only lawful intoxicating-cannabis market is hemp-derived cannabinoid products (HDCPs) under Title 57, Chapter 7 — and, as §1.5.8 explains, nearly every product lawful under Tennessee's 15 mg serving rule will be outside the federal definition of hemp after 11 December 2026 while remaining lawful under Tennessee law on a TABC-licensed shelf, unless Tennessee or Congress changes the law. No Tennessee adoption of the 0.4 mg cap and no TABC federal-date notice was located as of 25 October 2026; the TDA 14 November 2025 'programme will remain unchanged' notice predates the baseline dates and does not address the post-11 December 2026 cap.

Material limitation — read this before citing any statute quotation. Tennessee's official Code publisher is Lexis (advance.lexis.com). That site could not be opened directly in this audit environment, as in the original research. Statute quotations in this file were read from the Justia 2025 Tennessee Code reproduction, LawServer and FindLaw, and bill/amendment text was read directly from official capitol.tn.gov / wapp.capitol.tn.gov pages and PDFs. Every statute quotation resting only on a reproduction is therefore marked Unverified in the audit table, even where mirrors agree word-for-word. That is a limitation of this audit, not proof the quotation is wrong — but no such quotation should be relied on as a legal authority until it is re-checked against the official Lexis text or a Secretary of State chaptered PDF. Bill pages, fiscal notes, TABC/TDA/DOR pages and USDA pages cited as 'official, opened' were opened. Where two audit passes reached different conclusions on the same point (notably the §57-7-203 vs §57-7-103 seizure/forfeiture citation, and the SB 1603 / PC 698 / PC 1127 chapter numbers, where the Parts 0–3 pass had not opened the enrolment/chaptered text but later passes opened SOS act PDFs), this file follows the later, primary-source check and says so in the row — it does not silently pick a winner.

Audit verdict counts (sum exactly to the rows in the table below)

Confirmed: 34  |  Corrected: 40  |  Removed: 2  |  Unverified: 32  |  Total rows audited: 108. 'Confirmed' means only that the primary passage actually read in this audit says it. 'Corrected' means the claim was wrong or incomplete and the corrected wording in the table is the version used in the rewritten report below. 'Removed' means the claim was wrong and no correct version was found, or it must not be presented as Tennessee law at all. 'Unverified' means only a secondary source exists, or the primary passage was not opened — it is not a finding that the claim is false. Routine restatements of the same fee, date or count in several sections are grouped into one row per distinct claim, so the table records every material claim once; the rewritten report below applies each correction everywhere that claim appears.

Headline corrections applied in the rewrite (in one place, for checking)

  • Implied retail sales corrected: $209,567,933 (not $209,567,933) — the old figure divided the FY 2025–26 estimate, not FY 2023–24 collections, by 6%.
  • SB 1603 is enacted: 2026 Pub. Ch. 789, effective 23 April 2026 (§39-17-403(d)(2) rescheduling bar). The TACIR study was as-introduced only and is not law; all TACIR deadlines are removed as law.
  • PC 698 narrowed: bars TDA from issuing/renewing HDCP supplier and retail licences only (not wholesaler licences); warning minimum is 6-point, not 11-point, under PC 698 (statute controls over the rule text as read).
  • PC 1127 confirmed: 10% wholesale tax on inhalable cartridge-form HDCPs, additional to $4.40/gallon, effective 1 July 2026; FM3187's 80/10/10 allocation applies to that 10% tax only, not to the base wholesale tax.
  • Commission confirmed: PC 577 (SB 118/HB 490, signed/effective 27 May 2021); PC 50 extends it to 30 June 2029; PC 817 (effective 1 July 2026) takes it from 9 to 10 members (10th nonvoting).
  • Low-THC history corrected: 2014 PC 936; 2015 SB 280/HB 197, PC 352, signed/effective 4 May 2015 (not 5 or 7 May); 2016 HB 2144/SB 2125, PC 1083, <0.6% THC as enacted — and there is no 'Tennessee Alternative Treatment Act' title in the official fiscal note or enacted summary.
  • Ballot bills died: SB 0960/HB 1370 (3 questions) and HB 2238/SB 2097 (1 question) both died in committee in March 2026; no November 2026 ballot question resulted. SB 459 is deadline-only (deferred to summer study 25 Mar 2026); the 99-page draft programme is unfiled study material, not SB 459 and not law.
  • Tribal correction: no Tennessee-headquartered tribe or Tennessee tribal programme — but MBCI trust land in Lauderdale County, TN (2012–2013) is part of the Mississippi Band of Choctaw Indians reservation; 'no tribal reservation' as written was wrong.
  • AG Opinion 06-165 corrected: 89 individuals' recognition remained in force; the one organisation's recognition had effectively expired after 1995 — not a blanket 'no organisation' holding. AG citations corrected to Op. 23-004 (28 Feb 2023), Op. 14-086, Op. 08-022, all labelled opinion, not law.
  • Grower rules corrected: Part 11 citations to Chapter 0080-06-28 (2021) are superseded as current law by Chapter 0080-10-01 (predecessor only; full current text not yet read).
  • Licence counts decided, not blended: pre-transition official = 2,634 retailers + 230 suppliers/wholesalers combined (2025 fiscal note, TDA era). Current = secondary 1 Oct 2026 licences ~1,990 retailers / ~67 wholesalers / 184 suppliers (not businesses, not official totals). July 2026 snapshots, the ~4,400 'businesses identified' sweep and DOR registrant counts are different populations/dates and are never summed with either.
  • Brand fee confirmed: $300 per brand registration by category/type/delivery system (Form HDC 101), first due 1 Jan 2026, then each 1 Jan — not 'secondary' and not per-SKU.
  • Business tax confirmed: minimal-activity licence for more than $3,000 but less than $100,000; standard licence at $100,000 or more (Pub. Ch. 377, 2023); $15 clerk fee. Sales-tax clarification: 7% state + local option on the first $1,600 of a single article, plus a separate state 2.75% single-article tax on the $1,600.01–$3,200 slice only (max $44).
  • NASS decided: Tennessee floral figures confirmed for 2023–2025 (including $51.20/lb in 2025 — arithmetic checks); all-utilisations planted 335 acres (2023) and 315 acres (2024) confirmed, but 2025 planted acres are not asserted (text layer blank/withheld) and the secondary synthesis's 335 planted (2025) is rejected; all grower counts are secondary (including the 1,852 vs 1,859 conflict for 2020, shown as differing secondary reports, neither official).
  • Properties: Part 8 headline stands — ZERO verified cannabis-zoned Tennessee properties. Crossville listing status corrected (Date on Market 31 Jan 2025, Last Updated blank — current status unconfirmed); broker phone numbers are not reproduced.
  • Events downgraded: TSU Small Farm Expo — the TSU page confirms only an Industrial Hemp Field Tour; its 2026 date, hours and venue are unverified and the 'only fully official, dated event' claim is deleted.
  • Seizure/forfeiture citation left flagged, not silently 'fixed': TABC FAQ Q14 says §57-7-203(a)(2); the HB 1376 amendment text points to §57-7-103(a)(2). Lexis / enrolled PC 526 text is the final arbiter.
  • Employee age synthesised, not 'picked': under-18 (§57-7-103(b)(7)) and under-21 retailer-access (HB 0072, 2025) prohibitions coexist; for retailers the stricter under-21 rule controls in practice; the codification pinpoint stays open.
  • Hemani stated precisely: No. 24-1234, decided 18 June 2026 — 7 joined the opinion of the Court; all nine agreed on the judgment (affirmance). Not '7–2', not unqualified 'unanimous'. It creates no Tennessee medical-marijuana firearms safe harbour.

Part 2.2Ordinary sales tax: 7% state + up to 2.75% local; typical combined 9.25%-9.75%Correctedhttps://www.tn.gov/revenue/taxes/business-tax/registration-and-licensing.html (DOR pages as returned); §67-6-202State 7% + local option (levied on the first $1,600 of a single article; local rates vary, commonly 2.25%-2.75%, giving 9.25%-9.75% combined on low-priced items) PLUS, on a single article priced $1,600.01-$3,200, a separate state 2.75% single-article tax on that slice only (max $44). The Part 0 shorthand '7% + up to 2.75% local' must not be read as the single-article tax — they are different taxes.
Part 2.3Business tax: minimal-activity licence $3,000-$99,999 gross receipts/location; standard licence + state return $100,000+/location; thresholds raised by 2023 Pub. Ch. 377Confirmedhttps://www.tn.gov/revenue/taxes/business-tax/registration-and-licensing.htmlRemove the report's Verify flag. DOR states: separate $15 fee to county and/or municipal clerk per new business; in-state more than $3,000 but less than $100,000 = minimal-activity licence; $100,000 or more = standard licence; $100,000 thresholds updated by Pub. Ch. 377 (2023), tax years ending on/after 31 Dec 2023. At exactly $3,000 no licence is required.

Part 2.5FY 2023-24 former 6% tax collections $12,574,076; implied FY 2023-24 HDCP taxable retail sales $209,567,933 ($12,574,076 ÷ 6%); FY 2025-26 estimate $13,607,203 (pre-repeal baseline, not a collection)Correctedhttps://capitol.tn.gov/Bills/114/Fiscal/SB1413.PDF ; https://capitol.tn.gov/Bills/114/Fiscal/FM1177.pdf (cited, not opened in this audit)Implied FY 2023-24 taxable retail sales = $209,567,933 ($12,574,076 ÷ 6%). The report's $209,567,933 is $13,607,203 ÷ 6% — it divides the FY 2025-26 estimate, not FY 2023-24 collections, by 6%. The $13,607,203 estimate arithmetic itself checks ($12,574,076 × 1.0481 × 1.0325 = $13,607,203), but it is a pre-repeal estimate, NOT a collection, and must never be quoted as one. Replace $209,567,933 everywhere it appears. The $12,574,076 figure itself was not re-opened in this audit and stays fiscal-note-sourced via Part 2.

Method note on counts: licence, tax-collection, acreage and grower-count figures were reconciled by counting unit and date (licences vs businesses vs locations vs tax registrants vs filers; TDA era vs TABC era; snapshot date), not by averaging or by picking the largest figure. Where the authoritative source was not opened (Finance tables, TABC wholesaler/retailer PDFs, Lexis), this audit refuses to pick a single figure and says so, rather than presenting a secondary figure as official.

COVER — what this file is, and what it is not

This file covers Parts 0–12 in full: Parts 0–6 from Run 1 (carried forward in substance, with their tables, source links, and accessed dates) plus Parts 7–12 from Run 2, followed by a combined research notes section covering both runs. Research date / date accessed for sources: 25 October 2026, unless a source itself is dated otherwise. Place scope: Tennessee only. Same-name places elsewhere (Memphis, Egypt; Nashville, NC/GA; Jackson, MS; Franklin, OH; Cleveland, OH; etc.) were excluded where they appeared in searches.

Statute quotations in this file were read from the Justia 2025 Tennessee Code reproduction and LawServer, and bill/amendment text was read directly from official capitol.tn.gov / wapp.capitol.tn.gov pages and PDFs. Every statute quotation must be re-checked against the official Lexis text / Secretary of State publication before this file is relied on as a legal authority. That limitation is flagged throughout, not hidden.

Unavailable = searched for and not located (a search result as of 25 October 2026, not a proved negative unless stated otherwise). Secondary = a non-law source used only for a non-law fact (market figure, poll, event, bill-tracker vote) with its date given. Nothing marked Verify/Unavailable is asserted as settled law. No proposed, draft, or unfiled bill is presented as current law — pending/dead/draft items are labelled as such wherever they appear. Federal baseline — fixed context, as given (not re-researched)

  • Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final.
  • IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.
  • Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.
  • USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. There is no federal cannabis banking law.
  • Tennessee application — the decisive point: Tennessee has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme (Part 1.4). Therefore the Schedule III move has no Tennessee licensee to attach to, and marijuana remains Schedule I in Tennessee for federal purposes and Schedule VI under Tennessee state law (Tenn. Code Ann. §39-17-415). No Tennessee source located contradicts the federal dates above. The TDA 14 Nov 2025 'programme will remain unchanged' notice predates the baseline dates and does not address the post-11 Dec 2026 cap; no Tennessee adoption of the 0.4 mg cap or TABC federal-date notice was located as of 25 Oct 2026. Tennessee's only lawful intoxicating-cannabis market is hemp-derived cannabinoid products (HDCPs) under Title 57, Chapter 7 — and, as Part 1.5.8 explains, nearly every product lawful under Tennessee's 15 mg serving rule will be outside the federal definition of hemp after 11 December 2026 while remaining lawful under Tennessee law on a TABC-licensed shelf, unless Tennessee or Congress changes the law.

THCA / “THCA flower” — the press shorthand, correctedThere is no blanket ban on the THCa molecule at any level in Title 57, Ch. 7. THCa above 0.3% dry weight, and any product above 0.3% total / total theoretical THC, is excluded and its manufacture/sale is a Class A misdemeanour (§57-7-114). In practice that bans marketable high-THCa flower, pre-rolls and concentrates, which routinely test far above 0.3%. The July 2026 “ban” in press coverage is the full-effect date of the TABC regime, not a new THCa section.§§57-7-102(3)(C)(xi), 57-7-114 — HB 1376 enrolled text, opened.

Whether a given commercial lot (typically made by isomerising CBD) is a banned “synthetic cannabinoid” under §57-7-102(10) turns on “derived from hemp,” which the statute does not define for conversion. PART 1 — LAWS

1.1 Legal status — confirmed from statute

Recreational: illegal. Medical: no comprehensive programme. Low-THC/CBD oil only: a narrow possession exclusion (not a programme). Illegal otherwise. Marijuana and tetrahydrocannabinols are Schedule VI controlled substances. There is no statute authorising any marijuana product type — flower, edibles, vapes, concentrates or beverages — for recreational or general medical sale in Tennessee. FDA-approved prescription products are excluded from “marijuana” and lawful only as prescribed/dispensed under FDA approval.

Task-brief citation correction: no low-THC provision at §39-17-106 was located. The low-THC exception lives in §39-17-402(16)(E)–(F) and §68-7-101. Do not cite §39-17-106 for low-THC oil unless official Lexis text shows otherwise — Verify.

1.3 Possession, cultivation, paraphernalia, public consumption, product types (marijuana)

Cultivation: there is no standalone cultivation section. “Manufacture” (§39-17-402(15)) includes production/preparation/propagation/compounding/conversion/processing, and “production” (§39-17-402(24)) includes “planting, cultivating, growing or harvesting” — so home cultivation of marijuana is charged as manufacture under §39-17-417.

§39-17-417(a): “It is an offense for a defendant to knowingly: (1) Manufacture a controlled substance; (2) Deliver a controlled substance; (3) Sell a controlled substance; or (4) Possess a controlled substance with intent to manufacture, deliver or sell the controlled substance.”
§39-17-418(a): “It is an offense for a person to knowingly possess or casually exchange a controlled substance, unless the substance was obtained directly from, or pursuant to, a valid prescription or order of a practitioner while acting in the course of professional practice.” §39-17-418(b): “It is an offense for a person to distribute a small amount of marijuana not in excess of one-half (½) ounce (14.175 grams).” §39-17-418(c)(1): “Except as provided in subsections (d) and (e), a violation of this section is a Class A misdemeanor.”
ConductAmount / plantsClassStatute fine (max, in §39-17-417)Custodial span (all Ranges, §40-35-112)
Simple possession or casual exchange; distribute ≤½ oz (14.175 g)Any amount possessed (simple possession has no weight tier); distribution ≤½ ozClass A misdemeanour — §39-17-418≤$2,500 (§40-35-111(e)(1)); mandatory minimum $250 / $500 / $1,000 for 1st / 2nd / 3rd+ misdemeanour marijuana convictions (§39-17-428(b)(1)–(3))≤11 months 29 days
Manufacture / deliver / sell / possess with intent≥½ oz (14.175 g) to 10 lbs (4,535 g); or hashish ≤2 lbs (905 g)Class E felony — §39-17-417(g)(1)≤$5,0001–6 years
Same>10 lbs (4,536 g) to 70 lbs (31,696 g); or hashish >2 lbs (906 g) to 4 lbs (1,810 g); or 10–19 plants regardless of weightClass D felony — §39-17-417(g)(2)≤$50,0002–12 years
SameHashish >4 lbs (1,811 g) to 8 lbs (3,620 g); or 20–99 plantsClass C felony — §39-17-417(g)(3)≤$100,0003–15 years
Same70 lbs (31,697 g) to 300 lbs (136,050 g); or hashish >8 lbs (3,621 g) to 15 lbs (6,792 g); or 100–499 plantsClass B felony — §39-17-417(i)(14)≤$200,0008–30 years
Same≥300 lbs (136,050 g); or hashish ≥15 lbs 1 g (6,793 g); or ≥500 plantsClass A felony — §39-17-417(j)(14)≤$500,00015–60 years

Traps — do not smooth over: (1) §39-17-417(g) contains no plant tier below 10 plants. A 1–9 plant grow is classified by weight under (g)(1) (≥½ oz to 10 lbs = Class E) — or, if below ½ oz, the classification is not stated in (g) at all. Secondary summaries stating flatly “fewer than 10 plants = Class E” are imprecise — Verify against case law/official commentary. (2) The “1–6 years” etc. spans above are the full span across Ranges I–III in §40-35-112 (Range I: E 1–2, D 2–4, C 3–6, B 8–12, A 15–25; Range III: E 4–6, D 8–12, C 10–15, B 20–30, A 40–60), not a single Range I sentence. (3) §39-17-417(k)(1): recipient/intended recipient under 18 = one classification higher. §39-17-417(l): habitual-drug-offender sentencing (3+ prior Class A/B felony convictions under §39-17-417, different times / ≥24 hours apart) = one range higher + fine ≤$200,000. Felony mandatory minimum fines (§39-17-428(b)(9)–(11)): 1st $2,000; 2nd $3,000; 3rd+ $5,000 — minima sit inside the authorised range, not in addition to it. (4) §39-17-419 inference: amount + surrounding facts may support intent to sell; casual-exchange circumstances may support no intent — given to the jury in the judge's charge. A 2017 amendment snippet proposing that intent “shall not be inferred solely from possession or casual exchange of less than one (1) ounce” was not confirmed as enacted in the opened 2025 §39-17-419 text — do not cite it as current law. (5) §39-17-418(d): casual exchange to a minor by an adult at least 2 years the minor's senior, knowing the person is a minor, is punished as a felony as provided in §39-17-417. §39-17-418(e) (Class E felony on 2+ priors) applies where the current violation involves Schedule I heroin, not marijuana — do not apply it to marijuana. §39-17-418(f) allows a court to require drug-offender school or community service at a drug/alcohol rehabilitation/treatment centre.

Paraphernalia — §39-17-425 (opened): use/possession with intent to use = Class A misdemeanour (§39-17-425(a)); delivery/possession with intent to deliver/manufacture with intent to deliver, knowing or reasonably should know it will be used as paraphernalia = Class E felony (§39-17-425(b)(2)); delivery by a person 18+ to a person under 18 at least 3 years younger = Class E felony (§39-17-425(b)(3)); advertising paraphernalia = Class A misdemeanour (§39-17-425(c)). “Drug paraphernalia” (§39-17-402(12)) expressly includes objects for ingesting/inhaling marijuana, concentrates and oil (pipes, water pipes, bongs, etc., §39-17-402(12)(C)). Mandatory minimum fine for paraphernalia possession: 1st $150; 2nd+ $250 (§39-17-428(b)(7)–(8)). §39-17-424 (determination whether an object is paraphernalia) was not opened — Verify before quoting.

Public consumption: no standalone “public consumption of marijuana” offence was located in Part 4. Consumption/possession in public is charged as possession (§39-17-418) or manufacture/delivery/intent (§39-17-417). Drug-free school-zone enhancements (§39-17-432) and DUI law were not opened — Verify if needed in Run 2; do not import DUI penalties from secondary summaries here.

Marijuana-derived product typeLegal in Tennessee?
Flower / budNo — “all parts of the plant cannabis, whether growing or not” (§39-17-402(16)(A)).
Edibles, vapes, concentrates, beverages, oils (marijuana-derived)No — definition includes “every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation … including concentrates and oils.” Only exceptions: qualifying low-THC oil (<0.9%) and DEA-certified university research oil (<0.6%).
FDA-approved prescription productExcluded from “marijuana” (§39-17-402(16)(D)); lawful only as prescribed/dispensed under FDA approval and the §39-17-403 mechanism.
Hemp / hemp-derived productsExcluded from “marijuana” (§39-17-402(16)(C); §39-17-415(c)) — but which cannabinoids, limits and bans apply is the HDCP regime in §1.5, not inferred from this definition alone.

1.4 Low-THC oil, registry, Medical Cannabis Commission, broader medical legislation

History (secondary for pre-2021 steps — chapter numbers Verify): 2014 SB 2531 — first CBD/clinical-study model (secondary: MPP, Americans for Safe Access). 2015 SB 280 / HB 197 — removed the clinical-study requirement; fiscal memoranda FM0163 (10 Mar 2015) / FM0662 (31 Mar 2015) describe the rule as oil <0.9% THC, manufacturer-labelled <0.9%, with proof of legal order/recommendation from the issuing state and proof of a Tennessee-licensed doctor's diagnosis of intractable seizures; amendment added epilepsy; signed May 2015 (secondary date — Verify). 2016 — revision to the current two-track structure (seizures track + research track at <0.6% for DEA-certified higher-education institutions, for intractable seizures, cancer or other diseases) — chapter number Verify. 2021 SB 118 / HB 490, Pub. Ch. 577 — expanded the condition list and created the Commission (see below). The 0.9% threshold itself did not change in 2021 (it was already 0.9% since 2015/2016); secondary claims that 2021 “raised” the limit from 0.6% to 0.9% conflate the research track (0.6%) with the possession track (0.9%) — do not repeat that claim.

Research track — §39-17-402(16)(E): “‘Marijuana’ does not include cannabis oil containing the substance cannabidiol, with less than six-tenths of one percent (0.6%) of tetrahydrocannabinol, including the necessary seeds and plants, when manufactured, processed, transferred, dispensed, or possessed by a four-year public or private institution of higher education certified by the drug enforcement administration located in the state as part of a clinical research study on the treatment of intractable seizures, cancer, or other diseases;”

Possession track — §39-17-402(16)(F): “‘Marijuana’ does not include oil containing the substance cannabidiol, with less than nine-tenths of one percent (0.9%) of tetrahydrocannabinol, if: (i)(a) The bottle containing the oil is labeled by the manufacturer as containing cannabidiol in an amount less than nine-tenths of one percent (0.9%) of tetrahydrocannabinol; and (b) The person in possession of the oil retains: (1) Proof of the legal order or recommendation from the issuing state; and (2) Proof that the person or the person's immediate family member has been diagnosed with intractable seizures or epilepsy by a medical doctor or doctor of osteopathic medicine who is licensed to practice medicine in this state; or (ii)(a) The bottle containing the oil is labeled by the manufacturer as containing cannabidiol in an amount less than nine-tenths of one percent (0.9%) of tetrahydrocannabinol on a printed label that includes the manufacturer's name and the expiration date, batch number or lot number, and tetrahydrocannabinol concentration strength of the oil; and (b) The person in possession of the oil retains: (1) Proof of the legal order or recommendation from the issuing state; (2) Proof that the person or the person's immediate family member has been diagnosed with at least one (1) of the following diseases or conditions by a medical doctor or doctor of osteopathic medicine who is licensed to practice medicine in this state: [list below]; and (3) Proof that the person or the person's immediate family member has a valid letter of attestation, as defined in §68-7-101;”

THC limit: strictly “less than” 0.9% — oil at exactly 0.9% is not within the exclusion. There is no stated CBD minimum, no possession quantity/weight limit, and no stated “significant CBD content” requirement in the opened §39-17-402 text — do not add one from secondary summaries. The statute frames this as an exclusion from the definition of “marijuana”, not as an affirmative-defence section and not as a licence. Secondary sources variously call it a “defence” because in practice a person may still be arrested/charged and must produce the documentation — describe it precisely as a definitional exclusion with documentation conditions, and note arrest risk only as secondary commentary, not statute.

Documentation — what must be retained: Track (i) (seizures/epilepsy): manufacturer label stating <0.9% THC; proof of legal order/recommendation from the issuing state; proof of Tennessee-licensed MD/DO diagnosis of intractable seizures or epilepsy for the possessor or immediate family member. No letter of attestation required on this track. Track (ii) (broader conditions): printed manufacturer label with manufacturer name, expiration date, batch/lot number and THC concentration strength; proof of legal order/recommendation from the issuing state; proof of Tennessee-licensed MD/DO diagnosis of a listed condition; proof of a valid letter of attestation (§68-7-101).

“Obtained legally outside Tennessee” — precision flag: the current §39-17-402 text requires “proof of the legal order or recommendation from the issuing state.” It does not in the opened text contain the sentence “obtained legally in the United States and outside of this state” — that sentence appears in the 2015 HB 197 bill text (109th GA). Do not quote the 2015 bill sentence as current law. The practical position (no in-state dispensary or sale mechanism exists, so compliant oil necessarily originates under another state's order) is a correct inference but must be labelled as such, not as verbatim statute.

No out-of-state reciprocity. No statute located recognises another state's medical card. The low-THC documentation condition is not reciprocity and does not authorise possession of marijuana flower or any other form. A 2021 reciprocity bill (HB 0601 / SB 0025, possession of ≤½ oz with another state's card) did not pass — HB 0601 taken off notice in Criminal Justice Subcommittee; SB 0025 assigned to General Subcommittee of Senate Judiciary (wapp subject index, 2022).

Qualifying conditions — CONFLICT FOUND (do not merge silently)

Source text (opened)Conditions as written
§39-17-402(16)(F)(ii)(b)(2) (Justia 2025)(A) Alzheimer's disease; (B) ALS; (C) Cancer, when diagnosed as end stage or the treatment produces related wasting illness, recalcitrant nausea and vomiting, or pain; (D) Inflammatory bowel disease, including Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis; (E) Multiple sclerosis; (F) Parkinson's disease; (G) HIV or AIDS; (H) Sickle cell disease; (I) Quadriplegia — 9 items, no separate epilepsy entry (epilepsy is covered by track (i)).
§68-7-101(5) “Qualifying medical disease or condition” (LawServer, opened)(A) Alzheimer's; (B) ALS; (C) Cancer (same end-stage/treatment wording); (D) IBD incl. Crohn's/UC; (E) Epilepsy or seizures; (F) Multiple sclerosis; (G) Parkinson's; (H) HIV or AIDS; (I) Sickle cell disease — 9 items, no quadriplegia.
SB 118 as enacted / NORML summary (May 2021)Same 9 as §68-7-101 — no quadriplegia.

Resolution for this file: the distinct conditions across both codified lists = 10 (the 9 in §68-7-101 plus quadriplegia from §39-17-402). Secondary sources saying “10 listed conditions” are merging the two lists; sources saying “9” are quoting one list. Verify against official Lexis text whether §39-17-402(16)(F)(ii) currently includes quadriplegia (a post-2021 amendment not located) or whether the Justia reproduction reflects a different version. Until verified, do not publish a single flat “qualifying conditions” list without this footnote. The tension deepens because the letter-of-attestation definition (§68-7-101(6)) cross-refers to “qualifying medical disease or condition” as defined in §68-7-101(5) — i.e. the §68-7-101 list governs the attestation — so a quadriplegia patient relying on §39-17-402(F)(ii) would still need a “valid letter of attestation, as defined in §68-7-101,” whose definition points to a list omitting quadriplegia. Flag as an unresolved statutory tension, Verify.

Valid letter of attestation — §68-7-101(6) (LawServer, opened, verbatim in substance): a letter signed and dated by a practitioner with whom the patient has a bona fide practitioner-patient relationship, that (A) attests the patient has a qualifying medical disease or condition; (B) specifies it; (C) attests the patient has received conventional methods of treatment and those methods have insufficiently addressed the disease/condition or symptoms; and (D) is valid only 6 months from the practitioner's signature, or to a lesser date certain if specified. “Practitioner” (§68-7-101(4)) = a physician licensed under Title 63, Ch. 6 (medicine) or Ch. 9 (osteopathic medicine). “Bona fide practitioner-patient relationship” (§68-7-101(1)) requires a treatment/consulting relationship including assessment of history and current condition, appropriate examination, and confirmation of a qualifying condition. No Tennessee physician “certification” or card is created by this letter — it is an attestation letter the patient retains, not a state-issued document.

Registry: NONE. No patient registry, no caregiver registry, no card, and no dispensary licensing exists under current law for low-THC oil. The registry/card systems in SB 0489 / HB 0872 are proposed only. The Commission's 2022 report recommended a registry and cards for a future programme — recommendations, not law.

Tennessee Medical Cannabis Commission — creation, remit, current status

Creation: Title 68, Chapter 7, added by SB 118 / HB 490, Pub. Ch. 577 (2021). Numbering caution: in the official amendment PDF (SA0328), the new chapter is drafted as §68-7-101 (creation), §68-7-102 (members), etc. In the codified Chapter 7 index (LawServer), a definitions section has been inserted as §68-7-101, pushing creation to §68-7-102, members to §68-7-103, domicile to §68-7-104, etc. Cite the codified numbers in the final (creation = §68-7-102 as codified; definitions = §68-7-101), and note the amendment-vs-codified renumbering. The Pub. Ch. 50 text referring to creation by §68-7-102 confirms the codified numbering.

Remit (SA0328, verbatim): see the creation quotation in §1.2. In short: study federal/state law and prepare legislation for an effective, patient-focused programme upon federal rescheduling/descheduling from Schedule I; no programme may operate and no licences may be issued until that federal removal — and, after SB 1603 (if enacted as passed), until the General Assembly acts.

Composition (SA0328 §68-7-102 as drafted; codified §68-7-103): originally 9 members — 3 by the Senate Speaker (≥1 physician under Title 63 Ch. 6 or 9, ≥1 pharmacist under Title 63 Ch. 10), 3 by the House Speaker (same minima), 3 by the Governor (1 per grand division). Initial terms from 1 July 2021 expiring 30 June 2023 (Governor), 2024 (Senate Speaker), 2025 (House Speaker); thereafter 4-year terms from 1 July; max 2 consecutive full 4-year terms; removal only for just cause (misconduct, incompetency, wilful neglect) after charges; automatic removal for absence from >50% of scheduled meetings in 24 months; members ≥30 years old, state residents ≥2 years; no person with an economic interest in a medical/recreational cannabis or hemp business may be appointed, and a member may not acquire such an interest during the term or within 12 months after it. Quorum 5; 5 affirmative votes required to adopt/report a resolution or recommendation. Domicile Nashville; all meetings in Nashville; first meeting by 1 Oct 2021; at least once every 2 months before 1 Mar 2023, thereafter as necessary. Attached to the Department of Health for administrative matters (budgeting, audit, IT, HR, clerical). Executive director appointed by the Commission.

Duties (§68-7-109 as drafted, SA0328 §68-7-108): in anticipation of federal rescheduling/descheduling, examine federal law, other states' laws/legislation, and Tennessee law, specifically: (1) patient qualification; (2) patient registration; (3) role of physicians, NPs, PAs; (4) role of pharmacists; (5) preventing non-medical personnel from recommending/prescribing/dispensing; (6) licensing/regulation of cultivation, processing, labelling, transporting, shipping, distributing; (7) guidelines for acceptable medical uses; (8) testing for product safety; (9) role of other departments/agencies/boards; (10) role of law enforcement; (11) current criminal laws on possession/use; (12) taxes and fees; (13) recommended standard of care (certification, treatment methods, history/examination, prior medication/substance-abuse history, alternative modalities, dosing/route, drug interactions/contraindications) and requiring licensing boards to adopt a standard of care before their licensees may qualify/dispense; (14) any other relevant issues. The Commission must prepare recommendations including proposed legislation, including provisions creating an independent, financially self-sufficient commission to administer a future programme, with a strategy to repay the general fund, and must report findings and recommendations in writing to the chief clerks of the Senate and House and the legislative librarian no later than 1 January of each year, beginning in 2022 (report may be electronic).

Current status (as of 25 Oct 2026): Still exists; not sunset. Extended to 30 June 2029 by HB 0255 / SB 0077, Pub. Ch. 50 — official wapp page opened: House 90–0 (20 Feb 2025), Senate 28–1 (3 Mar 2025), signed 26 Mar 2025, Pub. Ch. 50 assigned 31 Mar 2025. (One secondary summary mis-dates signing as 2026; the official page controls — 2025.) Membership now 10 (from 1 July 2026): SB 0550 / HB 2404, Pub. Ch. 817 — Fiscal Memorandum FM2898 (23 Mar 2026, opened via search) states the additional appointment is by the Governor and must be a specialist in substance-abuse prevention, as a nonvoting member. As introduced, SB 0550 would instead have required a Senate-Speaker appointee from a Prevention Alliance of Tennessee list — do not cite the as-introduced mechanism as law. Effective date 1 July 2026 and signature 27 Apr 2026 are secondary-tracker only — Verify on wapp. Activity: 2022 report dated 4 Jan 2022 (official capitol PDF, opened via search; 9 members listed, 6 meetings Oct–Dec 2021, administrative support from the Department of Health). That report recommended a programme including oils, tinctures, patches, edibles, vapours and waxes but not dried botanical flower, plus employee protections and parental rights (secondary MPP summary — Verify against the PDF before quoting recommendations). Secondary MPP summary further states a 2025 report was “very limited, following difficulties meeting quorum in 2024” and “No report was submitted in 2026” — Verify both claims against the official archive; no dedicated Department of Health Commission web page was located — Unavailable / Verify: do not invent a tn.gov Commission URL or contact. 2026 draft programme / summer study — NOT a bill: secondary sources (Marijuana Herald, Sept 2026; Hemp Law Group, July 2026) report the Commission voted unanimously 5 Mar 2026 to approve draft trigger-bill legislation (effective 30 days after federal rescheduling from Schedule I to Schedule III; zones, lottery licensing via the Education Lottery Corporation, dispensary $5,000 application / $30,000 renewal, cultivator $5,000–$50,000 renewal, local opt-in vote required, seed-to-sale, testing, child-resistant packaging, advertising limits) and that the draft was deferred to summer study / Senate Judiciary study continuing into 2027. No bill number for that draft was located on wapp — Verify; treat all draft details as secondary and unfiled.

1.5 Hemp-derived cannabinoid products (HDCPs) — Title 57, Chapter 7

1.5.1 Two statutory eras — do not conflate them

2023 era (repealed for HDCPs): 2023 Pub. Ch. 423 (SB 378 / HB 403), former §§43-27-201–211. Definition former §43-27-202(2) included delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THCp, THCv and THCa, and excluded CBC/CBT/CBL/CBD/CBE/CBG/CBN/CBV, hemp feed/fibre/grain/topical products, and Schedule I substances on/after 1 July 2023. It also had 21+ sales, proof of age, behind-the-counter storage where under-21s were admitted, child-resistant packaging, QR-linked testing, and the 6% retail tax. Licensing under TDA took effect 1 July 2024. Status now: Part 2 was deleted by HB 1376 §1; it survives only as the legacy-licence regime to 30 June 2026.

2025 era (current law): 2025 Pub. Ch. 526 (HB 1376, substituted for SB 1413; House 69–21 on 10 Apr 2025 and Senate 23–9 on 17 Apr 2025 per secondary vote reporting; signed 21 May 2025). HB 1376 §13: “(a) For purposes of promulgating rules and carrying out administrative duties necessary to effectuate this act, this act takes effect upon becoming a law… (b) For all other purposes, this act takes effect January 1, 2026…” It creates §§57-7-101–117, deletes §67-6-232 (6% tax), conforms §53-11-451 forfeiture language, makes vending-machine sale of smoking hemp and HDCPs unlawful (§39-17-1507(a) as amended), and gives TABC/Department of Revenue administrative oversight in §39-17-1509(g).

1.5.2 Legal definition and how THC is measured — verbatim operative text

§57-7-102(3) “Hemp-derived cannabinoid”: “(A) Means: (i) A cannabinoid other than delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol, or an isomer derived from such cannabinoid, that is derived from hemp in a concentration of more than one-tenth of one percent (0.1%); (ii) A hemp-derived product containing delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol in a concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis; or (iii) A hemp-derived product containing a total THC content or a total theoretical THC content of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis; (B) Includes, but is not limited to: (i) Delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol; (ii) Delta-10 tetrahydrocannabinol; (iii) Hexahydrocannabinol; and (iv) Tetrahydrocannabivarin (THCv); and (C) Does not include: (i) Cannabichromene (CBC/CBCa/CBCv); (ii) Cannabicitran (CBT/CBTa); (iii) Cannabicyclol (CBL/CBLa); (iv) Cannabidiol (CBD/CBDa/CBDv/CBDp); (v) Cannabielsoin (CBE/CBEa); (vi) Cannabigerol (CBG/CBGa/CBGv/CBGm); (vii) Cannabinol (CBN/CBNa); (viii) Cannabivarin (CBV/CBVa); (ix) Hemp-derived feed products allowed under title 44, chapter 6; (x) Hemp-derived fiber, grain, or stalk; provided, that the product does not contain a hemp-derived cannabinoid in a concentration of more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis; (xi) Tetrahydrocannabinolic acid (THCa) in a concentration greater than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis; (xii) Tetrahydrocannabiphorol (THCp); (xiii) A synthetic cannabinoid; or (xiv) A substance that is categorized as a Schedule I controlled substance on or after July 1, 2023, including a substance that may be identified in subdivision (3)(B).”

§57-7-102(4) “Hemp-derived cannabinoid product” / HDCP: “(A) Means a product that contains or that is labeled as containing a hemp-derived cannabinoid and that is produced, marketed, or otherwise intended to be ingested orally, inhaled, or absorbed through the skin, including hemp and hemp plant parts, and any product that may contain a hemp-derived cannabinoid that is extracted from hemp plants or hemp plant parts; and (B) Includes: (i) Intermediate products intended for subsequent use as a component in a later finished HDCP; and (ii) Except as excluded under subdivision (3)(C)(x), harvested hemp and hemp plant parts, otherwise known as hemp flower.”

§57-7-102(2) “Dry weight”: “means the weight of plant material with a moisture content that does not exceed thirteen percent (13%)”

§57-7-102(15) “Total theoretical THC content”: “is the maximum amount of possible delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol if total conversion were to occur, calculated as the sum of the concentration of delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol added to the amount of tetrahydrocannabinolic acid after such amount is multiplied by eight hundred seventy-seven thousandths (0.877) on a dry weight basis and reported to two (2) significant figures, and expressed in a mathematical formula as follows: Total theoretical THC = ([delta 9 THC] + ([THCa] x 0.877))”

§57-7-102(10) “Synthetic cannabinoid”: “means a substance with an identical or substantially similar chemical structure to or the pharmacological activity of a cannabinoid, but that is not extracted from hemp or hemp plant parts or derived from hemp or hemp plant parts”

§57-7-102(11) “Tetrahydrocannabinolic acid” / THCa: “is the precursor of delta-9 THC”

§57-7-114 (prohibition): “(a) It is an offense to manufacture, cultivate, produce, or sell in this state: (1) Hemp, harvested hemp, hemp plant parts, HDCPs, or another product that contains a total THC content, or a total theoretical THC content, in excess of three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis; (2) A synthetic cannabinoid, or an HDCP or any other product, which contains a synthetic cannabinoid; or (3) A derivative of hemp or an HDCP that contains tetrahydrocannabiphorol (THCp). (b) A person who violates subsection (a) commits a Class A misdemeanor.”

Measurement rule in practice: Tennessee does not use delta-9-only for HDCPs. Both the delta-9 cap and the total/total-theoretical cap at 0.3% dry weight must be met. Commission testing must be by post-decarboxylation — §57-7-107(d)(4): “Commission testing must be conducted by post-decarboxylation to determine a cannabinoid profile of samples tested, including their THC concentrations. As used in this subdivision (d)(4), ‘post-decarboxylation’ means the quantification by percentage of the resulting tetrahydrocannabinol of a sample if carboxyl groups are removed from all molecules containing tetrahydrocannabinol within the sample.” TABC FAQ Q10/Q24 (official, opened) states the same formula in short form: “TABC licensees are subject to Public Chapter 526, enacted 2025, which defines total THC as (THCA x 0.877) + Δ9-THC”.

Topicals — change from 2023: TABC FAQ Q9 (official, opened) confirms: “Under Public Chapter 526, enacted 2025, products that contain or are infused with cannabinoids intended to be absorbed through the skin (e.g. bath bombs, massage oils, essential oils, lotions, transdermal patches, creams, balms) are included in the definition of regulated HDCPs.” This is a change from the 2023 exclusion of “topical products.” Pure CBD/CBG/CBN-only products, and non-detect THC products, remain outside the “hemp-derived cannabinoid” definition — TABC FAQ Q10 puts them in the “unregulated and legal” category and says they do not require a TABC licence. Note: a CBD product that also contains a regulated cannabinoid above 0.1% becomes an HDCP.

1.5.3 Allowed / banned by cannabinoid and product type

Cannabinoid / typeTennessee status under Title 57, Ch. 7Reasoning / caveat
Delta-9 THC (hemp-derived)Allowed only within HDCP limits — ≤0.3% delta-9 and ≤0.3% total/total-theoretical THC dry weight, plus mg serving/package capsAbove either 0.3% line = §57-7-114(a)(1) Class A misdemeanour; above the line it is marijuana, not hemp, for state purposes.
Delta-8 THCIncluded as an HDCP, NOT banned by name — but Verify in practiceIncluded at §57-7-102(3)(B)(i). Whether a given commercial lot is lawful turns on whether it is “derived from hemp” and not a “synthetic cannabinoid” under §57-7-102(10). The statute does not define “derived,” isomerisation, or conversion from CBD. Most commercial delta-8 is made by isomerising CBD. Secondary sources split; some treat that as synthetic and therefore banned, others as regulated-but-lawful. No TABC delta-8-specific guidance was located on tn.gov — Verify before stating a flat verdict. Do not copy the secondary “delta-8 banned” shorthand. Bass, Berry & Sims (JD Supra, secondary, opened via search) states this correctly: delta-8, delta-10, HHC and THCv “are included in the definition of HDCPs and will continue to be regulated subject to this legislation.”
Delta-10 THCSame as delta-8: included at (3)(B)(ii), subject to synthetic test and all capsSame Verify caveat on isomerisation.
HHC, THCvIncluded at (3)(B)(iii)–(iv), regulated, subject to capsSame synthetic test applies to HHC production method — Verify for any specific process.
THCaExcluded/banned only above 0.3% — not a blanket ban on the molecule at any levelExcluded at (3)(C)(xi) only “in a concentration greater than 0.3% on a dry weight basis,” and any product over 0.3% total theoretical THC is an offence under §57-7-114(a)(1). A product genuinely testing ≤0.3% total theoretical THC is not caught by (C)(xi) on THCa alone — but must still meet every other HDCP rule.
THCa flower / smokable hemp flowerEffectively banned as sold in the pre-2026 market; compliant low-THC flower remains a regulated HDCPFlower is expressly an HDCP at §57-7-102(4)(B)(ii), capped at ½ oz per package (§57-7-110(a)(4)). Secondary Hemp Law Group summary states flower may be sold only if ≤0.3% total THC — consistent with statute, but Verify against TABC Rule 0100-16 text, not opened in full.
THCpBanned in any amountExcluded at (3)(C)(xii); §57-7-114(a)(3) offence. TABC FAQ Q10/Q24: “prohibits products containing THCp … in any amount.”
Synthetic cannabinoids (incl. THC-O, any non-hemp-derived analogue)Banned in any amountExcluded at (3)(C)(xiii); §57-7-114(a)(2) offence. Definition turns on “not extracted from hemp or hemp plant parts or derived from hemp or hemp plant parts.”
CBD, CBG, CBN, CBC, etc. aloneOutside the HDCP definition; unregulated and legal without a TABC licence if non-detect / compliantExcluded at (3)(C)(i)–(viii). TABC FAQ Q10, category 1.
Flower, edibles, beverages, vapes/cartridges, pouches, topicals/transdermalsAll are HDCP product forms, regulated, not banned as forms — subject to form-specific caps belowNo form is banned by name in Chapter 7. Vapes are taxed additionally from 1 July 2026 (PC 1127). HDCP mixed/infused into beer or alcoholic beverages is separately prohibited — §57-7-110(d).
Products over any mg cap, unregistered brand, unlicensed saleUnlawful as non-compliant HDCPs, even if ≤0.3% THC§§57-7-103, 57-7-106, 57-7-110, 57-7-112; seizure as beer under §57-7-103(a)(2).

1.5.4 Per-serving / per-package limits, age, ID, registration, testing, labelling, packaging

FormCap (statute)
General / edibles (non-beverage, non-cartridge, non-pouch, non-flower)15 mg per serving; 20 servings / 300 mg per package — §§57-7-110(a)(1)(B), (f)(1). General retail package: “Be packaged in a single package or container that contains no more than the milligram equivalent of twenty (20) servings, or three hundred (300) milligrams of hemp-derived cannabinoids, in the aggregate, in a manner to be determined by the commission by rule.” Ingestible serving: “an ingestible HDCP containing a hemp-derived cannabinoid must not: (1) Be sold in a serving that contains more than fifteen (15) milligrams, in the aggregate, of one (1) or more hemp-derived cannabinoids.” Tightening vs 2023: 25 mg → 15 mg.
Beverages15 mg per serving; max 2 servings per container (30 mg) — §57-7-110(f)(2)(A); container >750 ml prohibited except 15.5-gal and 7.75-gal kegs for wholesale to retail for sale by the glass — §57-7-110(f)(2)(B); container ≤750 ml with >1 serving must be resealable for multi-day use — §57-7-110(a)(2)(B); other beverages must use traditional pull-tab, approved aluminium-can device, or screw-top/cork-style cap — §57-7-110(a)(2)(A). Verify the “pop-top” paraphrase in secondary sources against this text; the statute does not use the phrase “pop-top.”
Cartridges / vapes (inhalable in cartridge form)40 servings max, 500 mg max per cartridge — §57-7-110(a)(3): “packaged in a single cartridge that contains not more than forty (40) servings, not to exceed five hundred (500) milligrams per cartridge.” Labelling of per-serving mg is required for ingestible or inhalable-in-cartridge products — §57-7-110(a)(1)(C)(iii).
Hemp flower / plant parts½ ounce (0.5 oz) by weight per single package/container, regardless of mg content — §57-7-110(a)(4). Still subject to the 0.3% total THC cap.
Smokeless pouches15 pouches per tin/container; 6 mg per pouch max — §57-7-110(a)(5).
Infused flowerSecondary Hemp Law Group summary states infused flower may carry up to 300 mg added cannabinoids while still testing ≤0.3% total THC — secondary claim only, NOT a statutory cap (figure does not appear in §57-7-110 excerpts opened; may come from Rule 0100-15 — Verify). Verify — this figure does not appear in the §57-7-110 excerpts opened; it may come from Rule 0100-15. Do not state it as statute.

Minimum age 21 and ID — §57-7-103(b), §57-7-102(6). “Proof of age” means “a valid driver license or other government-issued identification card that contains a photograph of the person and confirms the person's age as twenty-one (21) years of age or older.” Offences, all Class A misdemeanours (§57-7-103(c)): “(b)(1) It is an offense to knowingly sell or distribute an HDCP without having first obtained proof of age from the purchaser or recipient. (2) It is an offense for a person to knowingly sell or distribute an HDCP to a person who is under twenty-one (21) years of age or to purchase an HDCP on behalf of a person who is under twenty-one (21) years of age. (3) It is an offense for a person to knowingly assist a person who is under twenty-one (21) years of age to purchase, acquire, receive, or attempt to purchase an HDCP. (4) It is an offense for a person who is under twenty-one (21) years of age to knowingly purchase, possess, or accept receipt of an HDCP or to knowingly present purported proof of age that is false, fraudulent, or not actually that person's proof of age…” Compliance checks using under-21 buyers with consent are expressly preserved — §57-7-103(b)(5) (minor with parent/guardian consent; non-minor under-21 with own consent). TABC FAQ Q16 confirms inspections at application, annually at renewal, and by random inspection including minor compliance checks. Employment age floor (distinct): §57-7-103(b)(7) — offence to knowingly employ a person under 18 for physical manufacture, storage, sale or distribution of HDCPs, or permit such a person on premises to do so.

Product (brand) registration — §57-7-112, Department of Revenue. “(a) … ‘brand’ means each category and type of HDCP, as distinguishable to a consumer by supplier, name or trademark, brand or product line name or trademark, delivery system, or another distinction between HDCPs as promulgated by rule of the department of revenue or commission. (b) Each brand of HDCPs, distinguishable by category, type, and delivery system, must be separately registered with the department of revenue. (c)(1) A supplier shall not deliver any HDCPs to a wholesaler for distribution in this state unless each HDCP brand is registered by the supplier with the department of revenue. (2) A wholesaler or retailer shall not place an order for, receive, accept, or offer for sale an HDCP unless the HDCP brand is registered with the department of revenue. … (e) The department of revenue shall collect from the supplier an annual brand registration fee of three hundred dollars ($300) per HDCP brand registered with the department.” Secondary (Hemp Law Group, reviewed Oct 2026) adds: registration on Form HDC 101 via TNTAP, $300 per brand per category (edibles / beverages / inhalables), first due 1 Jan 2026 with annual renewal 1 Jan, with label, COA from a TABC-certified lab, supplier licence, and wholesaler contract per brand. Confirmed on DOR Form HDC 101: $300 per brand registration, each brand registered separately by category/type/delivery system (edibles, sublingual, inhalation, transdermal, topical), first due 1 Jan 2026 and each 1 Jan thereafter. DOR also publishes a searchable Hemp Brand Directory on TNTAP (secondary, not opened — see Part 5). Important TABC clarification, Oct 2026 (secondary report of TABC guidance, 17 Oct 2026): neither a TABC business licence nor a DOR brand registration establishes that an individual product complies — Tennessee provides no product-by-product pre-approval. Compliance is batch/product-level. Verify against the TABC 16 Oct 2026 guidance itself; reported secondarily, not opened on tn.gov.

Testing — §§57-7-107(a)–(c). “(a) Testing of products and substances must be conducted as follows: (1) Full-panel testing on all active cannabinoid molecules must be conducted prior to final production of HDCPs; and (2) A potency test must be conducted on finished goods to confirm potency is consistent with stated potency on the packaging. (b)(1) A supplier must contract with a third-party laboratory to provide the testing required by subsection (a). Such laboratory may be located within or outside of this state; provided, that the laboratory is certified for testing by the commission. … (c) Each batch manufactured must undergo testing and obtain a certificate of analysis by a third-party laboratory qualified under subsection (b).” The Commission must maintain and post a registry of qualified labs (§57-7-107(d)(2)–(3)) and set pass/fail action levels for safety/toxicity by rule (§57-7-107(d)(1)). Verify: secondary/TABC-webinar claims that labs must be ISO 17025-accredited and DEA-registered, and that COAs must show heavy metals, pesticides, residual solvents, microbiologicals — those specifics are not in §57-7-107 as enacted and likely come from Rule 0100-15, not opened in full. The ±15% labelling tolerance is likewise a rule-level secondary claim — Verify against Rule 0100-15.

Labelling — §57-7-110(a)(1)(C) (as amended by PC 698 on (ii)). “Be labeled with: (i) A list of ingredients and possible allergens and a nutritional fact panel; (ii) A conspicuous warning statement having a minimum font size of six-point font [as amended by PC 698; originally eleven-point in HB 1376] concerning the risk of impairment from consumption of the product, keeping the product out of the reach of children, and other warning information as required by rule of the commission; (iii) If the product is ingestible or inhalable in cartridge form, the amount of hemp-derived cannabinoid in each serving of the product, measured in milligrams; (iv) The total amount of hemp-derived cannabinoid in the entire package, measured in milligrams; (v) The net weight of the product; (vi) A quick response (QR) code that can be scanned to access a website providing the product's batch number, date received, date of testing completion, and method of analysis for the testing report required under §57-7-107, including information regarding results of the product's full-panel and potency tests conducted pursuant to §57-7-107(a); (vii) An expiration date; and (viii) For hemp plant parts or hemp flower, the percentages and identity of each hemp-derived cannabinoid present in the HDCP.” PC 698 QR refinement (SA0696 text): a QR may link to a page carrying COAs for multiple products/batches, provided the specific batch COA is reachable within 3 navigational steps (or via batch-number search returning a direct link) and the page carries no more than 150 links/options; information must remain publicly accessible for the longer of 12 months after the product is no longer offered in Tennessee or 90 days past expiration. A QR that does not resolve to a valid COA is itself a violation (secondary summary of PC 698 / Rule 0100-15 — Verify the 150-link and retention language against PC 698 enrolled text, as the SA0696 excerpt opened ends mid-section). Consumer storage: §57-7-110(b) — a person who obtains a retail HDCP must store any unconsumed portion in its original packaging; violation is a Class C misdemeanour.

Packaging and child-appeal — §57-7-110(a)(1)(A), (c), (e), (f)(3), (g). “(a)(1)(A) Satisfy the child-resistant effectiveness standards under 16 CFR 1700.15(b)(1) when tested in accordance with the requirements of 16 CFR 1700.20” … “(c) A retailer or supplier of an HDCP shall not advertise, market, or offer for sale an HDCP by using, depicting, or signifying, in the labeling or design of the product or product packaging, or in advertising or marketing materials for the product, trade dress, trademarks, branding, or other related product imagery or scenery, characters, or symbols known to appeal primarily to persons under twenty-one (21) years of age, including, but not limited to, superheroes, comic book characters, video game characters, television show characters, movie characters, or unicorns or other mythical creatures.” … “(e) An HDCP must not be labeled or otherwise marketed to make any health-related claims, including, but not limited to, claims pertaining to diagnoses, cures, or mitigation or treatment of any human disease or other condition.” … “(f)(3) [An ingestible HDCP must not] Be formed into the shape of an animal or cartoon character.” Rule-level additions (secondary, Rule 0100-15-.07 — Verify against SOS rule text): prohibition on packaging mimicking commercial food/snack/candy/beverage brands, government seals/insignia, and false/unsupported health claims; inhalables require an additional lung-injury warning; “WARNING” in bold capitals.

THC beverages — specific rules. Beverages are lawful HDCPs, not a separate licence category, but carry the form-specific rules above plus: No HDCP in beer/alcohol: §57-7-110(d): “An HDCP mixed or infused with, or otherwise used as an ingredient in, beer or alcoholic beverages, must not be manufactured, sold, provided, or served to a consumer in this state and is otherwise strictly prohibited.” Display exception: a hemp beverage in a container ≥12 fl oz is exempt from the behind-the-barrier rule (§57-7-104(d)). On-premises sale: liquor-by-the-drink licensees holding an HDCP retail licence may sell only for on-premises consumption (§57-7-104(b)(2)). Kegs: only 15.5-gal and 7.75-gal kegs, wholesale to retail for sale by the glass (§57-7-110(f)(2)(B)). Tax: liquid HDCPs at wholesale $4.40/gallon (proportional for other sizes) — §57-7-108(a)(2)(B); plus 10% of wholesale cost if inhalable in cartridge form (PC 1127) — the $4.40 rate is not a beverage-only rate in the statute, it is the liquid-form rate.

1.5.5 Online and shipped sales

§57-7-106(b)(4): “The shipping of HDCPs directly to a retail licensee in this state or directly to a consumer in this state is strictly prohibited.” §57-7-106(b)(5): all retailer-to-consumer sales/transfers must be face-to-face at a licensed retail location; delivery directly or indirectly is strictly prohibited.
§57-7-113: “(a) It is unlawful for a person or entity: (1) To ship an HDCP directly to a consumer in this state; or (2) To utilize a delivery service to deliver an HDCP to a consumer. (b) A person or entity that violates subsection (a) is subject to a civil penalty levied by the commission of: (1) One thousand dollars ($1,000) for a first offense; (2) Five thousand dollars ($5,000) for a second offense; and (3) Ten thousand dollars ($10,000) for a third or subsequent offense. (c) This section does not prohibit a person or entity with a license issued under this chapter from shipping an HDCP outside of this state if such shipping of HDCPs is lawful in the jurisdiction in which the shipment is received.”

TABC FAQ Q5/Q7 confirm: TABC retail licensees are prohibited from direct/indirect delivery or shipment to consumers and from vending-machine/self-checkout sales; legacy TDA licensees could continue those activities only until their licence expired (30 June 2026). Transport (B2B) is different from consumer delivery: §57-7-109 requires any person transporting HDCPs into, within, or through Tennessee (other than a consumer holding retail-purchased product) to carry (1) documentation proving lawful USDA-approved state/tribal plan or USDA-licence production and compliance with cannabinoid limits, and (2) a bill of lading with owner name/address, origin, delivery point name/address, kind/quantity, and shipment date. Out-of-state sellers are not exempt: TABC FAQ Q22 — out-of-state suppliers must hold a TABC licence; it is an offence to manufacture, wholesale, or sell HDCPs in Tennessee without a valid licence. Inbound consumer shipments from out-of-state sellers are therefore unlawful under Tennessee law even if lawful where shipped — enforcement in practice falls on the seller, and no Tennessee enforcement action against a specific out-of-state shipper was located in this run (Verify if Run 1 names any).

1.5.6 Agency transfer: Department of Agriculture → TABC — dates and what moved

A TABC license is only required if a cultivator engages in manufacturing, distributing, or selling hemp-derived products directly to consumers” / “A hemp cultivator who merely grows and sells raw hemp does not require a TABC license. Anyone processing hemp (with any amount of THC) within the state in any form… must obtain a license from the TABC.” TDA's own notice confirms producer requirements unchanged under the USDA-approved state plan.TABC FAQ Q3/Q11; TDA hemp page, opened
