1.5.7 Agency transfer — TDA notices (official, opened)

TDA HDCP page: TDA “is no longer processing or issuing new HDC licenses” and directs applicants to TABC; TDA stopped accepting HDCP applications 5 p.m. CST, 22 Dec 2025; follow-up documentation/payment due 5 p.m. CST, 29 Dec 2025; licences issued in that window expired 30 June 2026 (TDA notice dated 6 Jan 2026, on the page). TDA notice 14 Nov 2025 (see §1.5.8) states Tennessee's hemp programme “will remain unchanged under existing state law” pending federal guidance; producer requirements unchanged; Public Chapter 526 interpretation is TABC's responsibility.

1.5.8 Tennessee response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates

What was found — official Tennessee statement (TDA, 14 Nov 2025), verbatim, TDA Hemp Industry page, opened 2026-10-25:

“Friday, November 14, 2025 — The Tennessee Department of Agriculture (TDA) is aware of the hemp-related provision included in the federal funding legislation approved by U.S. Congress and signed by President Trump on November 12, 2025. Until TDA has more information, Tennessee's hemp program will remain unchanged under existing state law while federal agencies develop guidance to implement the new provision. Similarly, at this time regulation of hemp production and hemp-derived cannabinoid products also remains unchanged under current state law. TDA remains committed to providing clarity and transparency and, as the legal landscape for hemp evolves, will notify stakeholders of regulatory changes that may impact them.” … “The Department's requirements for licensed hemp producers remain unchanged under the state hemp program, which complies with federal requirements. … TDA is not otherwise able to comment on the content, interpretation, or potential application of Public Chapter 526. That responsibility is assigned to the Tennessee Alcoholic Beverage Commission (TABC).”

What was NOT found — no Tennessee adoption, conforming bill, or TABC federal-date notice located: No Tennessee statute, bill, rule, or TABC notice adopting the federal 0.4 mg per container cap, or amending §57-7-102 to track PL 119-37 §781, was located after checking TABC FAQ (opened, no federal-date mention), TDA Hemp Industry page (opened, Nov 2025 notice only), and capitol bill searches for 2025–2026 HDCP bills. No TABC statement on the 11 December 2026 date was located on tn.gov in this run. Label any claim of a TABC federal-date notice as Unavailable / None located unless a TABC news page not in the opened FAQ is located. A capitol fiscal note for SB 2266 (2 Mar 2026, opened via search) states, in its assumptions: “The extent of stable revenues generated from the regulation of hemp-derived cannabinoid products is contingent on recent federal action and state agency enforcement of such action. At this time, the state will continue to regulate such products pursuant to state laws pending guidance from the United States Department of Justice.” That is a fiscal-note assumption, not a statutory response, but it is the closest official-legislative-branch statement located.

Why the federal dates matter in Tennessee even without a state response — secondary analysis, not Tennessee law: Hemp Law Group, “Tennessee Hemp After December 11, 2026,” current as of 30 Sept 2026, updated 19 Oct 2026 (secondary, opened 2026-10-25, Hemp Law Group Dec 11 analysis): Tennessee's line (0.3% total THC, dry weight) does not change on either federal date, because Chapter 7 does not incorporate the federal definition by reference. The federal line from 11 Dec 2026 adds a second, per-container cap: 0.4 mg total THC per container (PL 119-37 §781, as delayed by PL 119-103 §2019 to 11 Dec 2026; the 12 Nov 2026 date applies to the “synthesised/manufactured outside the plant” / non-naturally-produced cannabinoid prong). Arithmetic: a single 5 mg Tennessee-legal gummy or drink is 12.5× the federal cap; a 10 mg product is 25×; a 300 mg package is 750×; a 500 mg cartridge is 1,250×. In other words, nearly every product lawful under Tennessee's 15 mg serving rule will be outside the federal definition of hemp from 11 Dec 2026, while remaining lawful under Tennessee law on a TABC-licensed shelf. Practical consequences flagged in that analysis (not Tennessee law): loss of 2018 Farm Bill interstate-transport protection for non-hemp product; banking/payment-processor risk (e.g. Square's hemp/CBD cutoff reported as 15 Oct 2026, which did not move with the federal delay); federal tax (280E) risk if product is federally marijuana; contract change-in-law clauses. Tennessee's own wholesale tax, licence, sourcing, age, and serving rules do not change on 11 Dec 2026 under current state law. Open federal questions: FDA's list of THC-like cannabinoids and its “container” guidance were unpublished as of that 30 Sept 2026 analysis — Verify whether either has since published. Whether packaged retail flower is measured per-container under the federal cap is stated there as unsettled. Interaction with Tennessee's pre-existing total-THC rule: because Tennessee already excludes synthetic cannabinoids and already counts THCa, the 12 November prong (non-plant-produced cannabinoids) changes little in Tennessee that Chapter 7 had not already done — the material Tennessee impact is the 11 December per-container cap, which Tennessee does not have. No other 2025–2026 HDCP bill (federal-conformity bill, 0.4 mg adoption bill, or repeal of the TABC framework) was located in capitol searches in this run — label that as a search result as of 2026-10-25, not as a proved negative.

1.6 Advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer distance rules for HDCP retailers

Where retail sale is allowed — §57-7-104(b) (exhaustive list): “HDCPs may be sold at retail in the following locations: (1) An establishment that limits entry into the premises to individuals who are twenty-one (21) years of age or older; (2) An establishment that holds a valid license issued by the commission pursuant to §57-4-102 or §57-4-201 [liquor-by-the-drink]. An establishment that qualifies under this subdivision (b)(2) may only sell HDCPs in a quantity and manner intended for on-premise consumption…; (3) An establishment that holds a valid license issued by the commission pursuant to §57-3-204 [retail package store]; or (4) An establishment owned or leased by a licensee that holds a valid supplier license, wholesale license, and retail license for the same location where HDCPs will be manufactured and sold at retail, or [supplier + retailer at same location with a contracted wholesaler for tax/distribution].” Consequence: convenience stores, grocery stores, and gas stations are not in this list. Secondary sources state “gas stations are no longer eligible as of 1 Jan 2026” — that is a correct inference from §57-7-104(b), but the statute does not name gas stations; cite the list, not the shorthand.

Display, barrier, signage, vending — §57-7-104(a), (c), (d): “(a) … ‘barrier’ means the point of purchase having an actual physical separation between HDCPs on display and the consumer at a retail establishment or another designated area… that is inaccessible to the consumer and that requires assistance from a retail clerk in order to access and purchase HDCPs.” … “(c) Signage must be displayed in all areas where HDCPs are displayed, in a manner to be determined by the commission, clearly advising and warning the consumer that the HDCPs on display may have intoxicating effects and cause impairment. HDCPs may only be displayed in an area of the retail establishment that is constantly visible to a retail licensee employee. An HDCP in this state must not be dispensed by or sold via the use of a self-checkout retail system or vending machine.” … “(d) An HDCP must be maintained by a retailer behind a barrier unless the HDCP is a hemp-derived cannabinoid beverage product having a minimum container size of twelve (12) fluid ounces or unless the retailer is licensed pursuant to subdivision (b)(1) [21+-only premises].” Violation of §57-7-104 is a Class A misdemeanour (§57-7-104(e)).

School buffer — §57-7-106(b)(3) (current text; measurement changed from 2023): “(3)(A) An applicant for a license as a retailer with a proposed retail location that is within one thousand feet (1,000') of a private school, public school, or charter school that serves any grade from kindergarten through grade twelve (K-12) shall not sell HDCPs at such location unless the applicant provides the commission with documentation that establishes that HDCPs were being offered for sale at retail at such location on December 31, 2023. (B) The commission shall accept business records, photographs, and video recordings as documentation… (C) For the purposes of subdivision (b)(3)(A), measurements must be made in a straight line in all directions, without regard to intervening structures or objects, from the nearest exterior wall of the proposed licensed establishment to the nearest exterior wall of a building containing a private school, public school, or charter school that serves any grade from kindergarten through grade twelve (K-12).” Note: the 2023 version measured property-line to property-line; the 2025 version measures wall to wall. Cite the 2025 version as current. The former §43-27-206(b)(3) rule and the TABC framework §57-7-106(b)(3) are the same 1,000-ft / 31 Dec 2023-grandfather structure — Verify verbatim current §57-7-106 text on the official Code site before quoting it as the sole authority (the 2023 bill text and TABC FAQ summary agree on the structure).

Local ordinances / pre-emption: TABC FAQ Q21 (official, opened): “May local ordinances dictate where a hemp retailer may be located? A: No, but state law restricts hemp sales for retailers that are located within one thousand (1,000) feet of any primary or secondary education facility. The only exception are businesses that can provide documentation establishing they were selling hemp products prior to December 31, 2023.” This is consistent with §57-7-117 (Chapter 7 prevails over conflicting Code provisions) and with the wholesaler rule that a warehouse must still meet “all local requirements for the applicant's specific use” (§57-7-106(f)(1)(E)(i)) — i.e. local zoning/building/occupancy requirements still apply to the use, but a locality may not, per TABC, create its own HDCP location rule displacing the state 1,000-ft rule. Gap: whether a locality could impose a general (non-HDCP-specific) zoning restriction (e.g. retail use in a zone) is a Part 3 question and is not resolved by Q21 alone. Do not state that “all local regulation is pre-empted” without that qualification. See Part 3.2 for the fuller pre-emption analysis (no express pre-emption clause confirmed on an official Code page — Verify).

Advertising: child-appeal ban (§57-7-110(c)), health-claims ban (§57-7-110(e)), animal/cartoon shape ban (§57-7-110(f)(3)) — see §1.5.4. Sampling: §57-7-103(b)(6) — offence to knowingly distribute samples of HDCPs in or on a public street, sidewalk, or park. The “retailer” definition includes providing samples with or without fee (§57-7-102(7)), but no general in-store sampling permission is stated in Chapter 7 — Verify against Rule 0100-16 before stating any in-store sampling rule. No other advertising medium ban (e.g. billboards, social media) appears in Chapter 7 as enacted — Verify against Rule 0100-15/16; do not invent one.

Delivery: see §1.5.5 — delivery to consumers is prohibited, with civil penalties $1,000 / $5,000 / $10,000 (§57-7-113(b)) in addition to the Class A misdemeanour / licence-penalty regime for unlicensed or non-face-to-face sale.

1.7 Seed-to-sale or product tracking

No state Metrc/BioTrack-style system located. No Tennessee statute or rule located in this run requires a state-contracted electronic seed-to-sale system (Metrc, BioTrack, Leaf Data) for HDCPs, and no such system is named in Title 57, Chapter 7. What Tennessee does require instead (batch-level traceability, not plant-level tracking): batch definition and batch COA for every batch manufactured (§§57-7-102(1), 57-7-107(c)); QR code on every retail package linking to batch number, receipt date, testing-completion date, method, and full-panel + potency results (§57-7-110(a)(1)(C)(vi)); brand registration with wholesaler and county designations (§57-7-112(d)); wholesaler-only sourcing from 1 July 2026, with wholesaler monthly tax reports (§57-7-108(c): report by the 20th of each month); transport documentation and bill of lading (§57-7-109); TABC/Department of Revenue inspections, sampling, and confiscation (§§57-7-105, 57-7-103(a)(2)). Flag as Verify (negative finding): the absence claim rests on a search of Chapter 7 and TABC materials, not on opening the full Rule 0100-15/16 text. If those rules impose an inventory system, Run 2 Part 11 must correct this section. Do not state “Tennessee uses Metrc” — no source supports it.

1.8 Employment and drug testing protections

§57-7-111 (Limitations on right to use — Rights of others), verbatim in operative part: “(a) This chapter does not permit a person to: (1) Undertake any task under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP when doing so would constitute negligence or professional malpractice; or (2) Operate, navigate, or be in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft, motorized watercraft, or any other vehicle while under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or an HDCP. (b) This chapter does not require: (1) An employer to accommodate the use of hemp-derived cannabinoids or HDCPs in a workplace or an employee working while under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or an HDCP; (2) [A property owner/establishment to allow use on its property]; or (3) [To admit a visibly impaired guest/customer/visitor]. (c) This chapter does not exempt a person from prosecution for a criminal offense related to impairment or intoxication resulting from use of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP or relieve a person from any requirement under law to submit to a breath, blood, urine, or other test to detect the presence of a controlled substance. (d) This chapter does not: (1) Limit the ability of an employer to establish, continue, or enforce a drug-free workplace program or policy; (2) Create a cause of action against an employer for wrongful discharge or discrimination; or (3) Allow the possession, sale, manufacture, or distribution of any substance that is otherwise prohibited by title 39, chapter 17, part 4.”

This mirrors former §43-27-210. There is no HDCP-user employment protection, no drug-test carve-out, and no accommodation duty in Chapter 7. Verify — do not assert without opening the text: a secondary source claims Tenn. Code §50-1-304 (legal use of an agricultural product) formerly protected off-duty lawful-product use but excludes products regulated by the Alcoholic Beverage Commission, so HDCP users lost that protection on 1 Jan 2026. That reading was not verified against the current §50-1-304 text and must be flagged Verify, not stated as settled. Licence-holder/staff background checks are in §57-7-106 (TBI/FBI fingerprint checks; 10-year drug-felony ineligibility — §57-7-106(b)(2), (f)(2)), which is a licensing rule, not an employee drug-testing protection.

1.9 Pending legislation — bill numbers, summaries, status confirmed on the General Assembly's official site

Two-year General Assembly: the 114th General Assembly is 2025–2026. A bill filed in year 1 (2025) remains alive into year 2 (2026) unless it fails / is withdrawn, but any bill not enacted by the end of the 114th GA dies and must be refiled in the 115th GA. “Failed in committee” = dead for that GA; “assigned / taken off notice / deferred” without passage = stalled and, absent enactment by adjournment, dead at the end of the 114th GA. Final adjournment disposition for stalled bills below is therefore stated as “stalled in 114th GA; not enacted (Verify final adjournment status on wapp)” where the opened page did not show a failure vote. 115th GA (2027–2028) had not convened as of 25 Oct 2026, so “115th GA pending bills” is an empty set, not an omission.

Penalties and enforcement — HDCP summary for cross-reference (all from §1.5): unlicensed manufacture/wholesale/sale: Class A misdemeanour; product subject to seizure/forfeiture as beer (§57-7-103(a); TABC FAQ Q14, citing §§53-11-451 and 57-7-203(a)(2) — note the FAQ's §57-7-203 citation appears to be a typographical error for §57-7-103, Verify). Age / proof-of-age / under-21 purchase / assistance / public-sample / under-18 employment offences: Class A misdemeanour (§57-7-103(b)–(c)). Retail-display / barrier / signage / self-checkout / vending violations: Class A misdemeanour (§57-7-104(e)). Over-0.3% total THC, synthetic cannabinoid, THCp offences: Class A misdemeanour (§57-7-114(b)). Licence civil penalties (per violation of Chapter 7 or rule): $1,000 first; $2,500 second within 2 years; $5,000 third within 2 years; revocation on fourth within 2 years; plus employee retraining (§57-7-106(d)(2)). Direct-to-consumer shipping/delivery civil penalties: $1,000 / $5,000 / $10,000 (§57-7-113(b)). Consumer storage in non-original packaging: Class C misdemeanour (§57-7-110(b)). Concurrent jurisdiction: state/local law enforcement with TABC for §§57-7-103–104 offences (§57-7-103(d)); TABC + Department of Revenue for administrative enforcement and confiscation (§57-7-105).

PART 2 — TAXES

No marijuana tax exists because there is no legal marijuana sale to tax. Any figure labelled a “Tennessee cannabis tax” that is not the HDCP wholesale tax, the former 6% HDCP retail tax, ordinary sales tax, or franchise/excise tax should be treated as erroneous.

2.1 Specific HDCP taxes — chronology

PeriodTaxRate / baseWho pays / howSource (accessed 2026-10-25)

Drafting-history warning — do not quote early drafts as law: fiscal notes for HB 1376 / SB 1413 show earlier versions at $0.01/mg, $15/oz flower, and different allocations (e.g. FM0545, FM1311). The enacted rates above are the DOR SUT-112 rates. The final allocation of wholesale-tax revenue (earlier drafts: 80% General Fund / 10% DOR / 10% TABC; other drafts: 50% TABC / 50% General Fund, or 50% local roads / 50% TABC) was not confirmed from an opened current statute — Verify before publishing an allocation split.

Legacy-licence transition affecting the tax: under the TABC / Tennessee Healthy Alternatives Association agreed declaratory order (Oct 2025, cited in FM3187), holders of unexpired TDA licences remained under Title 43, Ch. 27, Part 2 until expiry 30 June 2026 and were not subject to the Title 57 wholesale tax or brand-registration requirement until they obtained a TABC licence. That is why early-2026 wholesale-tax collections are near-zero even though the tax was “effective” 1 Jan 2026. TABC FAQ Q1, Q8, Q23 confirm the same transition.

Brand registration — a fee that works like a tax: $300 per brand per year, paid by the supplier to DOR with Form HDC 101, before a brand may be delivered to a wholesaler for distribution in Tennessee (see §1.5.4). Whether registration is $300 per brand or per brand per product category (edibles / beverages / inhalables) is conflicting in secondary reporting — Verify with DOR. The “Hemp” revenue line in §2.6 includes brand fees as well as tax.

2.2 Ordinary sales and use tax — applies to HDCP retail sales

  • State rate: 7% on the retail price of tangible personal property, §§67-6-202–205. Confirmed in DOR's archived SUT-111 (which also states the former 6% HDCP tax was “in addition to the standard 7% state sales tax rate and the applicable local option sales tax rate”): SUT-111 (PDF).
  • Local option sales tax: up to 2.75%, levied by counties/cities under §§67-6-701–702, only on the first $1,600 of any single article of personal property. Typical combined register rates are therefore 9.25%–9.75% for low-priced items, but the local-tax cap and the separate 2.75% state single-article tax on the $1,600.01–$3,200 slice mean the effective rate falls on expensive single items. The $1,600 cap rule is stated in secondary summaries of §§67-6-202, 67-6-702; the HDCP-specific application of the single-article cap was not separately confirmed in a DOR page opened in this run — Verify for high-value HDCP bundles.
  • Food rate (4%) does not apply to HDCPs on any source opened; HDCPs are not food/food ingredients for this purpose. No DOR ruling to the contrary was located.
  • Hemp growers selling hemp they grew: exempt from business tax and from sales tax on those sales, and need no city/county business licence for those sales — DOR SUT-112. This exemption does not extend to a grower who manufactures or retails finished HDCPs.

2.4 IRS §280E and Tennessee conformity — “no decoupling statute found, and no market for it to apply to”

  1. No Tennessee statute decoupling from IRC §280E for marijuana was located. No bill, DOR notice, or §67-4-2006 adjustment mentioning §280E was found in official sources opened.
  2. Mechanism if one existed: Tennessee excise tax starts with federal taxable income (before federal NOL and special deductions), §67-4-2006(a)(1), as adjusted by §67-4-2006(b)–(c). The DOR Franchise and Excise Tax Manual (June 2026) states the same starting point: DOR Franchise & Excise Manual (June 2026). Tennessee's §67-4-2006(b)–(c) adjustments include items such as bonus-depreciation decoupling, but no §280E add-back or add-back reversal was identified in the manual excerpts returned — Verify by a full-text search of §67-4-2006(b)–(c) before advising a taxpayer. The logical consequence, if no such adjustment exists, is that a federal §280E disallowance would flow into the Tennessee starting point.
  3. Practical effect in Tennessee is nil for marijuana: Tennessee has no state-licensed medical or adult-use marijuana programme, so there are no state-licensed marijuana businesses to gain or lose §280E relief under the federal split scheduling (state-licensed medical + FDA-approved products to Schedule III, 91 FR 22714, eff. 28 Apr 2026; adult-use remains Schedule I). Marijuana therefore remains Schedule I in Tennessee for federal purposes (and Schedule VI under Tennessee state law), and any illegal marijuana activity remains fully subject to §280E federally.
  4. Hemp is outside §280E altogether: hemp as defined federally (≤0.3% delta-9 THC) is excluded from the CSA definition of marihuana, so lawful hemp / HDCP businesses were not subject to §280E on that ground while the product met the federal hemp definition in force at the time; after 11 December 2026 a Tennessee-lawful product over the 0.4 mg federal container cap is potentially federally marijuana and therefore potentially §280E-exposed. This is consistent with DOR SUT-112 treating hemp businesses as ordinary franchise/excise taxpayers.
  5. State-rescheduling block — secondary, Verify: secondary reporting (Mondaq, May 2026; Drug Policy Watch Tennessee brief) states Tennessee enacted a 2026 law preventing state agencies from following a federal rescheduling without an act of the General Assembly, and cites an unconfirmed bill pairing (HB 1972 / SB 1603). Bill number and chapter not verified on capitol.tn.gov in the tax workstream — do not cite as confirmed law on that basis alone (see Part 1.9, where SB 1603 passage is documented but signature/chapter remain secondary).

2.5 Revenue figures — official first, then dated secondary reporting of official tables

Official Fiscal Review Committee figures (from Capitol fiscal notes for HB 1376 / SB 1413): FY 2023–24 collections from the former 6% HDCP retail tax: $12,574,076. Fiscal notes: SB1413 fiscal note and FM1177. Implied FY 2023–24 HDCP taxable retail sales: $209,567,933 ($12,574,076 ÷ 6%) — FRC calculation, assuming the rate, not a DOR sales census. FY 2025–26 estimate for the 6% tax (pre-repeal baseline): $13,607,203 ($12,574,076 × 1.0481 × 1.0325, FRC sales-growth assumptions) — an estimate made before repeal, not a collection.

2026 wholesale-tax collections — secondary reporting of Dept. of Finance and Administration monthly tables (no official monthly HDCP table was opened in this run — Verify directly against Finance tables before republishing):

The Tennessean (20 Aug 2026) uses the $55.8m baseline. The HB 1376 fiscal note estimated TABC licence-fee + wholesale-tax revenue and General Fund effects in FY 2025–26 / FY 2026–27, but those were pre-enactment estimates built on the $0.01–$0.02/mg drafts and on an assumed licence base — they are not collections and should not be quoted as such. Hemp Law Group also reports ~$1.3m wholesale (Jan–Jun sales, collected Feb–Jul) + ~$156,000 late old-tax payments over 6 months = ~$1.46m total; old tax's last full month (Dec 2025 sales) $1.8m; Feb–Jul 2025 $9m vs Feb–Jul 2026 $1.4m — an 84% drop as calculated by Hemp Law Group.

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.1 Who does what: counties, cities, metropolitan governments

State framework — the state licence is the controlling HDCP licence. HDCP regulation transferred from TDA to TABC on 1 January 2026 under 2025 Pub. Ch. 526 (HB 1376 / SB 1413). TDA's own notice states it is “no longer processing or issuing new HDC licenses” and that TDA licences issued through 31 December 2025 remained valid under §43-27-201 et seq. only until they expired 30 June 2026. Sources (official, opened): TDA HDCP page; TDA hemp industry page (notice 14 Nov 2025).

Counties — Title 5 and Title 13, Chapter 7. Counties have no inherent land-use power; they exercise only delegated power. County zoning is under §13-7-101 et seq. and applies only in portions of the county outside municipal corporations — i.e. unincorporated territory. A county must adopt a zoning ordinance and map through the planning process; it cannot create broad rural land-use control by ordinary resolution alone. Separately, §5-1-118(c) lets a county, after adoption of a resolution by a two-thirds vote of the county legislative body, exercise the municipal powers in §6-2-201(22) and (23): to “define, prohibit, abate, suppress, prevent and regulate” acts, businesses and uses detrimental to health, morals, comfort, safety, convenience or welfare, and to prescribe limits within which nuisance-liable businesses may be established. Sources (official-adjacent, opened via search): UT / CTAS material summarising §§13-7-101, 5-1-118 and 6-2-201(22)–(23) — UT Farm Bureau law summary (PDF) and CTAS printable summary. Verify: verbatim current text of §§5-1-118, 13-7-101 was not opened on the state's official Code site in this workstream; citations are as summarised in those UT sources.

Cities / municipalities — Title 6 and Title 13, Chapter 7, Part 2. Municipal general police power: §6-2-201(22) (quoted above) and (23) (limits for nuisance-liable businesses). Municipal zoning: §13-7-201(a)(1) empowers a municipality to regulate uses of buildings, structures and land for trade, industry, residence, recreation and other purposes, through a comprehensive plan. Local decisions must not conflict with state law — see Binkley v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville and Davidson County, Tenn. Ct. App. 2011, summarising §13-7-201 and Metro's §17.40.280 special-exception procedure (secondary case report, not official Code text).

Metropolitan (consolidated) governments — Title 7. Only three exist in Tennessee: Nashville-Davidson County, Lynchburg-Moore County, and Hartsville-Trousdale County. Source: UT County Technical Assistance Service, “Metropolitan Government Charters” (CTAS, accessed 2026-10-25). Under §7-2-108, a metropolitan government is vested with all the powers of cities and all the powers of counties under general law. Nashville-Davidson is a partial consolidation: Belle Meade, Forest Hills, Berry Hill, Goodlettsville (partly in Sumner County), Brentwood (partly in Williamson County), Ridgetop (partly in Robertson County) and the former Lakewood/Oak Hill satellites elected not to join, so those satellite cities retain their own municipal powers inside Davidson County. Lynchburg-Moore and Hartsville-Trousdale are complete consolidations. Source: MTAS, “Metropolitan government” (accessed 2026-10-25). Practical consequence for HDCP shops: in Nashville-Davidson, Metro zoning, business licensing and smoke-free enforcement apply in the Urban Services District / General Services District, but a shop in Belle Meade, Berry Hill, Forest Hills, Goodlettsville, Brentwood or Ridgetop is governed by that satellite city for municipal matters, plus Metro for county functions. In Lynchburg-Moore and Hartsville-Trousdale there is a single consolidated government for both levels. Verify: no HDCP-specific ordinance was located for Lynchburg-Moore or Hartsville-Trousdale (see §3.3).

LevelHDCP licensing?Zoning / siting?General business licence / business tax?Smoking / public-health rules?
State (TABC / DOR)Yes — sole HDCP supplier / wholesaler / retailer licensor since 1 Jan 2026 (legacy TDA licences expired 30 Jun 2026)Yes — statewide 1,000-ft K–12 school buffer (see §3.4)State sales, wholesale HDCP and franchise/excise tax registration via TNTAPState 21+ sale, ID, packaging, advertising rules in Title 57, Ch. 7

3.2 Does state law pre-empt local regulation of HDCPs?

No express pre-emption clause in Title 57, Chapter 7 was confirmed on an official Code page opened for this workstream — flag as Verify. What is established:

  1. No local HDCP permitting system was enacted. HB 962 / SB 770 (114th GA, 2025) would have authorised local governments to create local HDCP boards, require a $250 annual local retailer permit per location, inspect, and impose $1,000 civil penalties — “as long as the regulations do not conflict with state law.” Both bills stalled in Finance committees in April 2025 and did not become law. Sources (secondary bill trackers, consistent with each other, accessed 2026-10-25). Verify final status on wapp.capitol.tn.gov before citing in the main report — the trackers agree the bills died in committee, but official bill pages were not opened for this pair in the local-rules workstream.
  2. A law-firm FAQ summarising TABC guidance states: “local ordinances do not control where hemp retailers may locate.” Source (secondary, reporting TABC FAQ): Hemp Law Group Tennessee Hemp TABC FAQ (accessed 2026-10-25). Verify: this is a secondary summary of TABC guidance, not TABC's own page opened in that workstream, and it must not be quoted as a statutory pre-emption clause. The TABC FAQ Q21 answer itself (official, opened in the HDCP workstream) is quoted in §1.6. If TABC's FAQ says this, its legal basis (state school buffer + state licensing exclusivity vs. general local zoning power) needs confirmation from TABC directly.
  3. General conflict rule: a local ordinance that conflicts with state law is invalid under Tennessee case law (see Binkley, above). That is a general principle, not an HDCP-specific pre-emption statute.
  4. What locals can still do without conflicting: general retail zoning (which districts allow retail), building/fire codes, signage, general business licensing, and smoke-free rules — all of which apply to HDCP shops as retail establishments, not as HDCP licensees. No evidence was found that any Tennessee locality has used those powers to impose an HDCP-specific cap, ban or buffer (see §3.3).

Bottom line: do not state “Tennessee pre-empts all local HDCP regulation.” State accurately: HDCP licensing is exclusively state (TABC); no local HDCP permit exists in law; whether a general local zoning restriction that singles out HDCP retailers would survive a conflict challenge is unresolved / Verify, and TABC guidance as secondarily reported leans against local siting control.

3.3 Local zoning ordinances, moratoriums, caps or bans — jurisdictions checked

No official statewide list or map exists. No list or map of local HDCP ordinances was found on TABC, TDA or DOR sites checked 2026-10-25. Unavailable as an official publication. The table below is the result of checking each required jurisdiction individually. “No HDCP-specific ordinance found” means no ordinance mentioning hemp-derived cannabinoids, HDCP, delta-8/THCA or smoke-shop siting for Tennessee was located in Municode / official city / council sources searched — it is not proof that no general retail zoning, sign or business-licence rule applies.

No Knoxville zoning cap or buffer located.

Franklin, TN's only confirmed local item is the general business-tax registration process (see §3.4).

Do not attribute any part of it to Cleveland, TN. Cleveland, TN's municipal code via MTAS was checked only for general provisions; no TN smoke-shop spacing rule was located.

Caps: No Tennessee locality was found to impose a numerical cap on HDCP retailers. Moratoriums: only the undated Memphis resolution above (historical, status Verify). Bans: no Tennessee locality was found to ban HDCP retail outright; the only bans in force are state bans (THCA >0.3% total THC, THCp, synthetics under Pub. Ch. 526).

3.4 Local business licence / permit fees and local buffer rules

General (non-HDCP-specific) business licence — official: Tennessee Department of Revenue, “Registration and Licensing” (official, opened via search): a separate $15 business licence registration fee must be paid to the county and/or municipal clerk for each new business. In-state: gross receipts >$3,000 but <$100,000 → minimal activity licence from county and/or municipal clerk; ≥$100,000 → standard business licence. Source: DOR Registration and Licensing (accessed 2026-10-25).

  • Nashville-Davidson (official, Nashville.gov): licence fee $30 if inside Nashville city limits (Urban Services District) and $15 if outside city limits but in Davidson County (General Services District), for a business-tax or minimal-activity licence, via the Davidson County Clerk. Source: Nashville County Clerk — Apply for Business License.
  • Franklin, TN (official city document): Business Tax Registration Application instructions confirm the same structure: $15 to the county clerk, plus an additional $15 to the city official if the city has business licensing; standard licence renewed by annual business-tax payment; minimal-activity licence renewed by $15 per clerk per year. Source: City of Franklin document (showdocument?id=3464, accessed 2026-10-25).
  • Memphis / Shelby, Knoxville / Knox, Chattanooga / Hamilton, Clarksville, Murfreesboro, Jackson, Johnson City, Kingsport, Cleveland (TN), Sevierville, Gatlinburg: no locality-specific fee schedule was opened on an official page for this workstream — Verify individually; do not assume the $15+$15 pattern, although DOR's statewide $15-per-clerk rule is the default framework.
  • Important exemption (official, DOR SUT-112, opened): “Hemp producers who sell the hemp they grow are therefore exempt from the business tax and are not required to collect sales tax. They do not need to get a business license from the city or county.” This exemption is for growers selling their own hemp, not for HDCP retailers, manufacturers or wholesalers.
  • No local HDCP permit fee exists in Tennessee law as enacted (HB 962's proposed $250 local permit did not pass — see §3.2). State HDCP fees belong in Part 4, not here.

Buffer rules: State buffer (the only confirmed HDCP buffer): 1,000 feet from a private, public or charter school serving any grade K–12, measured in a straight line in all directions, property line to property line under the 2023 rule and wall to wall under the 2025 rule (see §1.6), with a grandfather exception where HDCPs were offered for sale at retail at that location on 31 December 2023 (business records, photographs, video accepted as documentation). Local buffers for HDCP retailers: none verified for any Tennessee jurisdiction checked. The 500-ft / 2-mile figures in circulation belong to Cleveland, Ohio (§3.3) and must not be used for Tennessee. General alcohol buffers (e.g. liquor-store distances under Title 57, Chapter 3) are a different regime and do not apply to HDCP-only retailers. Tobacco / vapor context (state, not HDCP-specific): TDA tobacco-enforcement FAQ (official): Tennessee does not currently require or offer a state licence/permit to sell tobacco products as such, and all smoking paraphernalia must be behind the counter or in a locked display case. Source: TDA tobacco FAQ. This does not create a local HDCP buffer.

PART 4 — LICENCE TYPES

4.1 State cannabis licences: NONE

Tennessee issues no state cannabis (marijuana) licence of any kind — no cultivation, processing, manufacturing, testing, transport, or dispensary licence for marijuana exists in current law. The only state licences in this field are (a) TDA hemp-producer licences for growing hemp, and (b) TABC HDCP licences for finished hemp-derived products (below). The TABC FAQ covers only HDCP suppliers, wholesalers, and retailers, and states hemp cultivation remains with TDA (Q3, Q11): TABC FAQ. Any “Tennessee dispensary licence” in marketing material refers, at most, to a TABC HDCP retailer licence selling hemp-derived products — it is not a marijuana dispensary licence.

4.2 Current HDCP licences — TABC, Title 57, Chapter 7 (2025 Pub. Ch. 526, as amended 2026)

Common rules (TABC FAQ, opened): Applications opened 20 Dec 2025; licences approved before 1 Jan 2026 took effect 1 Jan 2026 (Q2, Q12). A person/entity may hold multiple types, including different types (Q4, Q13) — unlike alcohol three-tier, there is no ban on cross-tier ownership. A retail licence authorises only a single, contiguous premises; a separate licence is needed per location (Q6). New locations are inspected at application, annually at renewal, and by random inspection including minor compliance checks (Q16). Manufacturing, wholesaling, or selling HDCPs without a valid licence is a Class A misdemeanour, with product subject to seizure/forfeiture under §§53-11-451 and 57-7-203(a)(2) (Q14) — Verify the §57-7-203 citation (likely §57-7-103 typo, see §1.9). Out-of-state suppliers must also hold a TABC licence (Q22). From 1 July 2026 a TABC retailer may obtain new HDCP stock only from a TABC-licensed wholesaler (Q23), subject to the pre-1 July inventory sell-through in Q24.

Application requirements — all types (TABC FAQ Q18, opened, summarised verbatim in substance): legal name and DBA; physical address of every location (mailing if different); name of every individual with an ownership interest; biographic/contact information for each owner (DOB, SSN, mailing address, email, telephone); registered-agent contact if applicable; TBI or FBI background check for any individual owner, member-owner, or shareholder; verification of Tennessee Secretary of State registration if operating as a legal entity; architectural diagram with square footage/dimensions; deed or lease showing property rights; certificate of occupancy; business hours; identity/contact of any person legally responsible for management not previously disclosed. Statutory cites given in the FAQ: §§57-7-106(b)(1)(A) and 57-7-106(f).

Ineligibility (Q19): Anyone currently serving a sentence for a felony drug offence, or applying within 10 years of conviction for a felony drug offence, is ineligible, subject to analysis under the Fresh Start Act, §62-76-104.

LicenceAgencyWhat it allowsFees (official, TABC FAQ Q15)Extra requirements / bondingRenewal
HDCP Supplier — includes manufacturers and out-of-state companies that contract for manufacture and sell finished, packaged HDCPs into TennesseeTABCManufacture, or contract for manufacture of, hemp-derived cannabinoids / HDCPs; sell finished, packaged HDCPs to licensed wholesalers for resale, not for consumption$500 application (non-refundable, secondary) + $2,500 per location per yearStandard Q18 package. No $750,000 capital test. Must register each brand with DOR ($300, §2.1) before delivery to a wholesaler. Eligible retail venues under Title 57 (Q20): (i) businesses restricting entry to 21+; (ii) TABC retail package stores also holding a hemp retail licence; (iii) TABC liquor-by-the-drink establishments also holding a hemp retail licence (on-premises consumption only); (iv) establishments licensed as supplier/wholesaler/retailer at the same manufacturing premises. 1,000-foot school rule: state law restricts retail sales within 1,000 feet of a primary/secondary education facility, except businesses documenting HDCP sales at that location before 31 Dec 2023 (Q21; proof may be orders, POS records, photographs, video). Local ordinances do not dictate retailer location (Q21)Annual; inspection at renewal. Separate licence per contiguous premises (Q6)

What is NOT a separate TABC licence type on any official page opened: There is no separate “manufacturer” licence distinct from supplier; no separate “distributor” licence distinct from wholesaler; no separate “transporter,” “testing-lab,” or “on-premises-only” HDCP licence. Alcohol licences (package store, liquor-by-the-drink) are separate TABC licences that must be held in addition to a hemp retail licence where applicable (Q20). A temporary/event retail licence is mentioned only in secondary TABC-guidance summaries (one application/fee for a recurring event at one location) — Verify; no official event-licence page was opened.

Where to apply (secondary, Verify): TABC's online licensing system is reported as “Mockingbird” (secondary: Hemp Law Group TABC FAQ, citing TABC Retail Applicant Guide and Supplier and Wholesaler Applicant Guide). The TABC FAQ itself directs applicants to tn.gov/abc and gives TABC contact 615-741-1602 / TABC.Info@tn.gov via the TDA transition notice. The Mockingbird URL and the applicant-guide PDFs were not opened directly in this run — Verify before linking them as official.

4.3 Prior HDCP regime — TDA, 2023–2025 (Title 43, Ch. 27, Part 2; 2023 Pub. Ch. 423)

  • Types: Supplier and Retailer only (no separate wholesaler tier).
  • Fees (official summary on capitol.tn.gov): Supplier $500; Retailer $250 per location. Source: Bill Information summary for SB 1007 (114th GA), stating present law under Title 43: SB1007 summary — opened via search result. Requirements in that summary: submit information prescribed by rule; pay the fee; consent to inspection/sampling by TDA or DOR; submit to a criminal-history background check including fingerprint checks against state and federal records.
  • Rules: Permanent TDA rules for hemp producers and HDCP retailers/suppliers, Tenn. Comp. R. & Regs. Chapters 0080-10-02 and 0080-10-03, filed 27 Sept 2024, effective 26 Dec 2024 — TDA notice.
  • End of regime: TDA stopped accepting HDCP applications 5 p.m. CST, 22 Dec 2025; follow-up documentation/payment due 5 p.m. CST, 29 Dec 2025; licences issued in that window expired 30 June 2026. TDA “is no longer processing or issuing new HDC licenses” and directs applicants to TABC (same TDA page, notice dated 6 Jan 2026). Legacy licences remained valid under Title 43 until expiry, then a new TABC application (treated as an initial application, not a renewal) was required, ideally at least 30 days before expiry (TABC FAQ Q1, Q17).

Hemp-producer licence (still TDA, not TABC — for context, cross-referenced to Run 2 Part 7): Fees from the official TDA Hemp Producer Application Guide (1 Apr 2026 version): <5 acres $250; 5–20 acres $300; >20 acres $350; university waived; fees non-refundable; FBI Identity History Summary within 60 days for key participants; proof of Tennessee business registration or local business licence; EIN if applicable. Guide: Hemp Producer Application Guide V5 (1 Apr 2026, PDF). TDA confirms producer requirements are unchanged by Pub. Ch. 526.

4.4 PROPOSED licences in pending medical-cannabis bills — PROPOSED ONLY, none enacted

Do not present any of the following as current law.

HB 872 — “Tennessee Medical Cannabis Act” (companion bill text summarised on capitol.tn.gov)PROPOSED — no enactment, final action, or Public Chapter found in the summary opened; status Verify on capitol.tn.gov before citing. Summary opened: HB0872Commission to licence all medical-cannabis providers: cultivation, processing, transportation, manufacturing, packaging, dispensing, sale, and use. Phase 1: Urban Omni licences — max 12 statewide, max 3 per county, only in Davidson, Hamilton, Knox, Shelby counties, authorising all activities from cultivation to sale in the county; application fee $85,000 ($15,000 non-refundable; up to $38,300 retainable if denied for failure to provide information / pass inspection); 3-year Tennessee residency for majority owner who must serve as officer/executive director; FBI Level 2 background screening for owners/officers/board/managers; up to 3 wellness dispensaries per licence in the primary county. Rural Vertically Integrated (RUVI) licences — max 12 statewide, max 4 per grand division, not in urban-omni counties, cultivation/processing/manufacturing aggregable in adjacent counties; application fee $45,000 ($40,000 refundable if denied; up to $18,300 retainable); up to 3 dispensaries in primary or adjacent county; up to 6 cooperative entities/locations for cultivation/processing/manufacturing. Later stand-alone dispensary, cultivation, and processing/manufacturing licences based on market demand. One majority ownership per person; minority interests in other licences permitted. Transfers/changes of ownership permitted if compliant. Local opt-out by 2/3 vote of county/municipal legislative body by 31 Aug 2025. PROPOSED taxes in the same bill: 9% tax on sales price + up to 2.1% local tax + 10% excise on net earnings for all licensees except RUVI licensees (bill summary, “Taxes and Revenue” section). Two-year General Assembly note: the 114th GA is a two-year session (2025–2026). Bills not enacted by the end of the second year do not carry over as law; they must be refiled in the 115th GA. That is why SB 489 / HB 872 / SB 809, if not enacted in 2026, are dead in their 114th-GA form subject to refiling — confirm final disposition on capitol.tn.gov for each bill before Run 2 finalises “pending” vs. “dead.”

4.5 Social equity

  • Current state programme: NONE found. No social-equity licensing programme, fee waiver, priority, or set-aside exists in Title 57, Chapter 7 or in the TABC FAQ. TABC fees are flat ($500 application + $1,000 / $2,500 / $5,000 annual) with no equity carve-out identified. Secondary assessment agrees: “There are no social-equity licensing provisions, because there is no legal cannabis market to license” (Drug Policy Watch Tennessee brief, secondary).
  • Private, not state: “SEEN Tennessee, the Social Equity Empowerment Network,” launched 1 July 2026 as a trade/education association to help businesses navigate the transition (secondary, Chattanooga / FOX 17 report, July 2026). It is not a state programme and confers no licence advantage.
  • Proposed only: SB 809's claimed disadvantaged/veteran priority (above, Verify, PROPOSED) and a secondary claim that the 2026 medical-study proposal would give TDA-licensed hemp growers up to 5 points in a merit backup pool for forfeited licences (secondary: Hemp Law Group, “Tennessee medical cannabis bill in legislature 2026”) — neither is enacted law.

4.6 Estimated startup costs — NO official Tennessee estimate exists

No official Tennessee estimate of HDCP retail or manufacturing startup cost was located. The figures below are non-official, secondary, and not Tennessee-validated; they are included only because the task requires marking any such estimate as such, and they must not be presented as Tennessee costs:

  • THD Wholesale, “How to Open a Dispensary in Tennessee,” published 1 Feb 2024, updated 10 Sept 2026 (secondary, vendor blog, with a disclaimer that it is not a legal platform): “general proposed cost of opening one ranges from $150,000 to $2 million” for a hemp-derived “dispensary” in Tennessee. Caution: the same page contains outdated THCA claims inconsistent with the July 2026 THCA restrictions; the range is too broad to budget from.
  • CBDistillery, “Starting a CBD Business: Complete CBD Retail Guide 2026” (secondary, national, not Tennessee-specific): total startup $50,000–$250,000, broken down as inventory $10,000–$50,000; licensing/permits $500–$5,000; legal $2,000–$10,000; insurance $2,000–$8,000/year; POS $1,000–$3,000; buildout $5,000–$50,000; marketing $3,000–$15,000; working capital $10,000–$30,000.
  • Financial-model vendors (secondary, generic CBD-shop models, not Tennessee): e.g. $77,000 total capital in one hemp-shop model, $113,500 capex / $340,000 minimum cash in another — not used as a Tennessee estimate.

Official cost floor that CAN be stated (state fees only, before rent, inventory, testing, insurance, legal, local licences, and the wholesaler capital test): Retailer, first year, one location: $500 + $1,000 = $1,500 in TABC fees. Supplier, first year, one location: $500 + $2,500 = $3,000, plus $300 per brand for DOR brand registration. Wholesaler, first year, one warehouse: $500 + $5,000 = $5,500, plus proof of access to $750,000 (capital/bond/line of credit — this is a capacity test, not a fee paid to the state). Hemp producer (TDA): $250–$350 (see §4.3). All other startup costs (lease, buildout to meet the 1,000 sq. ft. wholesaler warehouse rule, barrier/display compliance, testing, insurance, bonding premiums, local business licence) are Unavailable as official Tennessee figures.

6.2 Tax collections by year

See Part 2.5–2.6 for the full series. Summary: Old tax (6% retail): FY 2023–24 actual $12,574,076 (official fiscal note); FY 2025–26 estimate $13,607,203 (pre-repeal baseline, not a collection). New wholesale tax: Feb–Jul 2026 ≈ $1.46m (secondary reporting of Finance tables; Tennessean rounds to $1.4m; April $318k vs $317k and July $427k vs ~$425k are rounding/timing conflicts — see Part 2.6). DOR SUT-112 (official, opened): “retail sales of HDCPs are subject to sales and use tax and through December 31, 2025, an additional 6% sales tax. Public Chapter 526 (2025) repeals the additional 6% sales tax on HDCPs sold effective January 1, 2026.”

6.3 Licensed retailer and manufacturer counts

  • TABC HDCP licensees (all tiers combined, statewide): >1,300 qualified (July 2026); 1,329 licensed at 8 July 2026 from ~1,500 applications; ~1,301 at early July 2026; ~4,400 hemp businesses identified March 2026 (see §3.5 table for sources and Verify flags). No official breakdown by supplier / wholesaler / retailer tier, and no county breakdown, was located — the 1 Oct 2026 tier counts (~1,990 / ~67 / ~180) are secondary only (see Part 5).
  • Legacy TDA HDCP licences: valid to 30 June 2026 only (TDA notice, §3.1). From 1 July 2026 a TABC licence is required to sell HDCPs; TABC's own July 2026 notice states it “will have full jurisdiction over all HDCP manufacturers, wholesalers, and retailers” from 1 July 2026 (secondary summary of TABC notice: Legal Libations TN, accessed 2026-10-25).
  • Wholesale-tax registrants (DOR, distinct from TABC licensees): 22 in January 2026, 48 by July 2026; 17–19 filed a return in any given month; state expected 140 (secondary reporting of DOR records response — see §3.5).
  • Hemp producers (growers): see §6.4. Growers are not HDCP retailers/manufacturers for counting purposes.

6.5 Official price data

  • Farm-gate floral hemp prices (official): NASS table above — $14.10/lb (2023), $5.18/lb (2024), $51.20/lb (2025) for Tennessee floral hemp. No other Tennessee-specific price series was located.
  • Retail HDCP prices: no official price data found — Unavailable. No TABC, DOR or TDA retail price survey was located. Do not use dispensary menus or brand websites as “official price data.”
  • Tax-as-price-proxy (official rates only): wholesale tax $0.02/mg, $50/oz flower, $4.40/gallon liquid (DOR SUT-112, §6.2) — these are tax rates, not prices.

6.6 Cannabis / hemp-friendly banks and credit unions operating in Tennessee

None verified. Do not publish a Tennessee bank list from the sources found. No list of hemp-friendly banks or credit unions was found on the Tennessee Department of Financial Institutions site in searches for this workstream, and no Tennessee-chartered institution was confirmed in an official source as serving Tennessee HDCP businesses. The only list found is a secondary advocacy page, TennesseeCannabis.org, “Cannabis Business Banking in Tennessee,” naming Amalgamated Bank, Bison State Bank, Chase (CBD only), Farmers Bank & Trust, First Citizens Bank, First National Bank of Pasco, Green Check (fintech), KeyPoint Credit Union, Lighthouse Biz Solutions and Safe Harbor Financial (accessed 2026-10-25). Safe Harbor is a fintech platform, not a Tennessee bank. Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): there is no federal cannabis banking law. Hemp businesses are bankable as lawful agricultural businesses in principle, but each institution's policy is its own — Verify with the institution and with TDFI before naming any bank in the main report.
