PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES

Consistency check: No contradiction of the Run 1 baseline was found. Three apparent tensions were resolved: (1) Rule chapter numbers — 0080-06-28 (filed 25 June 2021, effective 23 Sept 2021) is the September 2021 predecessor; the 2026 TDA guide cites 0080-10-01-.03(1) for the current producer licence — cite 0080-10-01 as current, 0080-06-28 only as predecessor, and flag any detail drawn only from the 2021 chapter Verify. (2) USDA plan approval — July 2020 and December 2021 are two different approvals, not one date (see §7.0). (3) TABC transition — TABC began accepting applications 20 Dec 2025, but a TABC licence became universally required only 1 July 2026, consistent with Run 1.

7.0 USDA plan: Tennessee runs its OWN USDA-approved state plan, not USDA direct licensing

USDA AMS state-and-tribal-plan review list (opened 2026-10-25; list updated 17 Feb 2026) lists “Tennessee | TN Approved Plan (pdf)*”, the asterisk meaning approved under the final rule effective 22 Mar 2021. The 17 Feb 2026 date is the list update date, not evidence of a plan amendment on that date. Source: USDA AMS state/tribal plan review (accessed 2026-10-25).

The USDA-hosted Tennessee Hemp Plan PDF (opened) is titled “Tennessee Hemp Plan, Submitted under 7 C.F.R. § 990, Domestic Hemp Production Program.” It states Tennessee's hemp regulatory programme has been in place since 2015; the General Assembly passed Public Chapter 916 in 2014, amended in 2019 by Public Chapter 87 (Attachment A), authorising TDA to promulgate rules; rules were first issued in January 2015 and rewritten in September 2021 (Attachment B). Source: Tennessee Hemp Plan (USDA) (accessed 2026-10-25).

Plan, Attachment A, §43-27-101 (verbatim in substance): “Hemp means the plant cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis.”

Plan, §43-27-102: any person who produces hemp in Tennessee shall obtain an annual licence from the department; to obtain and maintain it, the person must submit a description of all land on which hemp is produced, including GPS coordinates and other information sufficient to identify the property; submit other information prescribed by rule; consent to reasonable inspection and sampling; and not have been convicted of a state or federal felony drug offense within the previous 10 years. Plan, §43-27-104: the Commissioner may deny or revoke licences and issue civil penalties up to $1,000 for each violation; may establish reasonable fees, with fee revenue used exclusively for administration of a hemp regulatory programme by the department.

ApprovalDate / statusEvidence
Interim-final-rule planJuly 2020 (approved earlier in July 2020 for the 2021 season)Secondary: Cannabis Business Times 23 July 2020; Hemp Gazette; NORML (same July 2020 wave: Minnesota, Tennessee, Puerto Rico); CSBS Job Aid 1 July 2021 (approved as of 15 July 2020 but Tennessee continued under 2014 pilot rules); TDA statement republished 2020–2021 (Upper Cumberland Business Journal; Greenhouse Product News) that the 2021 plan “was approved by the USDA earlier this year” but the pilot was extended through 30 Sept 2021.
Final-rule planDecember 2021 — VerifySecondary only: pages stating the Tennessee State Hemp Plan was approved by USDA in December 2021 (announced January 2022); July 2022 CSBS Job Aid states approval under the final rule in 2021. Consistent with the September 2021 rule rewrite and the AMS list's final-rule asterisk, but no official USDA approval letter or TDA announcement for December 2021 was opened. Do not publish a single approval date.
Amendments after Sept 2021None established — VerifyNo specific later USDA-approved amendment or resubmission date was found on any opened official page.

Consequence for producers: Because Tennessee is a state-plan state, the USDA Hemp Public Search Tool (for USDA-licensed producers in non-plan states/territories) is not the authoritative Tennessee producer registry. Verify — no public downloadable TDA hemp-producer list was located; producer licences are issued through a secure TDA portal.

Federal-date response (official): The 14 Nov 2025 TDA notice on the TDA Hemp Industry page states Tennessee's hemp programme “will remain unchanged under existing state law” while federal agencies develop guidance for the federal hemp provision signed 12 Nov 2025, and “The Department's requirements for licensed hemp producers remain unchanged under the state hemp program, which complies with federal requirements.” Source: TDA Hemp Industry page (accessed 2026-10-25). No Tennessee adoption of the 0.4 mg/container cap and no TABC federal-date notice were located (see R1-14, carried forward).

7.1 TDA hemp PRODUCER licence — the only current TDA hemp licence confirmed in the 2026 guide

What it allows: Per the 2026 guide, citing Tenn. Comp. R. & Regs. 0080-10-01-.03(1): “A hemp producer license is required per person, per location for any person who possesses rooted hemp or who cultivates cannabis for introduction into commerce. The hemp producer license authorizes the producer to sell harvested hemp as an unprocessed commodity to wholesalers and distributors.” It does not authorise altering harvested hemp beyond its form as a farm commodity (e.g. trimming, filtering, packaging for component parts) or retail sale — those activities require the applicable TABC licence (see §7.4). The 2025 guide's version of this sentence cited former TDA rules 0080-10-02/0080-10-03, superseded for HDCPs by TABC Title 57, Chapter 7 — do not present the 2025 wording as current HDCP procedure.

Growing area at licensed addressAnnual fee
Less than 5 acres$250
5–20 acres$300
More than 20 acres$350
University (see §7.7)Waived

Fees confirmed in the 2026 guide (opened directly) and matching the Run 1 baseline exactly; fees are non-refundable. Digital licences are generally issued by email within two business days after payment and must be kept on file and posted conspicuously. Source: TDA Hemp Producer Application Guide V5, 1 Apr 2026 (accessed 2026-10-25).

Cycle and renewal: Licensing cycle runs 1 July–30 June annually; licences are issued per physical address, using the address on tax documentation for the property (or a 911 addressing-office address if none exists). Under the 2021 rule text, applicants submit application and fee on or before 1 July each year and licences expire on 30 June following issuance; the current 0080-10-01 renewal clause was not opened in full, so renewal mechanics rest on the guide's cycle statement plus the 2021 rule — Verify for current-chapter renewal wording.

Requirements (2026 guide, opened): Completed application, outlined aerial map, and FBI Identity History Summary (the guide requires the report be dated within 60 days of application) for key participant(s). If the applicant is not an individual or general partnership: proof of registration in its state of incorporation OR a current local business licence, plus federal EIN documentation if applicable. Information submitted may be subject to Tennessee public records law, Tenn. Code Ann. §10-7-501 et seq. TDA may deny any application not completed in accordance with regulatory requirements; material falsification makes the applicant ineligible (2021 rule).

Plot registration (2026 guide, opened): One licensed physical address may have multiple growing areas; GPS coordinates must be provided for each growing area's central-most point in decimal format (not degrees). A growing area is a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure containing the same variety or strain throughout; no more than one variety per growing area; hemp may not be grown outside designated areas. An aerial map of each growing area must be provided, outlined and labelled with the growing-area number matching the application. A growing area is indoors only if fully enclosed and temperature controlled; outdoor size in acres, indoor size in square footage; combined size of all growing areas is the total acreage at the address. All producers are subject to sampling and testing and all associated fees; each variety must be sampled and tested for ≤0.3% total THC under department rules and the USDA Final Rule. USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA) lot numbers (farm/tract/field/subfield) are required: report to the local FSA office within 30 days of crops being started and provide the numbers to TDA via the secure portal; report hemp crop acreage to FSA annually (street location, GPS, acreage, licence number) by USDA's deadline. Each variety and crop started must be reported to TDA by a Harvest Report at harvest through the secure portal; any change from the original application must be reported within 30 days using the Change Application form.

Background check / disqualification: FBI Identity History Summary for key participant(s) (2026 guide). Under the 2021 rule, “key participant” means a sole proprietor, a partner in a partnership, or a person with executive managerial control in a corporation (e.g. CEO, COO, CFO); a person convicted of any drug-related felony in any state or federal jurisdiction within the previous 10 years shall not participate or grow hemp for 10 years from conviction, with an exception for a person lawfully growing hemp under §7606 of the Agricultural Act of 2014 (7 U.S.C. 5940) before 20 Dec 2018 whose conviction also occurred before that date. Current-chapter wording Verify (2021 rule text only).

Non-transferability (2021 rule): Licences are not transferable from person to person or location to location; fees are not refunded for early termination. Current-chapter wording Verify.

Info link / contact (official, public): TDA Hemp Industry page: tn.gov/agriculture/farms/hemp-industry.html (accessed 2026-10-25). TDA Plant Certification Section (public programme contact printed on the guide): P.O. Box 40627, Nashville, TN 37204; 615-837-5137; industrial.hemp@tn.gov. The guide itself states: “This information is guidance only and does not supplant rule or law.” Note: the older number 615-837-5070 is superseded — do not publish it (resolved in Part 9, see §9.1).

7.2 Hemp BROKER and hemp PROPAGATOR licences — 2021-rule types; current status Verify

Under the 2021 rule (Chapter 0080-06-28-.02, opened): “Hemp broker means a person that purchases and sells hemp plants grown by others” and “Hemp propagator means a person that produces clones or seedlings for retail sale.” Under 0080-06-28-.03, broker and propagator licences each carried a Tier 7 fee, and a combination of producer/broker/propagator licences cost a single fee equal to the highest amount requested. Source (2021 rule PDF): SOS Rule 0080-06-28 (2021) (accessed 2026-10-25).

Current status is Verify: The 2026 producer guide describes only a producer licence and cites only 0080-10-01-.03(1); no broker or propagator licence type, application, or fee was confirmed in the current chapter excerpts opened (Justia 0080-10-01-.06, violations, confirms only that rooted hemp may not be sold/supplied/moved to a person not licensed under that division of rules). The Tier 7 dollar equivalent ($300, by mapping Tier 7 to the 2026 guide's 5–20-acre producer fee) is an inference, not a confirmed current fee — do not publish broker/propagator fees as current without opening the current 0080-10-01 fee text. Do not confuse broker/propagator with the separate Plant Dealer Licence for reselling seedlings/clones (§7.6, secondary only) — the two regimes overlap in subject matter but were not shown to be the same licence.

7.3 Movement permit (not a licence type)

Under the 2021 rule (.05, opened): licensees shall not move rooted hemp plants without a valid movement permit issued by the department, and shall not move any hemp to anyone who treats or transforms harvested hemp for distribution in commerce without a valid movement permit. Movement permits are required per vehicle per day; a request may require hemp licence number, origin and destination, date of intended movement, and weight, volume, or number of units. Current-chapter status Verify: the current 0080-10-01 movement-permit mechanics were not opened; Justia 0080-10-01-.06(f) (violations) confirms rooted hemp outside a licensed growing area is prohibited unless under immediate transport to another licensed growing area or a licensed HDCP manufacturer, distributor, or retailer, which implies a transport regime continues, but permit mechanics are not confirmed. Source (secondary reproduction of current chapter, violations section): Justia 0080-10-01-.06 (accessed 2026-10-25).

7.4 Processor / handler — CORRECTION: no separate current TDA hemp processor/handler licence; under TABC, processing = supplier licence (CONFIRMED)

No separate current TDA hemp processor or handler licence was established on any official page opened. The 2021 rule defines producer, broker, and propagator only; it defines no standalone “processor” or “handler” licence.

Secondary (pre-TABC) position, labelled as such: Miller & Martin PLLC via JD Supra (2019) states that in June 2019 TDA announced updated regulations applying only to hemp growers, meaning hemp processors would no longer be required to be licensed or registered with TDA under the hemp programme — except that processing hemp for human consumption required a food manufacturing facility licence (see §7.5). Hemp Alliance of Tennessee states the same: if not processing for human consumption, no TDA licence or registration is required for processing hemp. Sources (secondary): JD Supra / Miller & Martin (2019); Hemp Alliance of Tennessee, Licenses (accessed 2026-10-25).

TABC position — confirmed on the official TABC FAQ (opened): Q11: “Anyone processing hemp (with any amount of THC) within the state in any form, intermediate processing or final processing, must obtain a license from the TABC.” Q3: TDA retains oversight of hemp cultivation and agricultural production; a hemp farmer selling crops to manufacturers of consumer products does not need a TABC licence. Conclusion: under the current regime, processing for HDCPs = TABC supplier licence (§7.8). There is no separate TABC “processor” licence distinct from supplier. Secondary reporting (Hemp Law Group) confirms manufacturers fall under the supplier licence, including out-of-state companies that contract for manufacturing and sell into Tennessee. Source (official, opened): TABC FAQ (accessed 2026-10-25).

7.5 Food manufacturing licence/permit (for hemp products for human consumption)

Official TDA food-manufacturing licence exists (general): TDA's “Starting a Food Manufacturing Business in Tennessee” page states: “All manufacturers are required to obtain a license in accordance with Tennessee Food, Drug and Cosmetic Act and Chapter 0080-04-13”; plans and specifications must be submitted to TDA Consumer & Industry Services before construction/remodelling/conversion, and construction cannot begin until TDA review and approval; applications go through TDA's online application with an Application Checklist. Source: TDA food manufacturing startup page (accessed 2026-10-25).

Application to hemp specifically is secondary-only: the statement that processing hemp for a product for human consumption (tincture oil, gummies, chocolate, water, honey, anything ingested) requires a TDA Food Manufacturing Permit rests on Hemp Alliance of Tennessee and the 2019 Miller & Martin summary; the TDA food page excerpt does not mention hemp. Mark the hemp-specific application Verify against a TDA hemp/food page. Note the interaction with §7.4: a hemp food product that is also an HDCP would additionally need the applicable TABC licence; the food licence does not substitute for it.

7.6 Seed, plant-dealer, and nursery licences — secondary only; NOT confirmed on official TDA pages

TN Seed Sellers License (secondary only): Hemp Alliance of Tennessee states all seed sales, including hemp, are regulated in Tennessee and seed sellers are required to obtain a TN Seed Sellers License requested directly from TDA; a 2019 Miller & Martin summary states TDA will not issue licences to certified seed breeders, but “anyone manufacturing, distributing, or labeling seed should be licensed through TDA's Ag Inputs section.” An official TDA Feed/Seed/Fertilizer/Grain Forms page exists and contains a Seed section, but the excerpt opened did not state a seed-seller licence requirement, fee, or hemp application. Status: Verify on an official TDA page; do not present as a confirmed hemp licence type. Sources: Hemp Alliance, Licenses; TDA inputs forms page (accessed 2026-10-25).

Plant Dealer License (secondary only): Hemp Alliance states a person buying and reselling seedlings or clones needs a Plant Dealer License for each location where plants are sold, offered for sale, distributed, or held for distribution on a commercial basis. No official TDA plant-dealer page was opened. Status: Verify. Note the overlap with the 2021 propagator licence (§7.2) — whether these are the same or different licences is unresolved.

Hemp nursery licence: No distinct “hemp nursery” licence type was located on any official or secondary page checked. A nursery activity would fall under the producer licence (growing/clones for retail sale was the 2021 propagator definition) and/or the Plant Dealer Licence above. Status: Unavailable as a distinct licence type.

7.7 University / research licences — two DIFFERENT concepts; do not merge them

  1. University producer fee waiver (confirmed, 2026 guide + 2021 rule): The annual licence fee is waived for an institute of higher education that offers programmes of study in agricultural sciences seeking licensure for a growing area on university property (2021 rule .03(10); 2026 guide fee table: “University — Waived”). This is a fee waiver on the ordinary producer licence, not a separate licence type.
  2. Low-THC research-track exclusion (Run 1 baseline, not re-verified here): the separate exclusion under §39-17-402(16)(E) for DEA-certified four-year public or private institutions of higher education conducting a clinical research study (less than 0.6% THC oil). Carry forward from Run 1 only; no new verification in this session.
  3. No separate CCB-style research licence, no TDA research licence type was located. Unavailable as a distinct licence. See Part 11, §11.9 for university research work itself.

7.8 TABC hemp-derived cannabinoid product (HDCP) licences — cross-reference to Run 1 Part 4; fees RE-CONFIRMED, no change

Run 1 Part 4 fees are unchanged and were re-confirmed on two opened official sources in this session: the TABC FAQ (Q15) and the official TABC Fees page (opened directly). The Fees page also confirms TABC payments run “through the RLPS and Mockingbird systems,” which partially verifies the Mockingbird portal's existence (applicant guides themselves were not opened — Verify). Sources (official, opened): TABC Fees page; TABC FAQ (accessed 2026-10-25).

TABC licenceApplication feeAnnual feeWhat it allows
Supplier$500$2,500Manufactures/produces HDCPs (processing in any form, intermediate or final, in-state — FAQ Q11); sells finished, packaged HDCPs to wholesalers. Manufacturers fall here, including out-of-state suppliers selling into Tennessee (FAQ Q22: out-of-state suppliers must obtain a TABC licence; it is an offense to manufacture, wholesale, or sell HDCPs in Tennessee without a valid licence).
Wholesaler$500$5,000 per warehouse locationDistributes HDCPs to retailers. Requires proof of access to at least $750,000 (may include bonds and lines of credit) and a warehouse of at least 1,000 sq. ft. solely used for wholesale storage (FAQ Q18; architectural diagram with square footage/dimensions; receiving/inventory/storage/packaging documentation; business-records storage, security, and compliance plan; detailed business plan including capital documentation).
Retailer$500$1,000Sells HDCPs to the public at a single, contiguous premises only; a separate licence is needed per location (FAQ Q6).

Multiple licences permitted: FAQ Q4/Q13 — a person may hold multiple TABC hemp licences, including different types; unlike alcohol three-tier law, there is no prohibition on holding interests in more than one type.

Application requirements (FAQ Q18, opened): legal name and DBA; physical address of all locations (mailing address if different); name of any individual with an ownership interest; biographic/contact information for each owner (date of birth, Social Security number, mailing address, email, telephone); registered agent contact if applicable; copy of a background check from TBI or FBI for any individual owner, member-owner, or shareholder; verification of Tennessee Secretary of State registration if operating under a legal entity; architectural diagram including square footage and dimensions; deed or lease showing property rights; certificate of occupancy; business hours; identity/contact of any person legally responsible for management not previously disclosed. Statutory cites given as §§57-7-106(b)(1)(A) and 57-7-106(f).

Disqualification (FAQ Q19, opened): anyone currently serving a sentence for a felony drug offense, or applying within 10 years from conviction of a felony drug offense, is ineligible, subject to analysis under the Fresh Start Act, Tenn. Code Ann. §62-76-104.

Transition (FAQ Q1/Q8/Q12/Q17, opened): TDA licences remained effective until expiration, most continuing through 30 June 2026; legacy holders remained subject to 2023 Public Chapter 423 until 30 June 2026 and must obtain a TABC licence to operate beyond that date; TABC began accepting hemp licence applications 20 December 2025, with licences approved before 1 January 2026 effective 1 January 2026; TDA licensees must submit a new TABC application treated as an initial application, not a renewal; legacy licensees were encouraged to apply at least 30 days before expiration.

Retail restrictions (FAQ, opened): Retail licensees are prohibited from direct or indirect delivery or shipment to consumers and from vending-machine or self-checkout sales (Q5/Q7; legacy TDA retail licensees could continue those activities only until licence expiration). Retail sales are 21+ only. Location rule (Q21): local ordinances do not dictate where a hemp retailer may be located, but state law restricts sales within 1,000 feet of a primary or secondary education facility, with the only exception being businesses documenting hemp sales prior to 31 December 2023 (business records, point-of-sale documentation, photographs, video). Supply chain from 1 July 2026 (Q23/Q24): a TABC-licensed retailer may obtain HDCPs only from a TABC-licensed wholesaler; a retailer may continue selling compliant inventory already in its possession before 1 July 2026 even if obtained from another source, but may not obtain additional HDCPs from any other source after that date.

Product scope (FAQ Q9/Q10, opened): topicals intended to be absorbed through the skin are regulated HDCPs; products with non-detectable THC do not require a TABC licence; products at or below 0.3% total THC are legal only if they meet all other requirements; products above 0.3% total THC are illegal; total THC = (THCA × 0.877) + Δ9-THC; THCp and synthetic cannabinoids are prohibited in any amount.

Offense (FAQ Q14, opened): manufacturing, producing, wholesaling, or selling HDCPs without a valid licence is a Class A misdemeanour, with product subject to seizure and forfeiture under §§53-11-451 and 57-7-203(a)(2) as cited in the FAQ. Carry forward Run 1's flag: the §57-7-203 citation is a possible typo for §57-7-103 — Verify.

FAQ wording flag (carry forward): FAQ Q20's text on retail categories describes licensed businesses “restricting entry to customers under 21” — this appears to be a FAQ wording error; Run 1 correctly states 21+-only premises. Do not quote that phrasing without the note.

Inspections (FAQ Q16, opened): new locations are inspected at application, annually with each renewal application, and by random inspections, which may include minor compliance checks. Renewal fee amounts are not separately stated on the Fees page — Verify whether renewal equals the initial annual fee.

TABC rules (secondary only, Verify against SOS text — see Part 11 for the rules as actually read in Run 2): Rule Chapter 0100-15 (suppliers and wholesalers) and Chapter 0100-16 (retail sale), filed as emergency rules 26 December 2025; permanent versions filed 10/11 March 2026 (secondary sources differ by one day) and effective 9 June 2026. In Part 11, the SOS PDFs for both chapters were read in full and the 9 June 2026 effective date is confirmed on the rule documents themselves.

Secondary 2026 amendments — flag for verification, do NOT change Run 1: Hemp Law Group (secondary) reports 2026 Public Chapter 698 (SB1761) rewrote the HDCP supplier definition, set QR-code/certificate-of-analysis hosting rules, lowered the warning-statement minimum to six-point font, and barred TDA from issuing or renewing HDCP supplier and retail licences after its effective date (wholesaler licences are not named in §10); and 2026 Public Chapter 1127 (SB2295) added a 10% wholesale tax on inhalable liquid HDCPs in cartridge form effective 1 July 2026. Neither was verified on capitol.tn.gov in the Part 7 session. If confirmed, they are additions to — not contradictions of — Run 1 Part 4, and they do not change the fees above. Source (secondary): Hemp Law Group, July 1 2026 transition (accessed 2026-10-25).

Caps (Run 1 baseline, unchanged): 15 mg per serving; 300 mg per package (general); 30 mg per beverage container; 500 mg per cartridge; 0.5 oz per flower package; 6 mg per pouch; age 21+; 0.3% total/total-theoretical THC. Not re-verified in the Part 7 session; carried forward from Run 1.

7.9 DOR hemp brand registration — a registration, NOT a licence type

Brand registration is $300 per brand per year, paid by the supplier (Run 1, secondary-reported). Access is reported via TNTAP at tntap.tn.gov/eservices, choosing “View Approved Brands and Products Lists,” then “Hemp Brand Directory.” TNTAP was not opened directly in this session — Verify. No CSV/XLSX download or export button was reported.

Secondary note (CBD Marketer, 17 October 2026, reporting TABC guidance updated 16 October 2026): neither a TABC business licence nor a DOR brand registration establishes that an individual HDCP complies with state requirements; Tennessee provides no product-by-product preapproval through either process; suppliers and wholesalers remain responsible for the products and batches they introduce. Secondary — Verify against the TABC guidance itself before citing. Source (secondary): CBD Marketer (17 Oct 2026) (accessed 2026-10-25).

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES (TENNESSEE ONLY)

8.0 Verdict — No Tennessee property was verified as expressly cannabis-zoned with a current price and active status

Tennessee has no marijuana licensing programme, and no cannabis-specific zoning category was located in any listing or official source checked. What exists is hemp: three properties that mention hemp in their marketing were located, but all three fail the active-and-recent test or the hemp-dedicated test — each is therefore marked Status: Verify, not active. Two of the three do not even state a zoning type, and the one that does states only general agricultural zoning (“A”), not cannabis-specific zoning. The tables below keep hemp-mentioned, hemp-adjacent, and excluded properties strictly separate. Do not present any entry below as a currently available cannabis-zoned property.

Context for why the count is so low: under TABC FAQ Q21, local ordinances do not dictate where a hemp retailer may be located (the binding state restriction is the 1,000-foot school rule); and secondary Hemp Alliance material states there are no zoning requirements for a hemp licence. Hemp cultivation is therefore marketed, when it is marketed at all, as general agricultural potential — not as a cannabis zoning class. Sources: TABC FAQ Q21 (accessed 2026-10-25); Hemp Alliance FAQs (secondary, accessed 2026-10-25).

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

AUDIT CORRECTIONS — Part 10. No Tennessee-headquartered tribe and no Tennessee tribal cannabis/hemp programme — but MBCI trust land in Lauderdale County, TN (taken into trust 2012–2013) is part of the Mississippi Band of Choctaw Indians reservation (Mississippi-seated tribe; it creates no Tennessee tribe, plan or operation). AG Opinion 06-165, as an opinion not law: 89 individuals' recognition remained in force; the one organisation's recognition had effectively expired after 1995 — not a blanket “no organisation” holding. See Audit table, Part 10.1.

10.1 Tribal finding

Tennessee has no federally recognised tribe, no currently state-recognised tribe, no tribal reservation, and therefore no tribal cannabis/hemp operation, licence, compact, or tribal hemp plan in TN. Sources: NAIA FAQ (“Tennessee does not have any federal or state recognized Tribes”), TN Library Association, Native Languages project. Official BIA Indian Entities Recognized list itself NOT yet opened — final official check remains open. Wikipedia “Multiple states: Mississippi Band of Choctaw Indians” under TN is flagged as likely multi-state listing artefact (tribe's seat/reservation in Mississippi) — do not cite Wikipedia as authority; resolve on BIA list.

USDA tribal plans: No TN tribal plan in compilations checked (PMC content analysis, status 14 July 2021; CSBS Mar 2025). EBCI has an Independent Approved Plan — in North Carolina, not TN. AMS plan-review page itself not opened in that session — Verify.

State recognition history: TCIA granted recognition 19 June 2010 to 6 groups (Remnant Yuchi Tribe; United Eastern Lenape Nation of Winfield TN; Chikamaka Band; Central Band of Cherokee; Cherokee Wolf Clan; Tanasi Council), opposed by EBCI Principal Chief Michell Hicks. Agreement order in Davidson County Court (reported 7 Sept 2010, Cherokee One Feather) declared those actions void for Open Meetings Act violations; TCIA sunshined out 1 July 2010, defunct. AG Opinion 06-165 (2006, reproduced copy only — Verify) already concluded no organisation held official recognition then. Unrecognised groups must not be presented as recognised tribes or licence holders.

10.2 Interstate — TN law controls on entry in every case

  • Marijuana offences §§39-17-417/418 apply regardless of lawful purchase elsewhere. TN does not recognise another state's medical card. Only exception is low-THC oil §39-17-402(16)(F) with documentation — not reciprocity, does not authorise flower/other forms.
  • EXCLUDE StateRegsToday cross-border claims (flat “trafficking up to 15 years/$60,000”, “DOH reciprocity temporary card”, “no CBD exceptions”) — all contradicted by Run 1. Do not cite StateRegsToday for interstate law.
  • HDCP in/out: shipping to TN retail licensee or consumer strictly prohibited §57-7-106(b)(4); face-to-face only §57-7-106(b)(5); shipping/delivery service to consumer unlawful §57-7-113(a), penalties $1k/$5k/$10k §57-7-113(b); outbound shipping preserved §57-7-113(c) if lawful at destination; out-of-state suppliers must hold TABC licence (FAQ Q22); B2B transport §57-7-109 requires (1) documentation of lawful USDA-approved state/tribal plan or USDA-licence production + cannabinoid compliance, and (2) bill of lading (owner/address, origin, delivery point, kind/quantity, shipment date). Raw hemp grower movement permits ≠ HDCP retail delivery.
  • Online: No TN “online seller registry” / approved shipper list located; DOR Brand Directory lists brands/wholesalers/counties, not lawful direct-to-consumer shippers.
  • Interstate agreements: No TN interstate cannabis compact/agreement located — label as search result as of 2026-10-25, not proved negative, until capitol/TABC/AG “compact”/“agreement” search is run.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

AUDIT CORRECTION — Part 11. All grower-rule citations to Chapter 0080-06-28 (2021) in this Part are superseded as current law by Chapter 0080-10-01; 0080-06-28 is the predecessor only, and full 0080-10-01 text was not read — Verify. Warning font: statutory minimum 6-point under 2026 Pub. Ch. 698 (see Audit table). DEA-lab enforcement: delayed to 31 Dec 2026 (baseline). Under-18 and under-21 employee rules coexist; for retailers the stricter under-21 rule controls in practice (codification pinpoint open).

Scope: Each item separately for (a) hemp growers (TDA, Title 43, Chapter 27; Rule 0080-06-28; USDA-approved Tennessee Hemp Plan; 7 CFR Part 990) and (b) HDCP businesses (TABC, Title 57, Chapter 7; Rules 0100-15 and 0100-16). All sources searched or opened 2026-10-25. Pages were opened as text fetches, not by live-browser navigation; no sign-in, form, purchase or click was performed. Where a fact rests only on a search-result excerpt or a secondary source, it is labelled as such.

Rule versions actually read in full this session (this resolves Run 1 gap R1-12 for these chapters):

  • Tenn. Comp. R. & Regs. 0100-15, Rules for Suppliers and Wholesalers of HDCPs — SOS PDF, emergency rules filed 26 Dec 2025, new rules filed 11 Mar 2026, effective 9 June 2026: 0100-15 (9 June 2026)
  • Tenn. Comp. R. & Regs. 0100-16, Rules for Retail Sale of HDCPs — same administrative history, effective 9 June 2026: 0100-16 (9 June 2026)
  • Tenn. Comp. R. & Regs. 0080-06-28, Hemp (TDA) — SOS PDF, repeal and new rule filed 25 June 2021, effective 23 Sept 2021 (no later version was located in that session; the SOS file served is the 2021 version): 0080-06-28 (2021)
  • TABC official FAQ (opened in full, 24 questions): TABC FAQ
  • USDA AMS DEA-registered hemp laboratory directory (page 1 opened): USDA DEA labs directory
  • USDA Tennessee Hemp Plan (prior session, not re-read line-by-line): Tennessee Hemp Plan
  • TDA Hemp Producer Application Guide V5, 1 Apr 2026 (prior session): 2026 Guide

Standing limitation carried from Run 1 (unchanged): Tennessee's official Code publisher is Lexis (advance.lexis.com), which could not be opened directly. Statute quotations below marked “Justia reproduction” were read from Justia's Tennessee Code reproduction and must be re-checked against official Lexis text / Secretary of State publication before reliance as legal authority.

11.0 Headline corrections and confirmations against the Run 1 baseline — read first

  1. ISO 17025 for HDCP labs is now CONFIRMED in rule text, not just secondary. Rule 0100-15-.06(5)(a)2(iii)–(vi) requires a Commission-registered third-party laboratory to be “fully accredited to standards established under [ISO] 17025 by an [ILAC]-recognized accreditation body,” maintain that accreditation, and test/report with limits of detection/quantitation no greater than the rule tolerances (and cannabinoids at LOQ <1 mg/g). The TABC webinar secondary claim is therefore confirmed on ISO 17025, but see (2) and (3).
  2. DEA registration is NOT a TABC HDCP-lab registration criterion in Rule 0100-15-.06(5)(a)2 as read. The 2023 JD Supra summary stating operators “must contract with a testing lab that is ISO 17025 accredited and registered with the DEA” describes the old TDA-era bill summary and must NOT be carried forward as current TABC law. DEA registration is a separate federal requirement for hemp compliance laboratories under 7 CFR Part 990 (see §11.1(a)), with a dating conflict flagged below.
  3. “Automatic licence cancellation” for a bad COA was NOT found in rule text. Rule 0100-15-.06(5)(a)3 says failure to adhere to lab/COA requirements “is grounds for denial or revocation of any registration or authorization issued by the Commission” — discretionary grounds, not automatic cancellation. The webinar secondary claim overstates this. Likewise the secondary “QR within 3 navigational steps / max 150 links / retain 12 months after last sale or 90 days past expiry” details do not appear in Rule 0100-15-.07 as read; the rule requires only a QR code linking to a valid COA, and states a QR code that does not link to a valid COA “will be considered invalid and a violation of this rule.” Those extra QR details may come from 2026 Pub. Ch. 698 — Verify before inclusion.
  4. No Metrc/BioTrack seed-to-sale system — CONFIRMED by full-text read. A text find for “Metrc” in the full 0100-15 PDF returned no matches, and the full 0100-16 PDF (645 lines, read in full) contains no seed-to-sale system. Traceability in both chapters is batch number + COA + invoices/bills of lading + Commission inspection/sampling (see §§11.1–11.2). Run 1's negative finding stands; R1-20 is resolved for these chapters.
  5. No insurance, camera, discount, or standalone bond rule in either TABC chapter. Text finds in 0100-15 returned no matches for “insurance,” “bond,” or “camera,” and in 0100-16 no matches for “discount.” The $750,000 wholesaler figure is a financial-eligibility proof (bond is one accepted form of proof), not a bond minimum — see §11.2(b).
  6. Wholesaler annual fee — a secondary source is wrong. A Mondaq summary states a $500 annual wholesaler fee. The official TABC FAQ Q15 states: $500 application fee for all types; annual fees Supplier $2,500; Wholesaler $5,000; Retailer $1,000. Use the TABC FAQ figure; the Mondaq figure appears to confuse the application fee with the annual fee.
  7. §50-1-304 employment point — now resolved from statute text (Justia 2024 reproduction, Verify on Lexis). §50-1-304(d) protects only an employee discharged solely for use of “an agricultural product not regulated by the alcoholic beverage commission.” Since HDCPs are now ABC/Commission-regulated, that protection does not by its own terms cover HDCP use. Combined with §57-7-111 (no employer accommodation duty, no HDCP-user protection — Run 1 baseline), there is no HDCP-user employment protection. This resolves Run 1 gap R1-19 in substance, subject to the Lexis check.
  8. Under-18 vs under-21 employee handling — CONFLICT flagged, do not merge. Run 1 baseline (§57-7-103(b)(7)) states an offence to knowingly employ a person under 18 for physical manufacture, storage, sale or distribution of HDCPs. A LegiScan summary of enacted HB 0072 (2025, eff. 1 July 2025) states it “prohibits a retailer from knowingly allowing an employee younger than 21 to access, handle, or sell” HDCPs on the premises, with a 48-hour jail / $500 mandatory minimum for knowing sale to a person under 21. Both are reported; the current §57-7-103 text must be checked on Lexis to determine whether the under-21 employee rule supersedes or sits alongside the under-18 rule.

11.1 Testing: required tests, sampling rules, failed tests and recalls, accredited labs

(a) Hemp growers

What is tested, and how (Rule 0080-06-28-.06, SOS 2021 text, opened): Sampling by the department or a department-trained-and-approved designated representative; a sample collected under commissioner-approved uniform protocols “shall be deemed representative of the location, growing area, or lot from which the sample was obtained.” “Within 30 days prior to the anticipated harvest the department or a designated representative shall collect samples from the flower material for THC level testing.” “During a scheduled sample collection, the producer or an authorized representative shall be present at the growing site.” “A producer shall not harvest the hemp crop prior to samples being taken.” “Samples from different lots shall not be comingled.” Protocols “define the preparation and analysis of hemp samples for the quantitative determination of cannabinoids, including delta-9-THC, on a dry weight basis and is conducted in a manner similarly reliable to postdecarboxylation.” “Any sample test result higher than 0.3% THC concentration on a dry mass basis shall be conclusive evidence that one or more cannabis plants from the area sampled contains a THC concentration in excess of that allowed under the Act and shall be grounds for destruction or remediation.” “Licensees shall pay a Tier 4 laboratory analysis fee under T.C.A. § 43-1-703(f) for each sample tested by the department.” Dollar amount of the Tier 4 fee was not located in the rule — Verify against the current §43-1-703(f) schedule / TDA fee schedule. (Run 1 reported grower licence fees of $250 / $300 / $350 for Tier 6 / 7 / 8 from the 2026 TDA guide; the rule itself gives only Tier labels — see §11.3.) “The department and growers may utilize private laboratories for testing official samples, if the laboratory meets the standards set by USDA for such work and the sample is sent directly to the laboratory by the sampler. The department's laboratory shall serve as the reference laboratory for all samples.” TDA's laboratory uses LC-MS/MS (Tennessee Hemp Plan §2, prior session): “Total THC = (Conc THCA × 0.877) + Conc Δ9-THC, on dry-weight basis.”

Failed pre-harvest tests, negligence, remediation (Rule 0080-06-28-.07, opened; statute §§43-27-106/107 as reproduced in the Hemp Plan, prior session): Rule .02 definitions (opened): “Disposal means an activity that transitions non-compliant hemp into a non-retrievable or non-ingestible form. Such activities include, but are not limited to plowing, tilling, or disking plant material into the soil; mulching, composting, chopping or bush mowing plant material into green manure; or burying plant material into the earth and covering with soil”; “Remediation means the process of rendering non-compliant cannabis, compliant by removing and destroying flower material, while retaining stalk, stems, leaf material, and seeds. Remediation can occur by shredding the entire plant into a biomass like material, then re-testing the shredded biomass material for compliance.” Violations include “(i) Failure to destroy or remediate non-compliant hemp” and “(j) Failure to comply with a stop movement order.” “All negligent violations shall be corrected using a corrective action plan” with, at minimum, a reasonable correction date, periodic progress reporting, and a department inspection to determine implementation. “Hemp producers do not commit a negligent violation under this paragraph if they make reasonable efforts to grow compliant hemp and it does not have a THC concentration of more than 1% on a dry weight basis.” “A producer that negligently violates the Act or this chapter three (3) times within a five (5) year period shall be ineligible to produce hemp for five (5) years beginning on the date of the third violation.” “Culpable violations shall be reported to the U.S. Attorney General and the chief law enforcement officer of the state.” Statute §43-27-106 (Hemp Plan reproduction): commissioner may issue a written stop movement order or written destruction order; proof of testing >0.3% but not >1.0% is prima facie evidence of a negligent violation; prima facie evidence of a culpable mental state includes testing >1.0%, three unlicensed-rooted-hemp violations in five years, or violating a stop movement/destruction order; a person whose licence is revoked for violation is ineligible for reissuance for at least five (5) years.

Performance-based sampling / microgreens — NOT in Rule 0080-06-28 as read. The SOS 2021 rule chapter contains no performance-based sampling, variety re-testing cycle, microgreen 25% testing, or greens/transplant provision. Those concepts appeared only in search excerpts from the Tennessee Hemp Plan document; Verify in the full Hemp Plan PDF text before stating them as rules. Do not import them from the rule chapter.

Federal overlay (7 CFR Part 990): Under §990.3, compliance laboratories must meet criteria including quality assurance, validated fit-for-purpose methods, demonstrated validity, sensitivity, disposal procedures for non-compliant samples, measurement of uncertainty reported with results, and grinding for homogeneity. The rule text as reproduced states: “After December 31, 2022, States/Tribes shall require only DEA-registered laboratories may conduct testing under this section.” Dating conflict — flag explicitly, do not resolve silently: the USDA AMS Hemp FAQ as searched still states laboratories must be DEA-registered “by December 31, 2025” (extended 14 Nov 2024 from 1 Jan 2025), while the user federal baseline states USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing is delayed to 31 Dec 2026. All three dates are reported with their sources; the operative enforcement date for Tennessee state-plan growers is Verify against the current USDA notice. USDA FAQ also states: sampling agent collects no more than 30 days before anticipated harvest; sampling agent may NOT prepare (dry/grind) the sample — laboratories prepare samples; for state-plan producers, contact the state programme for sampling requirements, as they differ; USDA FSA reporting deadline is 15 July annually (in addition to Tennessee's 30-days-from-licence rule — see §11.3).

Accredited / DEA-registered labs serving Tennessee growers — what is verified and what is not:

  • TDA Technical Services Laboratory, Ellington Agricultural Center, Nashville (official TDA page, opened via search result): ISO/IEC 17025-accredited quality system; sample types include hemp; analytes include cannabinoids, pesticides, heavy metals, foodborne pathogens. The page states: “To meet the requirements of the USDA Interim Final Rule for hemp, the laboratory is in the process of registering with the DEA. All requirements are expected to be accomplished by the end of this calendar year.” The page is undated in the search index — do NOT present the TDA lab as DEA-registered; its current DEA status is Verify. TDA Technical Services Laboratory.
  • USDA AMS DEA-registered laboratory directory (opened, page 1): the first ~30 alphabetical entries include labs in Virginia, Florida, Indiana, California, Texas, Utah, Arkansas, Wisconsin, New York, Kentucky, South Dakota and Minnesota — no Tennessee-located lab appears in that slice, and state-filter search excerpts returned no Tennessee entry, but the full Tennessee-filtered directory was NOT opened in that session, so “no Tennessee DEA-registered lab” is NOT established — Verify by opening the Tennessee filter. Directory base: USDA DEA laboratories.
  • Named Tennessee labs found only secondarily (do NOT present as TABC- or DEA-certified without the registry checks above): Galbraith Laboratories, Knoxville — A2LA ISO 17025:2017 accreditation, described as the first cannabis testing lab accredited in Tennessee (Cannabis Industry Journal archive, old, date not stated — current status Verify); New Bloom Labs, Chattanooga (6121 Heritage Park Dr., Chattanooga, TN 37416; ISO/IEC 17025:2017 Cert# AT-2868 per a 2026 FarmToMed COA PDF) — proves it issued a COA in 2026, not TABC certification or DEA registration; Harvest Analytical Lab, West Knoxville (2019 WVLT news report — old, not current proof); SC Labs (vendor page listing Tennessee for hemp compliance testing — marketing, not an approval list); ACS Laboratory, Sun City Center, FL (vendor page: “recently applied to test hemp in Tennessee and Kentucky and [is] continuing due diligence” — i.e. not confirmed as Tennessee-certified).
  • TABC qualified-lab registry for HDCPs (§57-7-107(d)(2)–(3)) was NOT located as a published list in that session. The rule requires registration plus a landing page per lab (see 11.1(b)), but no public registry page with lab names was opened. Unavailable/Verify — check the TABC Public Information and Forms page via live browser if a named list is required.

(b) HDCP businesses (suppliers, wholesalers, retailers)

Statute baseline (Run 1, §57-7-107): full-panel testing on all active cannabinoid molecules before final production + potency test on finished goods; supplier must contract with a third-party laboratory certified by the Commission; each batch tested with a COA; Commission must maintain/post a registry of qualified labs and set pass/fail action levels by rule; Commission testing by post-decarboxylation, §57-7-107(d)(4). The rule below is where the action levels now live.

Rule 0100-15-.06 — Sampling and Testing (opened in full). Frequency: “HDCP supplier licensees must sample and test each batch of product created from hemp or hemp products for conformance with this rule.” “Once full panel testing required by this rule is conducted on hemp or a hemp product, additional testing in downstream commerce is not required except as provided”: (1) if a downstream supplier uses the product as an input and during production it undergoes a chemical change (e.g. heat or solvents) or reconstitution (addition/removal of components), each batch of the new HDCP must be sampled and tested; (2) if the downstream supplier does not alter chemical composition/formulation (e.g. raw flower only cut/filtered, or bulk repackaged into smaller units of like product), additional sampling/testing is not required and prior test results are “presumptively valid” — but this “shall not limit the Commission's authority to test any cannabis products for compliance.” “Prior to transport of any HDC product in commerce, HDC suppliers must confirm conformance of the batch to all testing requirements under this rule.”

Rule 0100-15-.06(2) — Standards / action levels (opened). Verbatim operative sentence: “Any test result exceeding allowable limits is grounds for embargo, recall, remediation, or destruction of the entire batch represented by the sample, regardless of whether the test result is discovered through manufacturing testing or subsequent sampling and testing of retail HDC product.”

Category (all HDCPs, §.06(2)(a))Limit
Prohibited analytes (no numeric tolerance printed in extracted rule text)Tetrahydrocannabiphorol (THCp); THC-O Acetates; Tetrahydrocannabiphorol acetate (THCP-O); Synthetic cannabinoids — treated as prohibited, consistent with TABC FAQ Q10 (“prohibits products containing THCp or synthetic cannabinoids in any amount”).
MicrobialShiga toxin-producing E. coli undetectable in at least 1 gram; Salmonella spp. undetectable in at least 1 gram.
MycotoxinsTotal Aflatoxin (sum of B1, B2, G1, G2, if determined individually) <20 µg/kg; Ochratoxin A <20 µg/kg.
Microorganism tableAspergillus fumigatus, flavus, niger, terreus — Not detected in 1 gram; Salmonella spp. — Not detected in 1 gram; Shiga toxin-producing E. coli — Not detected in 1 gram; Total Yeast and Mold Count (TYMC) <10,000 cfu/g; Total Aerobic Microbial Count (TAMC) <100,000 cfu/g.
Heavy metalsArsenic <0.2 ppm; Cadmium <0.2 ppm; Lead <0.5 ppm; Mercury <0.1 ppm.
Water activity (Rule .04(2)(c) / .05(2)(c))Edibles (infused products, gummies): aW <0.85 compliant. Inhalable (pre-roll, flower, oil, vape cartridge): aW <0.65 compliant. Suppliers/wholesalers prohibited from supplying HDCPs that have failed aW testing.

§.06(2)(b) “Additional testing requirements for HDCPs” repeats, in the text as extracted, only: Microbial — Aspergillus A. fumigatus, A. flavus, A. niger, A. terreus (undetectable in at least one gram); Heavy metals — Arsenic <0.2 ppm, Cadmium <0.2 ppm, Lead <0.5 ppm, Mercury <0.1 ppm. (No separate product category heading was visible in the extracted text for this subsection — Verify the subsection heading in the PDF before attributing (b) to a specific product class.)

Residual pesticides (ppm, CAS numbers in the rule; full table as extracted, Rule 0100-15-.06(2)(a)4): Abamectin 0.5; Acephate 0.4; Acequinocyl 2.0; Acetamiprid 0.2; Aldicarb 0.4; Azoxystrobin 0.2; Bifenthrin 0.2; Bifenazate 0.2; Boscalid 0.2; Carbaryl 0.4; Carbofuran 0.2; Chlorantraniliprole 0.2; Chlorfenapyr 1.0; Chlormequat chloride 0.2; Chlorpyrifos 0.2; Clofentezine 0.2; Cyfluthrin 1.0; Cypermethrin 1.0; Daminozide 1.0; DDVP (Dichlorvos) 0.1; Diazinon 0.2; Dimethoate 0.2; Ethoprophos 0.2; Etofenprox 0.4; Etoxazole 0.2; Fenpyroximate 0.4; Fenoxycarb 0.4; Fipronil 0.4; Flonicamid 1.0; Fludioxonil 0.4; Hexythiazox 1.0; Imazalil 0.2; Imidacloprid 0.4; Kresoxim-methyl 0.4; Malathion 0.2; Metalaxyl 0.2; Methiocarb 0.2; Methomyl 0.4; Methyl parathion 0.2; Myclobutanil 0.2 (prohibited at any concentration for inhalation); Naled 0.5; Oxamyl 1.0; Paclobutrazol 0.4; Permethrins 0.2 (cumulative cis- and trans-isomers); Phosmet 0.2; Piperonyl butoxide 2.0; Prallethrin 0.2; Propiconazole 0.4; Propoxur 0.2; Pyrethrins 1.0 (cumulative pyrethrin 1, cinerin 1, jasmolin 1); Pyridaben 0.2; Spinosad 0.2; Spiromesifen 0.2; Spirotetramat 0.2; Spiroxamine 0.4; Tebuconazole 0.4; Thiacloprid 0.2; Thiamethoxam 0.2; Trifloxystrobin 0.2. Any test result exceeding these limits is grounds for embargo, recall, remediation, or destruction of the entire batch (Rule .06(2)).

Residual solvents/manufacturing chemicals (ppm, Rule .06(2)): Acetone 1,000; Benzene* 2; Butanes (cumulative n-butane + iso-butane) 1,000; Ethanol 5,000; Ethyl Acetate 1,000; Heptanes 1,000; Hexanes* (cumulative n-hexane, 2-methylpentane, 3-methylpentane, 2,2-dimethylbutane, 2,3-dimethylbutane) 60; Methanol* 600; Pentanes (cumulative n-pentane, isopentane, neo-pentane) 1,000; 2-Propanol (IPA) 1,000; Propane 1,000; Toluene* 180; Total Xylenes* (cumulative 1,2-, 1,3-, 1,4-dimethylbenzene and non-xylene ethylbenzene) 430; any other solvent not permitted for use — undetected. *Asterisked solvents “are not individually approved for use. Due to their possible presence in other solvents that are approved for use, limits have been listed here for concentrations in final products.”

Supplier/wholesaler product bars, Rule .04(1) / .05(1): each product must contain a unique batch number; HDCPs may not contain nicotine; dimethylsulfoxide (DMSO) may not be used in any HDC product. Inhalable bars, Rule .04(2) / .05(2): no inhalable product made with a non-hemp-derived cannabinoid ingredient unless the ingredient is listed in, and concentration/route authorized under, the FDA inactive ingredient database (FDA IIG database); no inhalable product in whose manufacture any of the following were used: Vitamin E acetate; Medium-chain triglycerides; Polyethylene glycol; Propylene glycol; 2,3-butanedione. Solvents, Rule .04(3) / .05(3): suppliers/wholesalers barred from providing HDCPs in which solvents were used in manufacture, except: water, vegetable glycerin, vegetable oils, animal fats, butane, propane, carbon dioxide, ethanol, isopropanol, acetone, heptane, ethyl acetate, pentane. Butane/propane/heptane/pentane must be documented on the COA as at least 99% purity (supplier COA to be maintained two years); the others must be food grade under FDA standards, 21 CFR Part 174.

Sampling method, Rule .06(3)–(4): “HDC product manufacturers must draw random samples for testing that are representative of each batch. For testing purposes, the sample must be in the final retail sales packaging” — for each of: Flower; Oral (capsules, liquids, tinctures); Pre-rolled flower; Oils for vaporization; Topical; Transdermal — in each case “Samples must be packaged in final packaging as it would be sold to the consumer.” “Acceptable packaging for testing purposes includes, but is not limited to, sealed vaporization cartridges, edibles in mylar bags, flower in jars, pre-rolled flowers in tubes, and bottles of tincture. Final packaging, for testing purposes, does not require complete regulatory labeling, but, at minimum, must include the product name and form, specific unique lot or batch number, and net contents. While awaiting the COA, the remainder of the batch must be stored to prevent degradation, adulteration, contamination, or mix-up until such time that the final packaging can be applied.”

Laboratory registration and COAs, Rule .06(5)(a)–(b): “COAs required under this chapter may be supplied by a third-party laboratory provided the laboratory is registered with the Commission.” To register and maintain registration, the lab applicant must: complete a full application in a Commission-approved method; “Host and notify the Commission of one (1) landing page for retrieval of each applicable COA”; for any test method under the rule, be fully ISO 17025-accredited by an ILAC-recognized accreditation body and maintain that accreditation; test/report analytes using limits of detection and quantitation no greater than the rule tolerances; test/report hemp-derived cannabinoids using LOQ <1 mg/g; perform/report component testing as detailed in the rule; “Store all samples in a secure manner that reasonably protects them from degradation, contamination, and tampering; and, prior to its disposal, render all sample material unusable”; if available, produce reserve sample material to the Commission on request; provide other information required. “Registration is valid for two years from the date of approval.” Each COA must include at minimum: lab name/address as registered; manufacturer name/address; date lab received the sample; batch number and sample type; unique identifying information if applicable; a photo and the state of the sample received (e.g. finished packaging) that “must clearly indicate the batch number on the product sample”; sample history including date received and date range of each test; analytical methods, limits of detection, limits of quantitation, and test results for each analyte evaluated “regardless of whether the testing conducted is required by this rule”; testing method information (name, equipment, date of last validation); result for each type of analysis plus a collective “pass”/“fail” assessment for the entire batch accounting for passage of all or failure of any one test; attestation via signature of an authorized representative (e.g. lab director or QA/QC member) and date issued; date COA generated/reported; laboratory accreditation information. Quantitative results must include corresponding units as required for tolerances plus measurement uncertainties. “< LOQ” for below-limit-of-quantitation; “ND” for tested-and-not-detected. “Retention samples must be maintained under registered conditions for two years, with access limited to laboratory personnel.” Labelling tolerance, Rule .06(5)(c)3: “The measured cannabinoid content percentage for any product with a label claim content must be within fifteen percent of the label amount for each compound listed. If a wholesaler labels the package, the wholesaler must label the HDCP with cannabinoid content that is listed on the COA. The ±15% variance only applies when HDCPs are labeled on the package prior to the wholesaler taking possession for compliance testing.”

Failed tests, retesting, remedy, recall — Rule .06(5)(c) (opened): Retesting: “Any sample failure may be resubmitted as follows for confirmation of testing failure.” If a reserve sample was retained by the same Commission-registered lab that produced the failing COA, that lab may retest the reserve sample; the manufacturer may request a retest within seven (7) days of being notified of the failure. If the retested sample passes for the suspect component(s), a new sample from the same batch must be drawn and submitted to a second Commission-registered lab for complete retesting of all components; retesting may not be at a lab under the same ownership as the initial lab or a lab subcontracted for the initial testing. If the second retesting conforms to all tolerances, “the batch is deemed compliant with testing requirements and may be transported and distributed in commerce.” If a reserve sample is not available or if a sample fails either retest, “the batch is deemed nonconforming with regulatory requirements, and the batch must be held for destruction or remediation.” Remedy — microbial: supplier prohibited from transporting/allowing transport of a batch that failed microbial testing unless (I) further processed by a method that effectively sterilizes the batch, retested, and results conform; (II) supplier submits a corrective action plan for effective sterilization and receives written Commission approval; or (III) the batch is rendered unusable. Remedy — over-concentrated (THC): manufacturer prohibited from transporting/allowing transport unless (I) further processed by a method that effectively dilutes the batch, retested, and results conform; (II) manufacturer submits a corrective action plan for effective dilution and receives written Commission approval; or (III) the batch is rendered unusable. Remedy — all other component failures: “an HDCP manufacturer must render the batch unusable prior to disposition.” Recall consent (current TABC rules, not just former TDA rules): Rule 0100-15-.11(1)(f) — persons subject to the chapter must “Consent to recall of all associated HDCP batches when subsequent testing of HDCPs in commerce indicates a failure of testing requirements under this chapter, or a foodborne outbreak or other illness is causally linked by federal authorities or the Department of Health to particular HDCP batches.” Rule 0100-16-.07(1)(f) imposes the identical consent duty on retailers (failure under 0100-15-.06, or outbreak/illness linked by federal authorities or the Department of Health). Retail-level testing, Rule 0100-16-.06(3): “Upon purchase of HDCPs offered for retail sale, the Commission may sample and test or cause to be sampled and tested the product for compliance with the Act and the applicable Commission rules. Any test result exceeding allowable limits is grounds for embargo, recall, and remediation pursuant to provisions applicable under Tenn. Comp. R. & Regs. 0100-15-.06, and destruction of the batch of HDCP represented by the sample.” A sample collected/tested per Commission protocols “is deemed representative of the HDCP batch from which the sample was obtained.” No “automatic cancellation” and no recall-notice procedure (e.g. consumer notification, return logistics, timelines) was found in either chapter — Verify whether any recall procedure exists in statute (§57-7-117) or TABC guidance; do not invent one.

Records supporting testing traceability: Supplier Rule .09(1): for each batch manufactured/distributed, maintain for two years: COAs (copies submitted to all immediate downstream purchasers); current safety data sheets for all solvents used; invoices and bills of lading for all distribution. Retail Rule .05(1): for each HDCP offered for sale, maintain for two years and readily produce on request: COAs received from the immediate upstream seller; invoices and bills of lading for purchase/receipt. Transportation Rule .08(1): supplier licensees must make COAs “immediately available, upon request by the Commission, for any HDCP, including raw product, that is transported in commerce.”

11.2 Licence rules: transfers, ownership changes, renewals, relocation, suspension/revocation, insurance/bonding

(a) Hemp growers (TDA, Rule 0080-06-28, opened)

  • Transfer — prohibited: “Licenses issued under this chapter are not transferable from person to person or location to location.” (Rule .03(5).)
  • Relocation: No separate relocation procedure exists in the rule; because a licence is issued “to each person for each physical address where the person grows or possesses rooted hemp” (Rule .03(1)) and is non-transferable location-to-location, a new address requires a new licence application for that address. The rule states only a 30-day notice duty for contact-information changes (below) — it does not state that a growing-area change can be made by amendment. Verify against the 2026 TDA Hemp Producer Application Guide's Change Application form (prior session: guide states any changes from the original application “MUST be reported to TDA within 30 days using Change Application form on secure portal”) before stating a Change Application covers a new growing area.
  • Ownership changes: No ownership-change procedure in the rule. The application requires name/DOB of key participants and FBI Identity History Summary for key participants (see §11.8); a change in key participants is not expressly addressed — Verify against the TDA guide / Change Application. A change of the licensed person is a transfer and therefore prohibited as above.
  • Renewal / annual cycle: “License applicants shall submit an application and license fee to the department on or before July 1 of each year. … Licenses expire on June 30 following their issuance.” There is no TABC-style renewal-tolling provision in this chapter; the cycle is annual reapplication by 1 July. “The department will not refund fees for early termination.” “The department may deny any application for licensure that is not completed in accordance with this rule.” “Any person who materially falsifies any information in their application shall be ineligible to participate in the program.”
  • Suspension/revocation: The rule chapter does not use a suspension/revocation procedure section; enforcement is via §43-27-104/106 (commissioner may deny/revoke licences and issue civil penalties up to $1,000 per violation; stop movement/destruction orders; see §11.1(a)) and Rule .07 violations/corrective action plans/5-year ineligibility (3 negligent violations in 5 years; revocation for violation → ineligible for reissuance at least 5 years, per §43-27-106). No hearing procedure is stated in the rule chapter — Verify under the Uniform Administrative Procedures Act / §43-27-104.
  • Insurance or bonding minimums for growers: NONE LOCATED in Rule 0080-06-28 or in the Hemp Plan sections previously read. Do not import a grower bond/insurance requirement.

(b) HDCP businesses (TABC, Rules 0100-15 and 0100-16, opened)

  • Transfer — prohibited (both chapters): Rule 0100-15-.01(4) and Rule 0100-16-.01(4): “Licenses under this chapter are not transferable from person to person or location to location.” Rule 0100-15-.01(3) / 0100-16-.01(3): “The Commission will not refund fees for early termination of any license issued under this chapter.”
  • Ownership / application-content changes — 30-day notice, not a transfer: Rule 0100-15-.03(6) (suppliers/wholesalers) and Rule 0100-16-.03(4) (retailers): “Licensees must notify the Commission, in a manner approved by the Commission, of any changes to the contents of their approved application within thirty (30) days of the change, including any change in contact information.” Whether a change of ownership or a relocation is effected by this 30-day notice or requires a new application/licence is NOT stated in either rule — Verify against TABC applicant guides (Supplier and Wholesaler Applicant Guide; Retail Applicant Guide, cited in the Hemp Law Group FAQ source list but not opened). Given the express non-transferability rule, the safe synthesis is: notice covers changes to application contents; a new person or new location needs a new licence — but label the new-licence inference as such.
  • Renewals (both chapters, opened): Licences expire one (1) year from issuance, unless the licensee holds more than one TABC-issued licence of that type and the Commission prorated the fee to align expiration dates. Annual fee due on approval, paid in full before issuance; may be prorated in the initial year or after obtaining additional licences, provided the total prorated fee does not exceed the annual fee. Renewal is the licensee's responsibility: submit to annual inspection (if applicable / annual inspection for retail), complete renewal application in the Commission-specified manner, provide an updated criminal background check for each applicable individual, remit the annual fee before expiration. The expiration date printed on the licence “serves as notice”; no additional notice is required. If both application and fee are not received by expiration, the licence “will be closed on the business day after expiration.” If application and fee are received before expiration, closing is tolled and the licence “remains valid until the Commission reviews the application.” The applicant must resolve outstanding issues / submit additional documentation “no later than 30 days after expiration” for renewal processing. Divergence between chapters: supplier/wholesaler licences the Commission does not renew “within 30 days of expiration will be closed” (0100-15-.03(9)); retail licences the Commission does not renew “will be closed 45 days after the license expiration date” (0100-16-.03(7)). Renewal is complete when the Commission issues an updated licence. The Commission may deny any application it deems incomplete (0100-15-.03(10); 0100-16-.03(8)).
  • Transitional denial authority for prior Title 43 violations (both chapters, opened): Supplier/wholesaler (0100-15-.03(1)): for 1 year from the date of violation, the Commission may deny a licence application by a person/entity previously licensed under Title 43 by TDA if the applicant committed a violation of Title 43 for selling HDCPs without a proper licence, selling products failing former §43-27-209(a), or selling any controlled substance as defined by Title 39. Retail (0100-16-.03(1)): for 2 years from the date of violation, on a broader trigger — a violation of Title 43 relating to cultivation, manufacture, distribution, or retail sale of hemp or HDCPs. “Violation” in both means either a finding in a final order by TDA or another state agency under Title 43 or a court under Title 39, or conduct with no adjudication where the Commission, after written notice of intent to deny and opportunity for an evidentiary hearing, determines by a preponderance that it constituted a violation. Denial is discretionary, considering nature/seriousness, compliance history, corrective actions, and time elapsed. The retail provision states it “applies during the transitional period following transfer and remains in effect unless/until superseded by permanent Commission rules governing applicant eligibility.”
  • Multiple licences / vertical integration (official TABC FAQ, opened): Q4: “There is no statutory restriction on individuals or entities holding multiple hemp-related licenses including different types of licenses (supplier, wholesaler, and retailer).” Q13: three general licence types “like alcohol regulation” but “there is no prohibition against a person having interests in more than one of those three license types.” Q20 retail categories: (i) licensed businesses restricting entry to under-21s [sic — read as 21+-only premises]; (ii) TABC-licensed retail package stores also holding a hemp retail licence; (iii) TABC-licensed liquor-by-the-drink establishments also holding a hemp retail licence (on-premises consumption only); (iv) establishments otherwise licensed as hemp supplier, wholesaler, and retailer at the same physical location where manufacturing occurs. A secondary vertically-integrated warehousing exception (supplier selling its own product at retail on the same premises need not warehouse it through its contracted wholesaler, with each licence's activity in a separately designated area) is reported by Hemp Law Group, citing §§57-7-104, 57-7-106 — secondary only, Verify against statute/applicant guides.
  • Suspension and revocation — grounds stated in the rules (no hearing procedure stated): Rule 0100-15-.11(5) and Rule 0100-16-.07(5): “Each violation of the Act or this chapter is grounds for issuance of [embargo / hold] or destruction orders for any HDCP held by the violator or their agent, denial or revocation of any license or registration issued by the Commission, actions for injunction, imposition of civil penalties, and pursuit of criminal charges against the violator.” Rule 0100-15-.09(3): refusal of access, refusal to open/disclose records, refusal to furnish information, or furnishing false/misleading records/information “subjects the licensee to suspension or revocation.” Rule 0100-15-.11(3) / 0100-16-.07(3): “Violation of any workplace safety or environmental protection standard enforced by state or federal authorities is grounds for denial of program inspection and denial or revocation of any license issued by the Commission.” Rule 0100-15-.11(4) / 0100-16-.07(4): “A person is responsible for violations of the Act or this chapter when committed by either the person, their agent, or their employee.” No suspension/revocation hearing, notice-period, or interim-operation procedure appears in either chapter as read — Verify under Title 57, Chapter 7 (§57-7-117, whose authority is cited in both chapters) and the UAPA.
  • Civil penalty schedule — secondary only, Verify on Lexis: Hemp Law Group (secondary, last reviewed 5 Oct 2026) reports $1,000 first violation; $2,500 second within 2 years of the first; $5,000 third within 2 years of the first; revocation for a fourth within 2 years of the first; plus employee retraining. The same schedule appears verbatim in the 2023 Title 43 bill text (former §43-27-207(d)(2)). The current §57-7-117 schedule was not verified in that session — report the schedule as secondary/Verify, not as confirmed current statute.
  • Criminal / seizure exposure (official TABC FAQ, opened): Q14: “It is a Class A misdemeanor to manufacture, produce, wholesale, or sell [HDCPs] in Tennessee without a valid license. Additionally, any HDCP sold or offered for sale in violation of the licensing requirement is subject to seizure and forfeiture pursuant to Tenn. Code Ann. § 53-11-451 and 57-7-203(a)(2).” Q5: TABC retail licensees “are prohibited from direct or indirect delivery or shipment of HDCPs to consumers” and “must conduct all sales in person at licensed retail establishments” (legacy TDA retail licensees could continue delivery only until their licence expired 30 June 2026). Secondary direct-to-consumer civil penalties ($1,000 / $5,000 / $10,000) reported by Hemp Law Group are secondary, Verify.
  • Insurance minimums for HDCP businesses: NONE LOCATED in Rules 0100-15/16 as read (text find for “insurance” in 0100-15: no matches).
  • Bonding minimums for HDCP businesses: NO standalone bond minimum in the rules. The only bonding concept is wholesaler financial eligibility (below), where a bond is one accepted form of proof. Text find for “bond” in 0100-15: no matches.
  • Wholesaler financial eligibility (statute + official FAQ): TABC FAQ Q18 (opened): wholesalers only must provide “Proof that applicant has the financial means to engage in distribution (documentation of access to at least $750,000, which may include bonds and lines of credit).” Rule 0100-15-.03(5) requires, for wholesalers in addition to the core supplier items: security plan; architectural diagram including how product will be received, inventoried, stored, and packaged; owner affidavit confirming warehouse space meets §57-7-106(f)(1)(E)(i); records storage and records security policy; Certificate of Occupancy; detailed business plan; and “proof of financial eligibility as set forth in §57-7-106(f)(1)(E)(iv)” [the $750,000 proof]. Secondary sources (Hemp Law Group; EIN Presswire surety-bond vendor release, 7 Aug 2026) agree the $750,000 is a total to be proved and a bond need only cover the gap above liquid funds (example: $200,000 bank statement + $550,000 bond), and that TABC has published a surety bond form on its Public Information and Forms page — the surety form itself was not opened; Verify on tn.gov. The warehouse must be at least 1,000 sq ft solely used for wholesale storage (FAQ Q18; Rule .03(5) affidavit).

11.3 Cultivation rules for hemp: licensing, plot registration, pre-harvest sampling, indoor/outdoor rules

Licensing (Rule 0080-06-28-.03, opened; TABC FAQ Q3/Q11 boundary): “A hemp license is required to possess rooted hemp and is issued to each person for each physical address where the person grows or possesses rooted hemp.” Application on department forms, completed in full, including: name; date of birth of any key participant; proof of state incorporation registration or local business licence (non-individual / non-general-partnership applicants); contact information including key participants, telephone, email, principal place of business address; “Address of the location to be licensed for growth of hemp and description of all growing areas at the location, including total number of growing acres and Global Positioning System (GPS) coordinates from the areas' central most points”; criminal history report for key participants in the form of the FBI Identity History Summary; other information required. Annual fee under §43-1-703(f) by total growing area at the licensed address: less than 5 acres — Tier 6; 5–20 acres — Tier 7; more than 20 acres — Tier 8. Broker licence Tier 7; propagator licence Tier 7; combination producer/broker/propagator — single fee equal to the highest amount. Dollar amounts are not in the rule; Run 1 reported $250 / $300 / $350 for <5 / 5–20 / >20 acres from the 2026 TDA guide — retain as guide-sourced, Verify against the current §43-1-703(f) schedule. Annual licence fee waived “for any institute of higher education that offers programs of study in agricultural sciences seeking licensure for a growing area on university property” (see §11.9). TABC FAQ Q3/Q11 boundary (opened): “The Tennessee Department of Agriculture will retain regulatory oversight of hemp cultivation and agricultural production. A TABC license is only required if a cultivator engages in manufacturing, distributing, or selling hemp-derived products directly to consumers.” / “A hemp cultivator who merely grows and sells raw hemp does not require a TABC license. Anyone processing hemp (with any amount of THC) within the state in any form, intermediate processing or final processing, must obtain a license from the TABC.” This is the grower/HDCP boundary for Run 2: growing + selling raw hemp = TDA only; any in-state processing = TABC licence required.

Plot registration (Rule .03 + .04, opened; 2026 TDA guide, prior session): Growing areas are registered by address + total acres + GPS coordinates of each area's central-most point (Rule .03(2)(e)). “Growing area means any contiguous land area licensed for the growth of hemp. Bifurcation of a growing area by roads, fencing, or the like shall not render the area non-contiguous under this definition.” (Rule .02(g).) “Lot means a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure containing the same variety or strain of cannabis plant throughout the area.” (Rule .02(k).) USDA Reports (Rule .04): “All producers shall report hemp crop acreage to the USDA Farm Service Agency within 30 days of obtaining a license,” providing street address and GPS location of the lot/greenhouse/building/site, acreage (or greenhouse/indoor square footage), and hemp licence number. 2026 TDA Guide (prior session, secondary-official guidance): USDA FSA lot numbers (farm, tract, field, subfield) REQUIRED; report to local FSA office within 30 days of crops being started and provide to TDA Plant Certification Section via secure online portal; each time a crop is started, contact the local FSA office; each variety/crop started MUST be reported to TDA by Harvest Report at time of harvest via secure portal; all producers MUST report acreage to FSA annually by the USDA deadline (USDA FAQ: 15 July annually); any changes from the original application MUST be reported within 30 days using the Change Application form on the secure portal; checklist includes printing/posting the TDA hemp producer licence conspicuously.

Pre-harvest sampling: See §11.1(a) — within 30 days before anticipated harvest, department/designated representative collects flower-material samples; producer or authorized representative present; no harvest before sampling; lots not comingled; >0.3% = conclusive evidence + grounds for destruction/remediation.
