Secondary Hemp Alliance of Tennessee FAQs (prior session) add that growers should contact TDA / HATS (Hemp Administrative Testing and Screening, an independent third-party sampling service) at least 30 days before harvest and that all growers are sampled/inspected at least once per year — secondary only, Verify against the TDA guide/plan; the rule itself does not state a “contact 30 days before harvest” duty or an annual-minimum inspection frequency.

Indoor/outdoor rules: No separate indoor-growing rule was located. Indoor growing is contemplated only through: the “Lot” definition (field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure); FSA reporting of “greenhouse or indoor square footage”; and the guide's FSA lot reporting for greenhouse/building sites. No indoor-specific lighting, HVAC, energy, security, or setback rule for hemp growers was found in Rule 0080-06-28 — state this as none located, not as “indoor growing is unregulated” in general (general building/fire/electrical codes were not researched). Outdoor growing is the default: contiguous licensed growing area, GPS-registered, movement permits for rooted plants (Rule .05 — see §11.2(a)), inspection/sampling access during normal business hours (Rule .06(1); §43-27-104(a)(8)). No plot-size cap, plant-count cap, or distance/setback rule for hemp growing areas was located in Rule 0080-06-28. (The 1,000-ft school buffer applies to HDCP retail locations, not to hemp growing areas — do not transfer it to growers.)

11.4 Security requirements, including storage and display rules for HDCPs

(a) Hemp growers

No fence, camera, alarm, guard, vault, or lock specification for hemp growing areas was located in Rule 0080-06-28, §§43-27-101 et seq. as reproduced in the Hemp Plan, or 7 CFR Part 990 as read. Grower “security” in Tennessee law is administrative/physical-access control: licensed growing area with GPS coordinates; movement permits (per vehicle per day) for rooted hemp and for hemp moved to anyone who treats/transforms it for commerce; department entry during normal business hours to inspect plants, records, and material; and sampling access. State the absence explicitly; do not import other states' or marijuana security codes. Storage: No grower storage rule (warehouse, locked storage, inventory system) was located beyond the licensed-area and movement-permit framework. Harvested hemp possessed for transportation to a processor is within Rule .01 scope (possesses harvested hemp for transportation to anyone who treats/transforms it for distribution in commerce) and requires a movement permit under Rule .05.

(b) HDCP businesses

Wholesaler security plan — required, contents not specified in the rule: Rule 0100-15-.03(5)(h) requires a “Security plan” as part of the wholesaler application, and .03(5)(m) requires a “records storage and records security policy.” Neither the rule text as read nor the sections read specify camera, alarm, guard, vault, fencing, or lock contents for that plan — Verify against the TABC Supplier and Wholesaler Applicant Guide. Do not invent numeric security specifications. Supplier applications (Rule .03(3)) do not list a security plan; retailer applications (Rule 0100-16-.03(3)) do not list a security plan.

Storage (HDCP): Wholesaler application architectural diagram must describe “how product will be received, inventoried, stored, and packaged” (Rule .03(5)(i)); warehouse ≥1,000 sq ft solely used for wholesale storage (FAQ Q18; affidavit under §57-7-106(f)(1)(E)(i)). Batch awaiting COA: “the remainder of the batch must be stored to prevent degradation, adulteration, contamination, or mix-up until such time that the final packaging can be applied” (Rule .06(4)). Lab sample storage: secure manner reasonably protecting against degradation, contamination, tampering; render sample material unusable before disposal; retention samples under registered conditions for two years, access limited to laboratory personnel (Rule .06(5)(a)2(ix), (b)5). Retail inventory: areas where HDCPs are sold, offered, or held for inventory must be maintained readily accessible for inspection, with adequate lighting (Rule .07(1)(a)–(b)); full warrantless access to facilities, inventory, records, invoices (Rule .07(1)(c)). Supplier/distributor areas and vehicles: same readily-accessible + adequate-lighting + full warrantless access duties (Rule .11(1)(a)–(c)). No temperature, humidity (other than water-activity testing), refrigeration, vault, or safe specification for HDCP storage was located in either chapter — state as none located. “Distribute” includes holding: Rule 0100-15-.02 defines “Distribute” to include “holding for subsequent sale or manufacturing” (prior session). Secondary Hemp Law Group conclusion follows: storing/warehousing product for later sale requires a licence even if storage is the only activity at the location. Label the inference as secondary-derived from the rule definition.

Display and manner of sale (statute + Rule 0100-16-.04, opened): Rule .04(1): “HDCP retail licensees must not sell any HDCP to a purchaser unless the purchaser has provided proof of age showing the purchaser is, at least, twenty-one (21) years of age.” Rule .02 defines “Proof of age” as a valid driver's licence or other unexpired government-issued ID describing the individual, indicating age, containing a photograph/likeness, and appearing on its face to be valid. Rule .04(2): “HDCP retail licensees may not offer HDCPs for sale through use of vending machines or unstaffed kiosks or self-checkout.” Rule .04(3): “HDCP retail licensees may offer HDCPs for sale only if the product is maintained behind the counter, except as authorized in T.C.A. § 57-7-104(d), in a location or manner that requires physical assistance from the licensee for the customer to access the product.” Rule .02 defines “Counter” as “a physical barrier that necessitates seller's assistance to access product prior to sale.” Statute §57-7-104 (Run 1 baseline, to be re-checked on Lexis): “barrier” = point of purchase having actual physical separation between displayed HDCPs and the consumer, or another designated area inaccessible to the consumer requiring retail clerk assistance; signage in all areas where HDCPs are displayed, clearly advising/warning that HDCPs may have intoxicating effects and cause impairment; HDCPs only displayed in an area constantly visible to a retail licensee employee; no self-checkout or vending-machine sale; HDCP maintained behind a barrier unless (a) the retailer is a 21+-only premises under §57-7-104(b)(1), or (b) the product is a hemp beverage in a container ≥12 fl oz. Rule .02 defines a “Business for which entry is limited to individuals 21+” as a legal establishment that affirmatively restricts access at all times to persons 21+ by requiring each person attempting entry to submit for inspection a valid government-issued photo ID demonstrating age at least 21. Violation is a Class A misdemeanour under §57-7-104(e) (Run 1 baseline). Rule .04(4) (warning sign, opened): “HDCP retail licensees must post, in a conspicuous location, a warning sign that informs customers that HDCPs may be intoxicating and cause impairment. The letters comprising the word 'WARNING' shall be highlighted black lettering, and 'WARNING' should be larger than all other lettering on the sign. The size of the sign shall be approximately thirteen inches (13″) by nine and one-half inches (9½″).” Rule .04(5) (sourcing, opened): “HDCP retailers are prohibited from purchasing HDCPs from any person that is not an HDCP licensed wholesaler.” TABC FAQ Q23 supplies the effective date and sell-through: from 1 July 2026, a TABC-licensed retailer may obtain HDCPs only from a TABC-licensed wholesaler (a wholesaler licence list is stated to be available on the FAQ page); compliant inventory already in possession before 1 July 2026 may continue to be sold even if obtained from another source, but no additional product may be obtained from any other source after that date (Q24, same conditions: ≤0.3% total THC, no THCp, QR-linked COA, all other Title 57 requirements). Supplier manner of sale (Rule .04(4)): “HDC suppliers are prohibited from selling HDCPs for resale in Tennessee to any person that is not an HDC licensed supplier or wholesaler.” Wholesaler manner of sale (Rule .05(4)): wholesalers “are prohibited from selling or offering to sell HDCPs for resell in Tennessee to any person that is not an HDCP licensed wholesaler or retailer.” Retail licences are per single contiguous premises; separate licence per location (FAQ Q6, opened). Temporary locations (fairs, flea markets, farmers markets) may be licensed as fixed-address facilities' temporary locations under Rule .03(2), but “licence fees for temporary locations cannot be prorated on basis of temporary use.” Secondary Hemp Law Group guidance on recurring temporary events (one licence covering multiple non-consecutive dates at the same event/location; LBD Festival/Special Occasion licence interaction for public, non-age-restricted events) is secondary and stated by that source as not definitive — Verify against TABC before inclusion.

11.5 Waste disposal rules, including non-compliant hemp destruction

(a) Hemp growers

Disposal methods (Rule .02(d), opened — see §11.1(a)): plowing/tilling/disking into soil; mulching/composting/chopping/bush mowing into green manure; burying and covering with soil — in each case rendering non-compliant hemp non-retrievable or non-ingestible. Remediation (Rule .02(o), opened): removing/destroying flower material while retaining stalk, stems, leaf, seeds; or shredding the entire plant into biomass-like material, then retesting the shredded biomass. Enforcement trigger: >0.3% test result = grounds for destruction or remediation (Rule .06(5)); failure to destroy/remediate is itself a violation (Rule .07(1)(i)); stop movement/destruction orders under §43-27-106 (Hemp Plan reproduction). Federal verification (7 CFR §990.3(a)(6), prior session): disposal either by a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement, or on site at the farm/hemp production facility; verification by in-person State/Tribal representative verification or alternative proof such as pictures, videos, or other proof disposal/remediation occurred. Tennessee Hemp Plan §4 adopts by reference the USDA Remediation and Disposal Guidelines for Hemp Growing Facilities (15 Jan 2021). No grower landfill, incineration, composting-permit, or hazardous-waste rule specific to hemp was located — the Rule .02(d) methods (including composting into green manure and burial) are the operative disposal methods. General solid-waste law was not researched.

(b) HDCP businesses

Failed-batch disposition: held for destruction or remediation after failed retests (Rule .06(5)(c)1(iii)); microbial failures — sterilize + retest, or Commission-approved corrective action plan, or render unusable; THC over-concentration — dilute + retest, or Commission-approved corrective action plan, or render unusable; all other component failures — render unusable prior to disposition (Rule .06(5)(c)2). Lab sample material must be rendered unusable before disposal (Rule .06(5)(a)2(ix)). Disposal documentation (both chapters, opened): Supplier Rule .09(2) and Retail Rule .05(2): for any HDCP rendered unusable or disposed under the chapter, maintain documentation for two (2) years following disposal of: (a) date(s) and manner(s) rendered unusable/disposed; (b) batch number; (c) total volume disposed. Embargo / hold / destruction orders: Rule .11(5) (suppliers/wholesalers) — each violation is grounds for embargo or destruction orders; Rule .07(5) (retail) — each violation is grounds for hold or destruction orders; Rule .11(2)(g) / .07(2)(e) — violating a Commission order, including orders for embargo, destruction (supplier/wholesaler) or hold/dispose (retail), is itself a violation. Retail testing failures are grounds for “embargo, recall, and remediation pursuant to [0100-15-.06], and destruction of the batch” (Rule .06(3)(a)). Seizure/forfeiture (official FAQ Q14, opened): unlicensed product subject to seizure/forfeiture under §§53-11-451 and 57-7-203(a)(2). Former TDA-era embargo/recall/remediation/destruction language (Rules 0080-10-02-.10 / 0080-10-03-.07, eff. 26 Dec 2024) is historical — those chapters governed TDA licences that expired 30 June 2026; the current operative recall/destruction duties are the 0100-15/16 provisions above. Do not present the TDA-era rules as current TABC duties. No HDCP-specific landfill, incineration, composting, or hazardous-waste disposal method rule was located in Rules 0100-15/16. The operative requirements are “render unusable” + document (date/manner/batch/volume, 2 years) + compliance with any Commission hold/destruction order. Do not import other states' marijuana waste rules (e.g. rendering unusable by mixing with other waste at a ratio) — no such ratio rule was found in Tennessee text.

11.6 Environmental rules: water, pesticide limits, energy

(a) Hemp growers

Water: NO hemp-specific irrigation, water-use permit, runoff, or wastewater rule was located in Rule 0080-06-28, the Hemp Plan sections read, or 7 CFR Part 990 sections read. State explicitly as none located; general Tennessee water law was not researched for this Part. Pesticides — field use (no Tennessee-specific list or limit verified): EPA position (official EPA presentation, 2019): there are currently no pesticides registered by EPA specifically for use on cannabis; some pesticide labels do list industrial hemp among listed crops; no tolerances established for marijuana or hemp. Practical consequence under FIFRA label law: a grower may only use a product whose label includes hemp/cannabis as a listed crop/site — but this is a general FIFRA principle, and no Tennessee-specific approved-pesticide list or field-use limit was verified. A University of Tennessee master's thesis (Cosner, 2021, via UT TRACE) states “few synthetic chemical pesticides are labeled for use on hemp” — academic secondary support for the same point, not a regulation. Do NOT use the secondary site tennesseecannabis.org's list (Regalia, Amplitude/Stargus, Exile, Defguard as “approved for use on industrial hemp in Tennessee”) — undated, unverified against EPA labels/TDA. Do NOT use StateRegsToday's claim of a TDA 0.01 ppm pesticide limit for cannabis products — that site's Tennessee claims are inconsistent with Run 1 (it also asserts a medical-grower regime and a §39-17-423 marijuana packaging rule that do not exist in Tennessee) and must be treated as unreliable. The operative, verified pesticide numbers in Tennessee law are the finished-product residual limits in Rule 0100-15-.06(2)(a)4 (§11.1(b)), not field-application limits. Energy: NO hemp energy, lighting, HVAC, or carbon rule was located for growers, indoor or outdoor. State explicitly.

(b) HDCP businesses

Finished-product pesticide and solvent limits are in §11.1(b) (Rule 0100-15-.06(2), opened). Other finished-product environmental-adjacent limits: heavy metals (As <0.2, Cd <0.2, Pb <0.5, Hg <0.1 ppm); mycotoxins (total aflatoxin <20 µg/kg, ochratoxin A <20 µg/kg); microbial counts (TYMC <10,000 cfu/g, TAMC <100,000 cfu/g; pathogens ND in 1 g). Water and energy for HDCP businesses: NO HDCP-specific water-use, wastewater, or energy rule was located in Rules 0100-15/16. The only environmental hook in either chapter is the violation provision: “Violation of any workplace safety or environmental protection standard enforced by state or federal authorities is grounds for denial of program inspection and denial or revocation of any license issued by the Commission” (Rule 0100-15-.11(3); Rule 0100-16-.07(3)) — i.e. Tennessee incorporates other authorities' standards by reference as a licence ground; it does not set its own HDCP water/energy numbers. General food-manufacturing, wastewater, and building standards were not researched for this Part.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts and promotions

(a) Hemp growers

Compliance sampling is §11.1(a)/§11.3 (pre-harvest, department/designated sampler, no harvest before sampling). It is not a consumer-sampling programme. Consumer samples / giveaways by growers: NO grower-specific sample/giveaway rule was located. A grower who gives away finished HDCPs would be acting as a supplier/retailer and would need the matching TABC licence and be subject to the HDCP rules in 11.7(b). This is an inference from licence scope (TABC FAQ Q3/Q11: processing or selling HDCPs directly to consumers requires a TABC licence), labelled as such — not a separate grower-sample statute. Discounts/promotions by growers: NO rule located (growers sell raw hemp / agricultural product, not retail HDCPs; no HDCP discount/promotion rule exists to transfer — see 11.7(b)).

(b) HDCP businesses

Public samples — offence (former Title 43 text; current Title 57 equivalent Verify on Lexis). Former §43-27-203(c) (2024 Justia reproduction; 2023 amendment text on capitol.tn.gov): “It is an offense to knowingly distribute samples of products containing a hemp-derived cannabinoid in or on a public street, sidewalk, or park.” Former §43-27-203(d): “A violation of this section is a Class A misdemeanor.” Run 1 mapped this to current §57-7-103(b)(6); the current §57-7-103(b)(6) wording was not verified on Lexis in that session — Verify before quoting as current law. The former section also contains the full age regime: offence to knowingly sell/distribute without first obtaining proof of age; to sell/distribute to a person under 21 or purchase on behalf of a person under 21; to assist a person under 21 to purchase/acquire/receive; for a person under 21 to knowingly purchase/possess/accept receipt or present false proof of age — with a law-enforcement-use exception (minor with parent/guardian consent; person under 21 who is not a minor with consent).

In-store sampling: NO permission, quantity limit, or procedure was found in Rule 0100-16-.04 (manner of sale) or elsewhere in 0100-15/16 as read. The word “sample” in 0100-16 occurs only in the definitions (Commission testing samples) and in Rule .06 (Commission inspection/testing sampling). Tension to flag, not resolve: Run 1 notes the §57-7-102(7) “Retailer” definition includes providing samples with or without fee, but no in-store sampling permission is stated in Chapter 7 as enacted or in the rules as read. Do not assert that in-store sampling is permitted (e.g. 21+-only, face-to-face, or quantity-limited) without a TABC guidance source. All HDCP sales remain face-to-face at a licensed location, 21+ proof of age, behind the counter/barrier (§11.4(b)), and TABC retail licensees may not deliver/ship to consumers (FAQ Q5).

Discounts and promotions: NO HDCP-specific discount, buy-one-get-one, loyalty, coupon, happy-hour, or price-promotion ban or permission was located in Title 57 sections read or in Rules 0100-15/16 as read (text find for “discount” in 0100-16: no matches). State this explicitly; do not import alcohol happy-hour/discount rules.

Advertising/promotion bans that DO exist: Rule 0100-16-.07(1)(i) (retail): “Refrain from advertising products that are otherwise unlawful for sale under Tennessee law.” Rule 0100-15-.07(1)(h)–(m) (labels/packaging): no false, deceptive, or misleading statements or promotion of over-consumption; no depiction of a child or person under legal age consuming cannabis products; no seal/flag/crest/coat of arms/insignia reasonably misleading a person to believe endorsement/manufacture/use by any state, country, county, municipality, or agency; no packaging resembling/mimicking trademarked/characteristic/product-specialized packaging of any commercially available food, baked good, snack, lollipop, chewing gum, candy, or beverage; no statement/artwork/design reasonably misleading a person to believe the package contains anything other than an HDCP; no false/misleading/unsupported health claims — permitted health-related information must include: “This product has not been evaluated by the Food and Drug Administration and is not intended to diagnose, treat, cure, or prevent any disease,” unless FDA-evaluated and lawfully claimable under federal law. Rule .07(2) warnings (statutory minimum 6-point under 2026 Pub. Ch. 698; the 11-point figure in Rule 0100-15-.07 as read must be reconciled — statute controls on conflict, Verify rule text): all HDCPs — “Warning: Keep out of reach of children. Must be 21 or older to possess or consume. May be harmful to those who are pregnant or breastfeeding. May impair ability to drive or operate machinery. This product is not approved by FDA for cure, mitigation, treatment, or prevention of any disease.” (“Warning” in bold capitals.) Inhalables additionally: “Warning: Inhalation of cannabis smoke has been associated with lung injury.” No HDCP may be manufactured/distributed labelled as a dietary supplement (Rule .07(2)(b)3). Statute §57-7-110 (Run 1 baseline, Verify on Lexis): child-appeal ban (superheroes, comic/video/TV/movie characters, unicorns/mythical creatures, etc.); health-claims ban; animal/cartoon shape ban (§57-7-110(f)(3)). Secondary Hemp Law Group note: TABC has published a printable HDCP warning sign retailers can download/post — secondary pointer, Verify on tn.gov (the rule's sign dimensions are in Rule .04(4), §11.4(b)).

11.8 Drug testing and background check rules for licence holders and staff

Keep the two senses of “drug testing” separate: product lab testing is §11.1; this item is testing of people.

(a) Hemp growers

  • Background checks: Rule 0080-06-28-.03(2)(f) — criminal history report for key participants “in the form of the Federal Bureau of Investigation's Identity History Summary.” “Key participant” = sole proprietor, partner, or person with executive managerial control such as CEO, COO, or CFO (Rule .02(j)).
  • Ineligibility: Rule .03(13) — “A person convicted of any drug-related felony offense in any state or federal jurisdiction within the previous ten (10) years shall not participate in the program or grow hemp for ten (10) years from the date of the conviction.” Exception: a person lawfully growing hemp under §7606 of the Agricultural Act of 2014 (7 U.S.C. 5940) before 20 Dec 2018 whose conviction also occurred before that date. Statute §43-27-102(b)(4) (Hemp Plan reproduction) states the same 10-year bar as a licence condition.
  • Employee drug testing for growers: NO rule was located requiring drug testing of farm workers, key participants, or any grower staff. Do not assert one.
  • Grower staff background checks beyond key participants: NONE LOCATED in the rule — the FBI Identity History Summary duty is key-participant-only as written.

(b) HDCP businesses

  • Background checks — rule vs FAQ discrepancy (flag, do not merge): Rule 0100-15-.03(3)(e) and Rule 0100-16-.03(3)(e) require a “TBI or FBI criminal background check including fingerprint checks for any person legally responsible for management of applicant's operations.” The applications also require legal name/DBA, physical/mailing address, name/biographic/contact of “any person in the business,” and a valid government photo ID for “any person in [the] applicant['s business].” TABC FAQ Q18 (opened) is broader: “A copy of a background check from TBI or the FBI for any individual owner, member-owner, or shareholder” plus biographic/contact information (DOB, SSN, mailing address, email, telephone) for “any individual with an ownership interest.” Report both wordings with their sources; the rule's management-responsible-person test and the FAQ's owner/shareholder test are not identical — Verify against the TABC applicant guides which population must actually submit checks.
  • Renewal checks: updated criminal background check for each applicable individual at renewal (Rule 0100-15-.03(9); Rule 0100-16-.03(7)).
  • Ineligibility (official FAQ Q19, opened): “Anyone currently serving a sentence for a felony drug offense or those that are applying within ten (10) years from the date of conviction of a felony drug offense are ineligible subject to analysis under the Fresh Start Act, Tenn. Code Ann. § 62-76-104.” This matches the Run 1 baseline (10-year drug-felony ineligibility, TBI/FBI checks).
  • Transitional denial on background/compliance history: see §11.2(b) (1-year supplier/wholesaler, 2-year retail, prior Title 43 violations, with evidentiary-hearing protection for unadjudicated conduct).
  • Age of employees: see the under-18 / under-21 conflict flagged in §11.0 (item 8). At minimum, §57-7-103(b)(7) (Run 1) prohibits knowingly employing a person under 18 for physical manufacture, storage, sale or distribution of HDCPs or permitting such a person on premises to do so.
  • Employee drug testing for HDCP businesses: NO drug-test mandate for licence holders or staff was located in Rules 0100-15/16 or in §§57-7-103/106/111 as read. What exists is the opposite direction: §57-7-111 (Run 1) preserves employer drug-free workplace programmes and rights, imposes no accommodation duty for HDCP use, creates no drug-test carve-out, and creates no cause of action for wrongful discharge/discrimination based on HDCP use. §50-1-304(d) (Justia 2024 reproduction, opened via search): “No employee shall be discharged or terminated solely for participating or engaging in the use of an agricultural product not regulated by the alcoholic beverage commission that is not otherwise proscribed by law” (with working-hours policy compliance in (d)(1) and off-duty protection in (d)(2)) — by its own terms this does not cover ABC-regulated HDCPs (see §11.0, item 7).
  • Agent/employee attribution: a licensee is responsible for violations committed by the licensee, their agent, or their employee (Rule 0100-15-.11(4); Rule 0100-16-.07(4); Rule 0080-06-28-.07(7) for growers: “A person is responsible for violations … when committed by either the person or his agent”).

11.9 Research and university cannabis/hemp work

No Tennessee research licence type, no state marijuana research programme, and no state-licensed medical marijuana programme exist to report (Run 1 baseline, unchanged). University work located is hemp (agronomy, processing, pest/disease, cannabinoid chemistry) and, secondarily, cannabinoid pharmacology/drug development — not a state marijuana cultivation programme. Federal Schedule III split scheduling (user baseline) does not create a Tennessee university marijuana programme.

(a) The DEA-certified research-oil track — §39-17-402(16)(E)

Verbatim (Justia 2025 reproduction, Run 1 — Verify on Lexis): “'Marijuana' does not include cannabis oil containing the substance cannabidiol, with less than six-tenths of one percent (0.6%) of tetrahydrocannabinol, including the necessary seeds and plants, when manufactured, processed, transferred, dispensed, or possessed by a four-year public or private institution of higher education certified by the drug enforcement administration located in the state as part of a clinical research study on the treatment of intractable seizures, cancer, or other diseases.” This is a definitional exclusion, not a general university cannabis licence. No list of DEA-certified Tennessee institutions under this track was located — Unavailable/Verify. Do not name any institution as DEA-certified under (E) without a DEA or institutional source. No institution is named as such in this report. Do not confuse the 0.6% research track with the 0.9% low-THC oil possession exclusion (§39-17-402(16)(F), Run 1) — the 2021 “raised 0.6% to 0.9%” press shorthand was already corrected in Run 1; the two tracks are separate.

(b) University of Tennessee (UT) — Institute of Agriculture

  • Licence rule: Rule 0080-06-28-.03(10) — annual hemp licence fee waived for any institute of higher education offering agricultural-science programmes seeking licensure for a growing area on university property. This is the only university-specific hemp licence rule located.
  • Center for Agricultural Hemp (UTCAH) — secondary only: StateRegsToday synthesis (secondary, undated in excerpt) states UT's Institute of Agriculture has a Center for Agricultural Hemp dedicated to research on production, processing, and utilization of industrial hemp, offering educational programmes and hosting an annual hemp field day. The UT hemp FAQ page (hemp.tennessee.edu/faqs) as previously searched states UT hosts research scientists interested in refinement of the hemp value chain and collaboration from contracts to producer/processor information to competitive grant projects, with a published AgResearch contact for identifying researchers — but the search-index date on that FAQ text was stale (2,223 days before the prior search), so its currency is Verify; do not present the contact or programme description as current without re-opening the page.
  • Primary UT research outputs located (university / federal project pages): USDA NIFA: “Biopesticide Potential and Efficacy in Controlling Pathogens and Pests of Hemp” (UT-led) — identify plant extracts with biopesticidal activity against fungal, bacterial, nematode pathogens suitable/safe for hemp food/natural medicines in the Southeast; develop Extension materials; teach undergraduate/graduate students: NIFA 1024067. USDA NIFA multistate: “Industrial Hemp Production, Processing, and Marketing in the U.S.” (UT-led, with University of Kentucky and Virginia Tech) — identify/improve/adapt varieties for fiber, grain, or dual-purpose: NIFA 1017856. UT Institute of Agriculture 2019 Hemp Variety Trials (UTIA publication, primary): trials at Greeneville, Springfield, and Jackson, Tennessee; 29/19/16 treatments; varieties including Cherry and T1 (topped and not topped); fertility at Greeneville/Springfield 240 lb N, 96 lb P, 312 lb K per acre preplant; at Jackson 196 lb N, 148 lb P, 112 lb K; irrigated at transplanting, not after; weeds by cultivation: UTIA W900. UT theses (TRACE, university repository): corn earworm (Helicoverpa zea) feeding behaviour / varietal and fertilization effects on hemp damage (Cosner, 2021, noting few synthetic pesticides labelled for hemp); hemp pest phenology / tritrophic interactions (2023 dissertation); insect guilds on industrial hemp in Tennessee 2017–2018 (Crossville trials; lepidopteran larvae most damaging; corn earworm chosen for feeding trials). UT Research Foundation technology page (secondary-university commercialisation page): terpene nanoemulsions from CBD-production byproducts evaluated as bio-insecticide / natural antifungal, with near-100% reduction of hemp powdery mildew symptoms in testing described — technology-transfer summary, not a peer-reviewed efficacy finding; label as such.

(c) Tennessee State University (TSU) — College of Agriculture, Nashville

  • TSU dissertation (university repository): Anand Kumar, Ph.D. dissertation awarded 11 Dec 2025, “Agronomic Management Practices for CBD and Fiber Hemp in Southeastern [U.S.]”: TSU dissertation 327.
  • MDPI Agronomy article, published 7 Aug 2026, “Factors Influencing Biomass, Cannabinoid Profiles, and Regulatory Compliance in Floral Hemp”: two-year field study at TSU Agricultural Research and Education Center (TSU AREC), Nashville, Tennessee — Non-Chemical vs Chemical System at 100 kg ha⁻¹ nitrogen; four feminized floral cultivars (Fukuoka, Rushmore, Cherry, Virginia); plastic vs straw mulch; seed vs clone propagation; biomass, CBD, and THC compliance at the 0.3% threshold; plus a three-year fiber trial (Futura-83, Fibor-79, Muka-76; 15–20 t ha⁻¹ biomass; optimal planting window 18 May–10 June per dissertation abstract): MDPI Agronomy 16(16) 1514.
  • TSU Small Farm Expo, 3 Sept 2026, Nashville, included an Industrial Hemp Field Tour (Run 1, official TSU page: tnstate.edu/smallfarm).
  • TSU Newsroom article (university source, date not stated in excerpt — Verify date before citing as current): TSU partnering with Eufloria Medical of Tennessee, Inc. (subsidiary of Acacia Diversified Holdings), manufacturing material for university study; Dr. Ying Wu (associate professor, Food and Animal Science) investigating phytochemical profiles in hemp seeds, oils, extracts and related health benefits, aiming to develop a safe/chemical-free vehicle for whole-hemp-plant health benefits into food, beverages, topical creams; TSU College of Agriculture team developing hemp production practices for Tennessee, hemp nutritional products for human consumption, studying economic viability, growing/evaluating 10 varieties at time of article: TSU Newsroom.
  • TSU is also covered by the Rule .03(10) university fee waiver (above) as an institute of higher education with agricultural-science programmes, for growing areas on university property.

(d) Middle Tennessee State University (MTSU)

  • Primary (MTSU official pages, opened via search): Tennessee Center for Botanical Medicine Research (TCBMR) — “endorsed by Governor Bill Haslam and approved by the Tennessee Board of Regents on June 24, 2011. The primary goal of the TCBMR is to develop new drugs (pharmaceuticals) or nutritional supplements (nutraceuticals) from botanicals that have medicinal properties with an emphasis on plants that are native to Tennessee, such as ginseng and hemp.” Biologists screen plant extracts/compounds for antibacterial, anticancer, antifungal, antiprotozoal, antiviral and immunomodulatory activities; chemists purify active ingredients, determine structure, and develop synthesis/derivatization approaches; funded by federal and private grants: MTSU TCBMR.
  • MTSU TCBMR Biological Activity and Potency Testing page (official): protocols using human cell lines and mouse models for bioactivity/potency of supplements and pure compounds; testing “to determine the concentrations of specific ingredients that can be extracted from different botanicals, such as the levels of ginsenosides and complex polysaccharides in ginseng and cannabinoids and terpenes in hemp”: TCBMR Biological Activity.
  • MTSU theses (JEWL Scholar, university repository): elicitor stimulation of cannabinoid production in hydroponic hemp (varieties Canda, Cherry, Cherry Blossom; salicylic acid / methyl jasmonate; methyl jasmonate leaf treatment + Cherry produced greatest cannabinoid production, increased CBDA/CBC without increasing THC): JEWL 3be31e5a; cloning successive generations of hemp to assess cannabinoid profiles (Cherry, Cherry Blossom, Cherry x Workhorse, Sour Space Candy, The Wife; no significant difference across successive cloned generations): JEWL 1cae1e64; plus somatic embryogenesis in hemp tissue callus (Cherry, Cherry Blossom) and polysaccharides isolated from Cherry hemp root (ethanol/water extraction, anion-exchange + size-exclusion purification, PMP-HPLC / TMS-GC-MS).
  • MTSU School of Agribusiness and Agriscience grows hemp at the MTSU farm — Experiential Learning and Research Center, Lascassas, Tennessee — including greenhouse/vivarium and hemp grown with ultraviolet light (Newswise article, secondary; lawmaker tour, co-director Iris Gao, Chinese collaborations, student-involved anticancer-compound research, patents/papers — secondary, Verify against MTSU sources for current status; funding figures in that article ($147M Science Building; $10M in 2012 to $19.5M in 2017) are centre/college context, not hemp-only funding).
  • “Cannabis Science Research Group” (MTSU College of Basic and Applied Sciences) — secondary only. It appears only in the StateRegsToday synthesis (chemical composition, pharmacology, therapeutic potential, social implications). It was not confirmed on an MTSU primary page in that session — Verify before presenting as a current named MTSU group. The confirmed MTSU entity is TCBMR.

(e) Other Tennessee institutions — secondary only, do not present as verified

Vanderbilt University School of Medicine Cannabis Research Development Program (medical benefits/risks, cellular effects of cannabinoids); East Tennessee State University Department of Pharmaceutical Sciences laboratory on novel cannabinoid drug development (cancer, multiple sclerosis, epilepsy, pain); Welch College course “Introduction to Medical Marijuana” (healthcare administration programme) — all from the same StateRegsToday secondary synthesis, none verified against a primary university source in that session. Flag as secondary/Verify; do not expand into programme descriptions.

11.10 Federal-date interaction (for Part 11 context, secondary + baseline)

Secondary Hemp Law Group position (last reviewed 5 Oct 2026): the Tennessee programme does not end on the federal dates; Tennessee wrote its own hemp definition and Pub. Ch. 526 sets its own total-THC standard and “at present does not adopt the federal one,” so a TABC licence and brand registration stay put under Tennessee law; a product can be compliant under Tennessee law and non-compliant under federal law at the same time; watch bank/payment-processor, federal tax, interstate-shipment, and raw-material paperwork consequences. Secondary only — the federal dates themselves are the user baseline (PL 119-37 §781 eff. 12 Nov 2026, limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 Dec 2026; after 11 Dec 2026 the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply). No Tennessee statute or rule responding to those federal dates was located in Rules 0100-15/16 as read — Unavailable as a Tennessee response; do not state that Tennessee has conformed to the federal cap. Note for growers: a Tennessee-compliant hemp crop (≤0.3% total THC, TDA/USDA plan) and a Tennessee-compliant HDCP (≤0.3% total THC, caps in Run 1 baseline) are both measured on total THC (THCa × 0.877 + Δ9-THC), but the federal post-11 Dec 2026 per-container cap (0.4 mg) is a different metric from Tennessee's per-serving/per-package caps and has no Tennessee rule counterpart located.

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

Scope and method. This part covers only Part 12, in the task's eight subtopics, in order. All statute quotations below were read from Justia 2025 Tennessee Code reproductions or, for the 2025 expungement reorganisation, from the chaptered text of 2025 Public Chapter 268 (SB 1055, substituted for HB 1257) as republished by Legiscan. Standing flag for the whole part: every Justia/Legiscan quotation must be verified against the official Lexis text (advance.lexis.com) before publication, per the Run 1 limitation — Tennessee's official Code publisher is Lexis, which could not be opened directly. No contradiction of the Run 1 baseline was confirmed; three conflicts flagged in the prior research turn are now resolved (see §§12.3, 12.4, 12.6). Sources accessed 2026-10-25 unless otherwise stated. Secondary sources are labelled as such and are never cited as law. Attorney General opinions are labelled as opinions, not law. No 2026 bill is presented as current law.

Consistency baseline applied (Run 1, unchanged): no state-licensed marijuana programme; marijuana is Schedule VI under Tennessee law and Schedule I federally for Tennessee purposes (no Tennessee Schedule III licensee); low-THC oil is only a possession exclusion under §39-17-402(16)(F) (<0.9% THC, no registry, no card, no dispensary); HDCPs are governed by Title 57, Chapter 7 (2025 Public Chapter 526); simple possession/casual exchange §39-17-418 (Class A misdemeanour); manufacture/delivery/sale/possession with intent §39-17-417 (felonies graded by weight/count); paraphernalia §39-17-425.

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke/vape shop rules

Drug paraphernalia — offences (opened, Justia 2025)

§39-17-425, Unlawful drug paraphernalia uses and activities — Justia §39-17-425 (accessed 2026-10-25; history Acts 1989, ch. 591, §1; 2012, ch. 848, §19): (a)(1): “Except when used or possessed with the intent to use by a person authorized by this part and title 53, chapter 11, parts 3 and 4 to dispense, prescribe, manufacture or possess a controlled substance, it is unlawful for any person to use, or to possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, conceal, inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body a controlled substance or controlled substance analogue in violation of this part.” (a)(2): violation is a Class A misdemeanour. (b)(1): delivery, possession with intent to deliver, or manufacture with intent to deliver drug paraphernalia, “knowing, or under circumstances where one reasonably should know, that it will be used” for the same purposes, is unlawful (same authorised-person exception). (b)(2): Class E felony. (b)(3): a person 18+ who delivers paraphernalia to a person under 18 who is at least 3 years younger commits a Class E felony. (c)(1)–(2): placing an advertisement in any newspaper, magazine, handbill, or other publication, knowing or reasonably should know its purpose is to promote sale of objects designed/intended for use as drug paraphernalia, is unlawful; Class A misdemeanour.

§39-17-424, Determination whether object is drug paraphernalia — Justia §39-17-424 (accessed 2026-10-25; Acts 1989, ch. 591, §1; 2012, ch. 848, §18) — this section was not opened in Run 1 and is now opened, closing that gap: “In determining whether a particular object is drug paraphernalia as defined by § 39-17-402, the court or other authority making that determination shall, in addition to all other logically relevant factors, consider the following: (1) Statements by the owner or anyone in control of the object concerning its use; (2) Prior convictions, if any, of the owner or of anyone in control of the object for violation of any state or federal law relating to controlled substances or controlled substance analogues; (3) The existence of any residue of controlled substances or controlled substance analogues on the object; (4) Instructions, oral or written, provided with the object concerning its use; (5) Descriptive materials accompanying the object that explain or depict its use; (6) The manner in which the object is displayed for sale; (7) The existence and scope of legitimate uses for the object in the community; and (8) Expert testimony concerning its use.”

Carried from Run 1, not re-opened this turn [Verify on re-check]: “drug paraphernalia” under §39-17-402(12) expressly includes objects for ingesting/inhaling marijuana, concentrates, and oil, including metal, acrylic, glass, stone, or plastic pipes, water pipes, and bongs under §39-17-402(12)(C); mandatory minimum fines for paraphernalia possession are 1st $150, 2nd+ $250 under §39-17-428(b)(7)–(8).

Smoke/vape shop rules — no Tennessee smoke-shop licence; tobacco/vapor/smoking-hemp regime (Title 39, Chapter 17, Part 15)

No Tennessee state licence or registration specific to “smoke shops” was located. The Run 1 finding stands: Tennessee does not currently require or offer a state licence/permit to sell tobacco products as such (TDA tobacco-enforcement FAQ, cited in Run 1, not re-opened this turn). That is distinct from HDCP retail, which requires a TABC retailer licence under Title 57, Chapter 7 (Run 1) — a shop selling HDCPs is therefore licensed as an HDCP retailer, not as a “smoke shop,” and a shop selling only tobacco/vapor/smoking hemp is governed by Part 15 below. No Tennessee smoke-shop spacing, zoning, or density rule was located; do not import other states' rules (the Cleveland, Ohio package remains excluded).

Definitions, §39-17-1503 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §39-17-1503: “Smoking hemp” (15): “hemp that is offered for sale to the public with the intention that it is consumed by smoking and that does not meet the definition of a vapor product.” “Vapor product” (17): “any noncombustible product containing nicotine or any other substance that employs a mechanical heating element, battery, electronic circuit, or other mechanism… that can be used to produce or emit a visible or non-visible vapor,” including e-cigarettes, e-cigars, e-pipes, cartridges and refill substances; excluding products regulated under Chapter V of the FD&C Act. “Proof of age” (8): “a driver license or other generally accepted means of identification that describes the individual as twenty-one (21) years of age or older, contains a photograph or other likeness of the individual, and appears on its face to be valid.” “Retail tobacco store” (10) and “Retail vapor product store” (11) are defined by largest sales category (tobacco products/accessories; vapor products/accessories respectively) — these are definitions, not licences. “Age-restricted venue” (1): a legal establishment that affirmatively restricts access at all times to persons 21+ by requiring ID inspection on entry.

Sales to under-21s, §39-17-1504 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §39-17-1504 (history ends 2025, ch. 118, §1; 2025, ch. 324, §1): (a): “It is unlawful for any person to sell or distribute any tobacco, smoking hemp, vapor product, or smokeless nicotine product to another person who has not attained twenty-one (21) years of age or to purchase [one] on behalf of such person under twenty-one (21) years of age.” (b): unlawful to persuade, entice, send or assist a person under 21 to purchase, acquire, receive or attempt to purchase/acquire/receive such a product (law-enforcement compliance checks with parental consent for minors, or consent for under-21 non-minors, are preserved). (c): no distribution of samples in or on any public street, sidewalk, or park. (d): a seller/employee “shall not make or permit to be made any sales… to a person under twenty-one (21) years of age. Prior to making a sale…, a consumer whose physical appearance does not reasonably demonstrate an age of fifty (50) years or older must present… a valid, government-issued document, such as a driver license or other form of identification… that includes the photograph and birth date of the consumer.” Mail distribution requires an affirmative statement of 21+ age and notice of the prohibition.

Under-21 purchase/possession, §39-17-1505 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §39-17-1505: (a): unlawful for a person under 21 to possess, purchase, or accept receipt of a tobacco, smoking hemp, vapor product, or smokeless nicotine product, or to present false/fraudulent/not-own proof of age to purchase/receive one. (b): product seized as contraband whether or not a citation is issued. (c): violation is a civil offence; general sessions or juvenile court may impose a civil penalty of $10–$50 (charged against the person if 18–20, or against a parent/guardian/custodian if a minor), and for a second or subsequent violation within one year may also impose community service up to 50 hours or a prescribed court programme. (d): compliance-check cooperators are not sanctioned. (f)(1): handling/transport in the course of employment is not unlawful for a person under 21 if supervised by an employee 21+; (f)(2) and (g) preserve agricultural-fair projects and agricultural production/storage/transport handling. Contrast with HDCPs (important, do not conflate): under-21 HDCP purchase/possession/false ID is a Class A misdemeanour under §57-7-103(c) (Run 1), while under-21 tobacco/vapor/smoking-hemp possession is a civil offence ($10–$50) under §39-17-1505(c). Conversely, HDCP law prohibits employing a person under 18 for physical manufacture, storage, sale, or distribution of HDCPs (§57-7-103(b)(7), Run 1), while §39-17-1505(f)(1) permits supervised under-21 handling of tobacco/vapor/smoking hemp at work.

Vending machines, §39-17-1507 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §39-17-1507 (history ends 2025, ch. 118, §2; 2025, ch. 526, §6): (a): “It is unlawful for a person to sell smoking hemp and hemp derived cannabinoid products through a vending machine.” Tobacco, vapor, or smokeless nicotine products may be sold through a vending machine only (1) in non-public areas of factories/businesses/offices; (2) in public places to which under-21s are denied access; (3) where alcohol is sold for on-premises consumption, under continuous supervision and inaccessible when closed; or (4) elsewhere, under continuous supervision or token operation, and inaccessible when closed. (b): where supervision/token operation is required, proof of age must be demanded if the purchaser appears possibly under 50.

Signage, §39-17-1506 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §39-17-1506: every retail seller of tobacco, smoking hemp, vapor, or smokeless nicotine products must conspicuously post at each point of sale a sign at least 93½ square inches stating: “STATE LAW STRICTLY PROHIBITS THE SALE OF TOBACCO PRODUCTS, VAPOR PRODUCTS, SMOKELESS NICOTINE PRODUCTS, OR SMOKING PARAPHERNALIA TO PERSONS UNDER THE AGE OF TWENTY-ONE (21) YEARS PROOF OF AGE MAY BE REQUIRED.” (b): unless federal law requires another notice, this notice and the §39-15-411 notice are the only notices required to be posted in such a store.

Smoking paraphernalia display, §39-17-1511 — confirmed via the Tennessee Department of Agriculture's compilation of §§39-17-1501–1511 (TDA PDF, 8 Apr 2024, TDA compilation) and Justia 2021 text (Justia 2021 §39-17-1511); 2025 text not re-opened — [Verify]: “smoking paraphernalia” means a cigarette holder; a smoking pipe of metal, wood, acrylic, glass, stone, or plastic (with/without screens, permanent screens, hashish heads, or punctured metal bowls); a water pipe; rose and pen combinations; or vapor products — excluding a pipe/device primarily made of briar, meerschaum, clay, or corn cob when sold at retail. “All smoking paraphernalia shall be maintained behind the counter of a retail establishment in an area inaccessible to a customer or in a locked display case that makes the products unavailable to a customer without the assistance of an employee.” Violation is punishable as provided in §39-17-1509 (civil-penalty enforcement by the Commissioner of Agriculture, including random unannounced inspections; proof-of-age reliance is a defence to civil penalty under §39-17-1509(c) as reproduced in the TDA compilation). The TDA retailer quick guide (TDA Tobacco Retailer Quick Guide T21) confirms the same placement rule in enforcement guidance: self-service displays found by inspectors draw fines. This display rule is separate from the HDCP barrier rule in §57-7-104(d) (Run 1) and must not be conflated with it.

No rule located for: a state smoke-shop licence, smoke-shop spacing/buffer from schools, or a local smoke/vape-shop ordinance regime beyond Run 1 Part 3 (no HDCP-specific local ordinance in the jurisdictions checked). Where Tennessee has no rule, that is stated here, not filled in.

12.2 Minimum age and ID rules for hemp-derived THC products — cross-reference only (Run 1 unchanged)

Per the task instruction this section does not change Run 1; it cross-references it: HDCP sale/distribution without first obtaining proof of age, sale/distribution to a person under 21, purchase on behalf of a person under 21, assisting a person under 21 to purchase/acquire/receive or attempt to purchase, and purchase/possession/acceptance by a person under 21 or presenting false/fraudulent/not-own proof of age, are offences under §57-7-103(b), all Class A misdemeanours under §57-7-103(c), subject to the compliance-check consent preservation in §57-7-103(b)(5). “Proof of age” under §57-7-102(6) means a valid driver licence or other government-issued identification card containing a photograph and confirming age 21 or older. Employment of a person under 18 for physical manufacture, storage, sale, or distribution of HDCPs, or permitting such a person on premises to do so, is a separate offence under §57-7-103(b)(7). Retailer-to-consumer sales must be face-to-face at a licensed retail location; delivery and shipment to consumers are prohibited under §§57-7-106(b)(4)–(5) and 57-7-113, with civil penalties $1,000 / $5,000 / $10,000 (Run 1). A person under 21 who lawfully possesses low-THC oil under §39-17-402(16)(F) is in a different category from a person under 21 purchasing/possessing an HDCP under §57-7-103(b)(4); do not conflate the two. Interaction with §12.1 above: smoking hemp sold as a tobacco/vapor product is age-21+ under §39-17-1504, but an HDCP (a product meeting the §57-7-102 definition, e.g. containing a regulated cannabinoid above 0.1%) is governed by Title 57, Chapter 7's licensing, venue (§57-7-104(b) exhaustive list), barrier (§57-7-104(d)), and testing/labelling rules — the Part 15 age rule alone does not authorise HDCP sale.

12.3 Impaired driving law and testing — impairment-based; NO per se THC limit

The DUI offence — §55-10-401 (opened, Justia 2025)

Justia §55-10-401 (history ends Acts 2015, ch. 126, §1): “It is unlawful for any person to drive or to be in physical control of any automobile or other motor driven vehicle on any of the public roads and highways of the state, or on any streets or alleys, or while on the premises of any shopping center, trailer park, or apartment house complex, or any other premises that is generally frequented by the public at large, while: (1) Under the influence of any intoxicant, marijuana, controlled substance, controlled substance analogue, drug, substance affecting the central nervous system, or combination thereof that impairs the driver's ability to safely operate a motor vehicle by depriving the driver of the clearness of mind and control of oneself that the driver would otherwise possess; (2) The alcohol concentration in the person's blood or breath is eight-hundredths of one percent (0.08%) or more; or (3) With a blood alcohol concentration of four-hundredths of one percent (0.04%) or more and the vehicle is a commercial motor vehicle as defined in §55-50-102.”

State clearly: Tennessee DUI for drugs is impairment-based. There is no per se THC limit, no THC blood/urine concentration threshold, and no THC-metabolite rule in §55-10-401 as opened, and no statute was located treating mere presence of THC or metabolites, without impairment, as DUI under (a)(1). The 0.08% and 0.04% figures are alcohol figures only. The drug prong turns on impairment as defined in (a)(1), not on presence alone. §57-7-111(a)(2) confirms the same for HDCPs (see §12.5): Chapter 7 “does not permit a person to… [o]perate, navigate, or be in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft, motorized watercraft, or any other vehicle while under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP,” and §57-7-111(c) provides Chapter 7 “does not exempt a person from prosecution for a criminal offense related to impairment or intoxication resulting from use of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP or relieve a person from any requirement under law to submit to a breath, blood, urine, or other test.” Historical proposal — NOT law: an old bill text, SB0451 (107th General Assembly, capitol.tn.gov PDF), proposing an “any amount of Schedule I” DUI prong and an “impaired to the slightest degree” standard, appeared in search results. Its effective-date line is 1 July 2011. It is a historical proposal and must not be quoted as §55-10-401.

Testing and implied consent — §55-10-406 (opened, Justia 2025)

Justia §55-10-406 (history ends 2025, ch. 403, §§2–4): (a): an officer with probable cause to believe the operator is driving while under the influence of any intoxicant, controlled substance, controlled substance analogue, drug, substance affecting the central nervous system, or combination thereof as prohibited by §55-10-401, or committing vehicular assault (§39-13-106), aggravated vehicular assault (§39-13-115), vehicular homicide (§39-13-213(a)(2)), or aggravated vehicular homicide (§39-13-218), may request a test or tests for alcohol or drug content, or both, of the operator's blood. (b): breath tests may be administered on implied consent, consent, search warrant, incident to lawful arrest for a listed offence, or where required under (c)(1). Blood tests may be administered on implied consent, consent, search warrant, exigent circumstances, or where required under (c)(2) with a warrant or exigent circumstances. Oral fluid tests may be administered on consent, search warrant, incident to lawful arrest, or exigent circumstances — implied consent is NOT listed for oral fluid tests in (b)(3) as opened. (c): mandatory breath/blood testing where the operator was involved in a collision resulting in injury or death of another and committed a listed offence; committed a listed offence with a passenger who is a child under 18; or committed a listed offence and has a prior conviction of a listed offence (including an out-of-state offence that would constitute a listed Tennessee offence). Blood tests required under (c)(1)(B) must be performed under §55-10-408 and pursuant to a search warrant or exigent circumstances, regardless of consent. (d)(1): “The operator of a motor vehicle in this state is deemed to have given implied consent to breath tests, blood tests, or both tests, for the purpose of determining the alcohol or drug content of that operator's blood. However, no such tests may be administered pursuant to this section unless conducted at the direction of a law enforcement officer having probable cause to believe the operator was in violation of one (1) or more of the offenses set out in subsection (a) and the operator signs a standardized waiver developed by the department of safety and made available to law enforcement agencies.” (d)(2)–(3): before a test, the officer must advise the operator that refusal will result in suspension by the court of the operator's driver license, and may result, depending on prior criminal history, in a requirement to operate only a vehicle equipped with a functioning ignition interlock device if convicted under §55-10-401, as described in §55-10-405. If the operator is not so advised, the court lacks authority to suspend the license or require an ignition interlock device under §55-10-417 for a violation of (d). (d)(4) (opened this turn): “If the operator is placed under arrest, requested by a law enforcement officer to submit to breath tests, blood tests, or both tests, advised of the consequences for refusing to do so, and refuses to submit, the operator shall be charged with violating subdivision (d)(1). The operator's refusal is a violation of subdivision (d)(1), even if the operator's blood sample is obtained pursuant to a search warrant, court order, exigent circumstances, or other lawful means.” Determination is made at the same time and by the same court disposing of the arrest offence on the state's oral/written motion, or at first appearance/preliminary hearing in general sessions court, no later than bind-over to the grand jury. (e): qualified practitioners who withdraw blood at an officer's written request (physician, registered nurse, licensed practical nurse, clinical laboratory technician, licensed paramedic, licensed EMT approved to establish intravenous catheters, technologist, trained phlebotomist under hospital protocol/training, physician assistant) are protected from civil/criminal liability except for negligence; the sample must be withdrawn as soon as practicable without jeopardising life; qualified practitioners presented with a warrant/court order are acting in good faith, must not require additional consent, and “may use all reasonable force to obtain the sample of blood.” (f): an unconscious operator, or one otherwise incapable of refusal, “shall not be subjected to blood tests unless law enforcement has obtained a search warrant or an exigent circumstance exception[] to a search warrant appl[ies].” (g)–(k): hospital/medical-care blood analysis obtained in the ordinary course of treatment is not affected, but must be memorialised and produced on warrant/subpoena/court order, with residual sample provided as soon as practicable; blood/breath results authorised and conducted under §§55-10-406/408 “shall be reported in writing… shall have noted on the report the time at which the sample analyzed was obtained… and shall be made available to the operator, upon request; and shall be admissible in evidence at the trial of any person charged with an offense specified in subsection (a)”; oral fluid results conducted under the section are admissible at trial of a listed offence; an officer's failure to request a test is itself admissible evidence at trial.

Testing procedures — §55-10-408 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §55-10-408: (a): a blood sample is validly procured if collected by a §55-10-406(e)(2) qualified person or a person acting at the direction of a medical examiner or other physician holding an unlimited Tennessee medical licence under Department of Health procedures; limited testing of a §55-10-406 sample for alcohol/drug content “shall be considered a reasonable search for all evidentiary purposes and shall be allowed into evidence without further need of a search warrant or court order.” (b)(1): on receipt of the specimen and the “toxicology request for examination” form (stating whether a breath alcohol test was administered and its result), the TBI director or the director of an accredited crime laboratory “shall have the specimen examined for alcohol concentration, the presence of narcotics or other drugs, or for both alcohol and drugs, if requested by the arresting officer, county medical examiner, or any district attorney general,” with a certificate/report stating the accused's name, date, time, and by whom the specimen was received and examined, and the alcohol concentration or presence of drugs. (b)(2): “accredited crime laboratory” is limited to state/political-subdivision-owned-and-operated labs accredited under ISO/IEC 17025 with associated supplemental requirements, providing testing within accreditation scope sufficient to meet forensic-service-provider requirements. (d): the duly attested TBI/accredited-lab certificate is admissible in any criminal proceeding as evidence of the facts stated and test results, if the specimen-taker and tester are available as witnesses on subpoena on either party's demand, or submit depositions if unable to appear. (e): the person tested is entitled to have an additional blood or urine sample procured and tested by a medical laboratory of their own choosing at their own expense, provided the laboratory is licensed under Title 68, Chapter 29. No Tennessee Drug Recognition Expert (DRE) statute or oral-fluid programme guidance from the Department of Safety/TBI was opened for Part 12. Secondary summaries mention DRE testimony and blood testing as the standard for drug impairment because breath tests detect alcohol only — that remains secondary and is labelled as such. No per se THC testing threshold appears in §55-10-408 either.

Implied-consent penalties — CONFLICT RESOLVED: the penalty section is §55-10-407, not §55-10-417

The prior turn's conflict (secondary sources giving 18 months / 1 year / 2 years / 5 years) is resolved by opening the current text. §55-10-417 in the 2025 Code is ignition interlock devices, not implied-consent penalties (see §12.3, DUI penalties below). The penalty section is:

§55-10-407, Penalty for violations of §55-10-406 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §55-10-407 (history ends 2025, ch. 403, §1): (a): “If the court finds that the driver violated § 55-10-406, the driver is not considered as having committed a criminal offense; provided, however, that the court shall revoke the license of the driver for a period of:” (1)(A) one (1) year, if the person does not have a prior conviction as defined in (e); (1)(B) one (1) year and six (6) months, if the person (i) does not have a prior conviction as defined in (e), (ii) is suspected of having committed a misdemeanour violation of §55-10-401, and (iii) violated §55-10-406 by refusing to submit to a law enforcement officer's request for a blood test; (2) two (2) years, if the person does have a prior conviction as defined in (e); (3) two (2) years, if the court finds the driver, in a collision in which one or more persons suffered serious bodily injury, violated §55-10-406 by refusing a test; (4) five (5) years, if the court finds the driver, in a collision in which one or more persons were killed, violated §55-10-406 by refusing a test. (b): if a licence was suspended for a §55-10-406 violation before disposition of the arrest offence, the disposing court may order the Department of Safety to reinstate it if the arrest offence is dismissed on a finding that the officer lacked sufficient cause for the initial stop. (c): the §55-10-406 suspension runs consecutive to the §55-10-401 conviction suspension if the court finds the §55-10-406 violation and finds a conviction or juvenile adjudication within five years of the §55-10-406 violation for implied consent, underage driving while impaired (§55-10-415), open container (§55-10-416), or reckless driving (§55-10-205) where the charged offence was §55-10-401. (d): a person who violates §55-10-406 by refusing either or both tests under §55-10-406(d)(4) “shall be charged by a separate warrant or citation that does not include any charge of violating § 55-10-401 that may arise from the same occurrence.” (e): “prior conviction” is determined on a 10-year look-back (a violation 10+ years before the present violation is not a repeat; every violation within 10 years of the immediately preceding violation counts; a violation more than 20 years before the instant violation is never a prior offence); out-of-state DUI, vehicular homicide/assault involving an intoxicant, and aggravated vehicular homicide/assault involving an intoxicant count as priors as defined in §55-10-405. Effective date [secondary-confirmed, flag]: multiple secondary sources (law-firm analyses accessed 2026-10-25) state the 18-month provision in (a)(1)(B) took effect 1 January 2026 under 2025 Public Chapter 403, and that a first refusal of a breath test remains one year — the “18 months for every first refusal” shorthand in some secondary sources is wrong. The Justia history line confirms 2025, ch. 403, §1 as the last amendment but does not itself state the effective date in the portion read. The Tennessee Department of Safety's “DUI Offenses” page (tn.gov/safety DUI outline) still lists the pre-2026 schedule (1 year 1st offence; 2 years 2nd; 2 years crash with bodily injury; 5 years crash with death) and does not show the 18-month blood-test provision — treat the Department page as stale on this point. A further amendment to §55-10-407 scheduled for 1 January 2027 is mentioned in one secondary source (accessed 2026-10-25) — [Verify on capitol.tn.gov; not detailed here because its text was not opened].

DUI penalties, driving prohibition, and ignition interlock (opened, Justia 2025)

  • Fines, §55-10-403 — Justia §55-10-403: (a)(1) 1st offence $350–$1,500; (2) 2nd $600–$3,500; (3) 3rd $1,100–$10,000; (4) 4th or subsequent $3,000–$15,000; (5) any offence while accompanied by a child under 18, an additional $1,000. Minimum fines are mandatory unless the judge finds indigency under §40-14-202(b).
  • Incarceration, §55-10-402(a) — Justia §55-10-402: 1st offence “not less than forty-eight (48) consecutive hours nor more than eleven (11) months and twenty-nine (29) days” (7 consecutive days minimum if BAC 0.15%+ — an alcohol provision, not a THC provision); 2nd offence minimum 45 consecutive days; 3rd minimum 120 consecutive days; 4th offence sentenced as a felon, minimum 150 consecutive days up to the Class E felony maximum; 5th offence Class D felony; 6th or subsequent Class C felony. (b)(1): accompanied by a child under 18 adds a mandatory minimum 30 days; (b)(2)–(3): if the child suffers serious bodily injury, Class D felony (vehicular assault, §39-13-106); if the child is killed, Class B felony (vehicular homicide involving intoxication, §39-13-213(b)(2)). These penalties apply to drug DUI under §55-10-401(a)(1) on the same terms as alcohol DUI — the statute does not create a separate drug-DUI penalty track.
  • Driving prohibition, §55-10-404(a)(1) — Justia §55-10-404: on conviction under §55-10-401 the court “shall prohibit [the person] from driving a vehicle in this state for a period of: (A) One (1) year, if the conviction is a first offense; (B) Two (2) years for a second offense; (C) Six (6) years for a third offense; and (D) Eight (8) years for a fourth or subsequent offense.” Restricted licences are available under §55-10-409 (not opened; do not detail its conditions).
  • Ignition interlock, §55-10-417 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §55-10-417 (history ends 2025, ch. 184, §§1, 2): (a)(1)(A) a court may order an ignition interlock device for any §55-10-401 conviction if the licence is no longer suspended/revoked or the driver has no prior conviction as defined in §55-10-405, for up to one year after reinstatement; (b) calibration setting 0.02% breath alcohol concentration. Note: interlock devices detect alcohol, not THC; no THC interlock provision was located. Interlock violations are Class A misdemeanours under (j)(1), with minimum incarceration 48 hours (1st), 72 hours (2nd), 7 consecutive days (3rd+), and a convicted (h)(2) violator must complete an additional consecutive 365-day interlock period under §55-10-425.

12.4 Expungement and record clearing for marijuana offences — petition-based; NO automatic or marijuana-specific clearance

The 2025 reorganisation — old citations are obsolete

The laws on court expungements were largely reorganised in 2025 (University of Tennessee County Technical Assistance Service, “Expunging Court Records,” CTAS expungement guide, accessed 2026-10-25): “The primary provisions relative to the expungement of dismissed offenses was moved from 40-32-101 to 40-32-106. The primary provisions relative to the expungement of certain less serious convictions was moved from 40-32-101 to 40-32-107 and 40-32-108.” The enacting law is 2025 Public Chapter 268 (SB 1055, substituted for HB 1257; Legiscan chaptered text, Pub. Ch. 268 chaptered text (Legiscan); Legiscan bill page confirms “Comp. became Pub. Ch. 268” on 29 Apr 2025, Legiscan HB1257). Its summary: “As enacted, reorganizes present statutes concerning expunction and specifies that facilitation, attempt, or solicitation of an offense that is presently eligible for expunction is also eligible for expunction.”

Consequences for citation: §40-32-101 in the 2025 Code is definitions only — Justia §40-32-101 (history Acts 2025, ch. 268, §1) — defining “court,” “criminal offense” (including, as context requires, an implied consent violation under §55-10-406), “mistaken identity,” and “public records” (for expunction only, excluding arrest histories, investigative reports, appellate records/opinions, signed confidential expunction orders, law-enforcement intelligence information, confidential district attorney files, and certain Department of Children's Services / Department of Human Services records). Do not cite old §40-32-101(g)/(k) pinpoints as current law. The operative provisions are now §§40-32-106 (dismissed/non-conviction), 40-32-107 (eligible petitioners for convictions), and 40-32-108 (procedure). The 2025 reorganisation did not create automatic expungement and did not broaden marijuana eligibility beyond the pre-existing lists (secondary confirmation: Collateral Consequences Resource Center Tennessee profile, CCRC Tennessee profile, accessed 2026-10-25, as summarised in a secondary 2026 drug-policy brief).

Which marijuana offences are eligible — CONFLICT RESOLVED (no fine cap in current text)

§40-32-107(a)(1) (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §40-32-107 — lists, for offences committed on or after 1 November 1989, including attempt, conspiracy, facilitation, or solicitation:

ClassEntryOffence
Class C felonies, (a)(1)(A)(xxii)Section 39-17-417(g)(3)Manufacture, deliver, sale, or possession of Schedule VI controlled substance.
Class D felonies, (a)(1)(B)(xxv)Section 39-17-417(g)(2)Manufacture, deliver, sale, or possession of certain Schedule VI drugs.
Class E felonies, (a)(1)(C)(xxxiii)Section 39-17-417(g)(1)Manufacture, delivery, sale, or possession of not less than one-half ounce (½ oz.) and not more than ten pounds (10 lbs.) of Schedule VI drug marijuana. Resolution of the prior-turn conflict: the current inclusion list as opened states no fine cap for this entry. The secondary extracts giving “$2,500” (Burch & Stewart blog) and “$1,000” (kruoil.com reproduction) for Schedule VI marijuana do not match the current text as read; neither figure should be published. [Verify against official Lexis text before publication, since the older §40-32-101(g) list may have carried a fine limitation that the 2025 reorganisation dropped or moved — the chaptered Pub. Ch. 268 text read for §40-32-107(a)(1)(C)(xxxiii) likewise states no fine cap.]
Class E felonies, (a)(1)(C)(xxxv)Section 39-17-418(e)Simple possession or casual exchange (third offense).
Class E felonies, (a)(1)(C)(xxxviii)Section 39-17-425(b)(1), (2), (3)Unlawful drug paraphernalia uses and activities (the felony delivery/manufacture and adult-to-minor delivery offences; see §12.1).

Other drug entries on the same lists (for completeness): Class C (a)(1)(A)(xx) §39-17-417(c) Schedule II (incl. cocaine/methamphetamine <0.5 g); (xxi) §39-17-417(e) flunitrazepam; (xxiii) §39-17-454(c) controlled substance analogue (2nd+ violation). Class D (a)(1)(B)(xxiii) §39-17-417(d) Schedule III; (xxiv) §39-17-417(e) Schedule IV; (xxviii) §39-17-438 salvia/synthetic cannabinoids (1st violation); (xxix) §39-17-454(c) analogue (1st violation). Class E (a)(1)(C)(xxxii) §39-17-417(f) Schedule V; (xxxiv) §39-17-417(h) Schedule VII. Misdemeanour analogue/salvia entries are excluded: (a)(1)(D)(xxiv) §39-17-438 possession of salvia/synthetic cannabinoids and (xxv) §39-17-452 synthetic methcathinone derivatives are on the misdemeanour exclusion list.

Misdemeanours generally, (a)(1)(D): “a person who was convicted of a misdemeanor offense committed on or after November 1, 1989” is an eligible petitioner except for the listed exclusions. Simple possession/casual exchange 1st and 2nd offences (Class A misdemeanours under §39-17-418) and paraphernalia use/possession under §39-17-425(a) (Class A misdemeanour) do NOT appear on the (a)(1)(D) exclusion list as opened, and are therefore eligible as misdemeanours subject to the §40-32-107(a)(3) requirements below. The exclusion list does include, relevantly: (xlv) “Section 55-10-401 — Driving under the influence of an intoxicant” (so a drug DUI conviction is never expungeable under §40-32-107(a)); (xl) §39-15-412(b) disseminating smoking paraphernalia to a minor after three prior violations; and the firearm, assault, domestic-violence, stalking, and drug-test-falsification entries. Higher marijuana felonies are NOT eligible: §39-17-417(g) offences above the (g)(3) Class C tier (i.e. Class B and Class A weight tiers, cited in Run 1 as (i)(14)/(j)(14)) do not appear on any §40-32-107(a)(1) list as opened. Do not state they are expungeable.

Pre-1 November 1989 offences, (a)(1)(E): a person convicted of a felony or misdemeanour committed before 1 November 1989 is eligible if they never had a previous conviction expunged via diversion (§§40-15-102–40-15-106 or §40-35-313) and the offence (i) did not have as an element the use/attempted/threatened use of physical force, (ii) did not involve a substantial risk of physical force by its nature, (iii) did not involve a deadly weapon, (iv) was not a registrable sexual offence or a sexual offence involving a minor, (v) did not result in death/serious bodily injury/bodily injury, (vi) did not involve alcohol or drugs and a motor vehicle, (vii) did not involve sale/distribution of a Schedule I or Schedule II drug in a §39-17-417(i) amount, (viii) did not involve a minor victim, and (ix) did not cause victim loss of $60,000+.

Single-episode rule, (a)(1)(F): multiple listed convictions arising contemporaneously, at the same location, as a single continuous criminal episode with a single criminal intent, where all are eligible, “must be considered a single offense for the purposes of this section.”

Requirements and waiting periods — from the chaptered Pub. Ch. 268 text (§40-32-107(a)(3), (b))

The Justia 2025 rendering of §40-32-107 as opened contains only the (a)(1) lists; the operative requirements sit in §40-32-107(a)(3) and (b) as enacted. They were read in the chaptered text (Legiscan PDF, lines 1135–1309, cited above) and are quoted here from that text [Verify against official Lexis text and the capitol.tn.gov chaptered PDF before publication]:

  • Single-offence track, (a)(3): an eligible petitioner under (a) may petition “if: (A)(i) The offense… occurred prior to any conviction for a criminal offense that is ineligible for expunction, including convictions for federal offenses and offenses in other states that would be ineligible for expunction in this state; and (ii) The person has not previously been granted expunction for another criminal offense under this subsection (a), subsection (b), or subsection (c); (B) At the time of the filing of the petition for expunction at least: (i) Five (5) years have elapsed since the completion of the sentence imposed for the offense…, if the offense is a misdemeanor or Class E felony; or (ii) Ten (10) years have elapsed since the completion of the sentence…, if the offense is a Class C or D felony; and (C) The person has fulfilled all the requirements of the sentence…, including: (i) Payment of all fines, restitution, court costs, and other assessments; (ii) Completion of any term of imprisonment or probation; (iii) Meeting all conditions of supervised or unsupervised release; and (iv) If so required by the conditions of the sentence…, remaining free from dependency on or abuse of alcohol or a controlled substance or other prohibited substance for a period of not less than one (1) year.”
  • Two-offence track, (b)(1): “an 'eligible petitioner' means a person who is seeking expunction of no more than two (2) offenses and: (A) Each of the offenses… [is] eligible… under subdivision (a)(1); and [occurred] prior to any conviction for a criminal offense that is ineligible…; (B) The offenses were: (i) Two (2) misdemeanors; or (ii) One (1) felony and one (1) misdemeanor; (C) At the time of the filing… at least: (i) Five (5) years have elapsed since the completion of the sentence… for any misdemeanor or Class E felony…; and (ii) Ten (10) years have elapsed since the completion of the sentence… for any Class C or D felony…; (D) The person has fulfilled all the requirements of the sentences… [same four items as (a)(3)(C)]; and (E) The person has not previously been granted expunction under subsection (a), this subsection (b), or subsection (c).” Contemporaneous single-episode convictions count as a single offence for (b) as well, under (b)(2).
  • Commercial-driver exclusion, (a)(2): “A person is not an eligible petitioner… if the person was convicted of an offense involving the manufacture, delivery, sale, or possession of a controlled substance and at the time of the offense the person held: (A) A commercial driver license… and the offense was committed within a motor vehicle…; or (B) Any driver license and the offense was committed within a commercial motor vehicle…”
  • Other tracks in §40-32-107 (not marijuana-specific): (c) illegal registration/voting (§2-19-107) — 15 years; (d) pardoned nonviolent crime (governor's pardon + positive Board of Parole vote required).

Procedure, fee, effect, and non-conviction track
