Procedure, §40-32-108 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §40-32-108 (Acts 2025, ch. 268, §2): (a) petition in the court of conviction; “A person applying for expunction of records pursuant to this section must be charged the appropriate court clerk's fee pursuant to § 8-21-401.” The old Davidson County checklist figure of $450 (2014/2016) is NOT confirmed as current and must not be published as the fee; §40-32-108(a) states only the §8-21-401 clerk's fee [amount Verify]. (b) clerk serves the district attorney general, who may submit recommendations within 60 days, with a copy to the petitioner. (c) both sides may file evidence; the district attorney may file evidence under seal (confidential, not a public record). (d)(1) the court “shall enter an order granting or denying the petition no sooner than sixty-one (61) days after service,” after reviewing the TBI certificate required by §40-32-102(c) and considering all evidence. (d)(2) the court “shall weigh the interest of the petitioner against the best interests of justice and public safety; provided, that if the petitioner is an eligible petitioner pursuant to § 40-32-107(a)(1)(A)-(E) and meets the applicable requirements in § 40-32-107, then there is a rebuttable presumption that the petition should be granted.” (d)(3) if denied, no new petition until at least two years from denial. (e) the petition and proposed order “must be prepared by the office of the district attorney general and given to the petitioner to be filed with the clerk”; a copy of the expunction order “is sufficient proof that the person named in the order is no longer under any disability, disqualification, or other adverse consequence resulting from the expunged conviction.” (f) expunged records are confidential, “can only be used to enhance a sentence if the petitioner is subsequently charged and convicted of another crime,” and are accessible only to the district attorney general, the defendant, the defendant's attorney, and the judge. (i) an order granted under the section has the effect provided in §40-32-110 (not opened; do not paraphrase its effect beyond §40-32-108(e)).

TBI certificate, §40-32-102(c) (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §40-32-102: TBI “shall develop a request for certification form to be completed by the court and submitted to the TBI prior to entering an order of expunction” (not required for §40-32-109 or §40-32-106 expunctions, unless the §40-32-106 expunction results from successful pretrial/judicial diversion). The certificate contains the person's name, date of birth, social security number, offence, arrest date, and state control number (§8-4-115). TBI “shall determine if the submitted offense is eligible for expunction pursuant to § 40-32-107 or § 40-32-105 and note the determination on the certificate.” “An order of expunction must not be entered by the court unless a certificate… is attached.” The certificate certifies only offence eligibility, not the defendant's eligibility; “the court continues to have the duty to determine eligibility.” (a)–(b): agencies and court clerks must remove and destroy records within 60 days of the expunction order; TBI must remove expunged records from criminal history within 60 days of receipt. (d): the court must send the order to TBI within 30 days for entry into the expunged offender and pretrial diversion database. (e): release of confidential expunged records other than to law enforcement for law-enforcement purposes is a Class A misdemeanour punishable as described in §40-32-104.

Non-conviction track, §40-32-106 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §40-32-106: (a)(1) all public records of a person charged with a misdemeanour or felony “must, upon petition…, be removed and destroyed without cost” if: (A) the charge was dismissed for a reason other than successful pretrial/judicial diversion; (B) a no true bill was returned; (C) the person was arrested and released without being charged; (D) a nolle prosequi was entered; (E) a not-guilty verdict was returned on all charges; (F) except as provided in (b)(2), the person was charged with an implied consent violation under §55-10-406 and it was dismissed without cost; (G) the charge abated by death (personal representative may apply); (H) mistaken identity (expedited expunction available on a court finding); or (I) the conviction was for protesting/challenging a racial-segregation/discrimination law or ordinance, 37+ years elapsed, no other conviction (excluding minor traffic) in that period, supervision completed, offence a misdemeanour or Class C/D/E felony not otherwise excluded, not a Class A/B felony, not a registrable sexual offence, not a Title 55, Chapter 10, Part 4 offence, not vehicular assault, and the district attorney does not object within 20 calendar days of certified-mail service. (b) bars expunction where: the implied-consent violation was dismissed with costs; the implied-consent dismissal without costs involved a commercial driver/vehicle; the person was found not guilty by reason of insanity or incompetent to stand trial; or, except as provided in (c), the person was not convicted of the charged offence but was convicted of a related/lesser-included offence, or was charged with multiple offences/counts in one indictment and convicted of one or more of them or a related offence. (c)(2) partial-expungement safety valve: a person ineligible under (b)(4) is still entitled, on petition, to removal of public records from electronic databases (NCIC and similar state databases, and court-clerk public electronic databases) for charges other than the offence of conviction, with entry into TBI's expunged criminal offender and pretrial diversion database; clerks need not expunge records of the offence of conviction. (d): records dismissed on successful pretrial diversion (§§40-15-102–40-15-107) or judicial diversion (§40-35-313) may be petitioned for expunction, except where the diverted offence was a sexual or violent sexual offence (§40-39-202); the §8-21-401 clerk's fee applies. (e)(2): on a not-guilty verdict on all charges, the judge must ask the acquitted person whether they want the records removed and destroyed without cost and without a petition; if they decline then but request later, they must petition.

Judicial diversion is distinct from expungement and was not opened for Part 12 beyond the §40-32-106(d) cross-reference above; do not conflate diversion dismissal with conviction expungement. No marijuana-specific, no automatic: no Tennessee statute providing automatic sealing/clearance of marijuana records, and no marijuana-specific expungement track, was located. Every track above is petition-based and requires a court order. 2026 changes [search result, not a proved negative]: no enacted 2026 law creating automatic or marijuana-specific expungement was located in searches on 2026-10-25. Proposals that would have shortened waits were not shown as enacted: SB 956 (114th General Assembly — as introduced, misdemeanour/Class E waits 5→3 years, Class D 10→6 years; secondary tracking shows it passed the Senate 13 Mar 2025 and was held on the House desk 17 Mar 2025) and HB 835 (as introduced, Class D wait 10→8 years; secondary tracking shows it reset off the Criminal Justice Subcommittee calendar 5 Mar 2025 and dead) — both secondary (BillTrack50/TrackBill/LegiScan), [Verify final disposition on capitol.tn.gov]. SB 1232 (2025, expunction of a later offence 10+ years after a DUI conviction) likewise was not shown as enacted. Do not present any of these as law.

Gun-rights interaction (secondary, dated — do not use as authority): a 2012 secondary summary states expungement restores firearm possession/carry rights. The official text to use instead is §39-17-1351(j) (see §12.6), including the (j)(3) carve-out that citizenship restoration does not apply to a person convicted of a felony drug offence (among others).

12.5 Landlord and tenant rules — eviction for drug use is fast, but the drug-specific 3-day rule sits in different sections for the two regimes

Two regimes, confirmed on the statute text

URLTA applicability, §66-28-102(a) (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §66-28-102: “(a) This chapter applies only in counties having a population of more than seventy-five thousand (75,000), according to the 2010 federal census.” This confirms the secondary summary (Nolo) that URLTA applies in counties over 75,000 on the 2010 Census (Shelby, Davidson, Knox, Hamilton, Rutherford given as examples — county list is secondary; the threshold itself is statutory). (d): URLTA “shall not apply to any occupancy in a public housing unit or other housing unit that is subject to regulation by [HUD] and owned by a governmental entity or nonprofit corporation to the extent such regulation conflicts with state law, but shall apply to the extent that any such regulations defer to the application of state law.” (e): in URLTA counties, the chapter “occupies and preempts the entire field of legislation concerning the regulation of landlords and tenants.” Non-URLTA counties are governed by Title 66, Chapter 7.

URLTA counties — §66-28-505 (general breach) and §66-28-517 (3-day danger termination)

§66-28-505 (opened via FindLaw reproduction, prior turn) — FindLaw §66-28-505: (a)(1) on material noncompliance with the rental agreement or noncompliance with §66-28-401 materially affecting health and safety, the landlord may deliver written notice specifying the acts/omissions constituting the breach and that the agreement shall terminate as provided in (a)(2) or (a)(3). (a)(2) if the breach is remediable by payment of rent, repairs, damages, or any other amount due, the landlord must allow 14 days to remedy after receipt, with a 7-day termination notice if substantially the same act/omission recurs within six months. (a)(3) if the breach is not remediable by payment/repairs/damages, the landlord may terminate on a date not less than 14 days after receipt. The §66-28-505 extract as returned contains no drug-specific rule.

§66-28-517 (opened, Justia 2025, this turn — correcting the prior turn's THDA-summary reliance) — Justia §66-28-517: (a): “A landlord may terminate a rental agreement within three (3) days from the date written notice is received by the tenant if the tenant or any other person on the premises with the tenant's consent: (1) Willfully or intentionally commits a violent act; (2) Behaves in a manner which constitutes or threatens to be a real and present danger to the health, safety or welfare of the life or property of other tenants or persons on the premises; (3) Creates a hazardous or unsanitary condition on the property that affects the health, safety or welfare or the life or property of other tenants or persons on the premises; or (4) Refuses to vacate the premises after entering the premises as an unauthorized subtenant or other unauthorized occupant.” (b): the notice “shall specifically detail the violation” and is effective only from the date of receipt. (c): on receipt, the tenant “shall be entitled to immediate access to any court of competent jurisdiction for the purpose of obtaining a temporary or permanent injunction against such termination.” (d): nothing in the section allows the landlord to recover/take possession other than by action, including by wilful diminution of services (e.g. cutting electric/gas/essential services), except on abandonment or surrender. (e): if the landlord's termination under the section is wilful and not in good faith, the tenant may recover actual damages plus reasonable attorney's fees. (f): failure to seek an injunction may not be used as evidence in a possession action. Correction to the prior turn: §66-28-517 as opened does not name drug-related criminal activity. A URLTA landlord seeking a 3-day termination for drug activity must therefore fit the conduct into (a)(2) (real and present danger) or (a)(3) (hazardous/unsanitary condition), or proceed under §66-28-505. The THDA rule summary's sentence — “Some landlords also will terminate the lease with a 3-day notice for drug-related criminal activity by a household member, guest, or person under the tenant's control” (THDA Rule 0770-01-05-.27, Justia Reg. 0770-01-05-.27) — is an administrative summary of landlord practice, not a quotation of §66-28-517, and must be labelled as such.

Non-URLTA counties and housing authorities — drug activity IS named in §66-7-109(d)

§66-7-109 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §66-7-109: (a)(1): 14 days' notice is sufficient for (A) tenant neglect/refusal to pay rent due and in arrears on demand; (B) damage beyond normal wear and tear by the tenant, household members, or guests; or (C) the tenant or any other person on the premises with the tenant's consent wilfully or intentionally commits a violent act or behaves in a manner constituting/threatening a real and present danger to the health, safety, or welfare of the life or property of other tenants, the landlord, the landlord's representatives, or other persons on the premises. (a)(2): for (a)(1)(A)/(B), if the breach is remediable and adequately remedied before the notice date, the agreement does not terminate; recurrence of substantially the same act/omission within six months allows termination on at least 14 days' written notice. (b): for all other lease defaults, a 30-day termination notice is required. (g): “Nothing in this section shall apply to rental property located in any county governed by [URLTA].” (d) — the drug-specific 3-day rule: “Notwithstanding § 66-7-107 or this section to the contrary, three (3) days' notice by a landlord is sufficient notice of termination of tenancy to evict a residential tenant in a housing authority created pursuant to title 13, chapter 20, part 4 or 5, or a residential tenant, who is not mentally or physically disabled, in a rental property located in any county not governed by [URLTA], if the tenant, in either case, or any other person on the premises with the tenant's consent, willfully or intentionally: (1) Commits a violent act; (2) Engages in any drug-related criminal activity; or (3) Behaves in a manner that constitutes or threatens to be a real and present danger to the health, safety, or welfare of the life or property of other tenants, the landlord, the landlord's representatives, or other persons on the premises.” (f): 3 days' notice is also sufficient to evict an unauthorised subtenant/occupant who refuses to vacate.

§66-7-107, Termination for knowing controlled substance or prostitution violations (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §66-7-107: (a)(1): “An occupant's tenancy may be terminated where the premises or the area immediately surrounding the premises is knowingly used or occupied in whole or in part to violate § 39-13-513, § 39-13-515 or § 39-17-417.” (Note: this reaches manufacture/delivery/sale/possession with intent under §39-17-417, not simple possession under §39-17-418 as a standalone matter, and requires knowing use/occupancy.) (a)(2): the identity of any person providing evidence/information resulting in an eviction/termination under the section is confidential and not a public record of the law-enforcement agency or district attorney general. (b): the district attorney general for the district may serve the owner/landlord (personally, or by registered/certified return-receipt mail) with written notice requiring the owner/landlord to inform the district attorney in writing of intent to diligently and in good faith seek eviction; if the owner/landlord does not so inform within five days, or does not diligently prosecute the eviction in good faith, the district attorney may bring the eviction proceeding in general sessions court (including a designated environmental court) or circuit court as though the district attorney were the owner/landlord, with precedence over later or non-diligently-prosecuted landlord proceedings; the possessor and the owner/landlord are respondents. (c) costs and reasonable attorney's fees to the district attorney may be ordered (multiple respondents jointly and severally liable). (e): the owner/landlord must pay the costs of physically removing the tenant's belongings under a court-ordered eviction brought by the district attorney under the section, capped at $200 per eviction order.

Property-owner rights under §57-7-111 and the absence of tenant protection

§57-7-111 (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §57-7-111 (Acts 2025, ch. 526, §2): (a): “This chapter does not permit a person to: (1) Undertake any task under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP when doing so would constitute negligence or professional malpractice; or (2) Operate, navigate, or be in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft, motorized watercraft, or any other vehicle while under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP.” (b): “This chapter does not require: (1) An employer to accommodate the use of hemp-derived cannabinoids or HDCPs in a workplace or an employee working while under the influence of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or an HDCP; (2) An individual or establishment in lawful possession of property to allow a guest, client, customer, or other visitor to use a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP on or in that property; or (3) An individual or establishment in lawful possession of property to admit a guest, client, customer, or other visitor who is visibly impaired as a result of the person's use of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP.” (c): Chapter 7 “does not exempt a person from prosecution for a criminal offense related to impairment or intoxication resulting from use of a hemp-derived cannabinoid or HDCP or relieve a person from any requirement under law to submit to a breath, blood, urine, or other test to detect the presence of a controlled substance.” (d): Chapter 7 “(1) [does not] Limit the ability of an employer to establish, continue, or enforce a drug-free workplace program or policy; (2) Create a cause of action against an employer for wrongful discharge or discrimination; or (3) Allow the possession, sale, manufacture, or distribution of any substance that is otherwise prohibited by title 39, chapter 17, part 4.” Application to tenancy (stated as analysis of the text, not as a separate tenant statute): by the same structure, Title 57, Chapter 7 creates no tenant right to use marijuana or HDCPs in a rental unit, and §57-7-111(b)(2)–(3) expressly preserves a property possessor's right to refuse use on the property and to refuse admission to a visibly impaired visitor. No Tennessee statute creating a tenant protection for lawful HDCP use, low-THC oil use, or marijuana use was located in searches on 2026-10-25. Lease terms prohibiting smoking, drug use, or illegal activity remain enforceable through the §66-28-505/517 (URLTA) or §66-7-107/109 (non-URLTA) regimes above, subject to the notice periods for the applicable county regime. Smoking in a unit is also governed by the lease and, where applicable, the property's smoke-free policy — no Tennessee statute was located that gives a tenant a right to smoke hemp or marijuana in a rental unit. Eviction procedure generally (secondary, not opened — [Verify if included in Run 2]): detainer warrant in General Sessions Court, 10 days to appeal to Circuit Court, appeal bond including rent owed (Nolo, accessed 2026-10-25; §§29-18-126, 29-18-128 cited secondarily).

Public housing and voucher consequences

Federal lease term (case law, secondary summary of the statute/regulation): Memphis Housing Authority v. Thompson (Tenn. 2000, Justia, Thompson) discusses the federal requirement, 42 U.S.C. §1437(d)(l)(6) and 24 C.F.R. §966.4(f)(12)(i)(B), (l)(2)(ii)(B), that public housing leases provide that drug-related criminal activity on or off the premises by a tenant, household member, guest, or person under the tenant's control is cause for termination of tenancy and eviction. A secondary commentary on the case (Casemine, accessed 2026-10-25) states the Tennessee Supreme Court applied a knowledge-based standard for eviction based on third parties' drug-related activity — i.e. the tenant must have known or failed to act — rather than strict liability. [Verify against the opinion itself before stating the knowledge standard; the case text was located but its holding language was not read in full for Part 12.]

THDA voucher termination protocol (administrative rule summary, Justia Regulations) — Justia Reg. 0770-01-05-.27 (rule title cites 24 C.F.R. §§982.552(b), 982.310, 982.455, 982.354): in a 3-day termination case, THDA follows the same protocol as for lease-violation terminations; the participant is not eligible to relocate; the owner must evict by court order; THDA continues to pay through eviction proceedings unless there is a separate cause for THDA to terminate; if the participant is evicted by court order, assistance is terminated, the participant gets the regular appeal period, but THDA cannot continue the Housing Assistance Payments contract/payment at the current unit due to lease termination; if the family appeals, a voucher to relocate is issued through the hearing period. The rule also references THDA-initiated termination of assistance for violent criminal activity. The federal regulations cited in the rule title (24 C.F.R. Part 982) were not opened for Part 12 and must not be summarised from the rule title alone.

Local PHA practice (secondary, illustrative only): a Tennessee Valley Housing Services continued-occupancy policy (ACOP rev. 7/26, eff. 1/1/27, TVHS ACOP, accessed 2026-10-25) denies admission where any household member is currently engaged in, or within the past five years engaged in, drug-related criminal activity as defined by HUD (24 C.F.R. §5.100: illegal manufacture, sale, distribution, or use of a drug, or possession with intent to manufacture, sell, distribute, or use). This is one PHA's policy, not Tennessee law, and is included only to show that federally assisted housing treats marijuana — illegal federally — as drug-related criminal activity even where Tennessee HDCP use is lawful under state law. No Tennessee-specific public-housing marijuana/HDCP rule beyond the THDA protocol above was located.

Bottom line for Part 12: because marijuana remains illegal under federal law, federally assisted housing may treat marijuana use/possession as drug-related criminal activity; Tennessee's own HDCP law (§57-7-111) creates no countervailing tenant protection; and the state-law eviction routes are §§66-28-517/505 (URLTA) and §§66-7-107/109 (non-URLTA/housing authority).

12.6 Gun ownership conflicts — federal prohibition, Tennessee permit law, under-the-influence possession, and Hemani

Tennessee handgun carry permit — §39-17-1351 (opened, Justia 2025)

Justia §39-17-1351: (b): any Tennessee resident who is a U.S. citizen or permanent lawful resident (as defined by §55-50-102) who has reached 18 years of age may apply to the Department of Safety for a handgun carry permit. “If the applicant is not prohibited from purchasing or possessing a firearm in this state pursuant to §39-17-1316 or §39-17-1307(b), 18 U.S.C. §922(g), or any other state or federal law, and otherwise meets all requirements, the department shall issue a permit.” (c)(10) (verbatim in substance): the applicant must disclose and confirm under oath “That the applicant is not an unlawful user of or addicted to alcohol, any controlled substance or controlled substance analogue, and the applicant has not been either: (A) A patient in a rehabilitation program pursuant to a court order or hospitalized for alcohol, controlled substance or controlled substance analogue abuse or addiction pursuant to a court order within ten (10) years from the date of application; or (B) A voluntary patient in a rehabilitation program or voluntarily hospitalized for alcohol, controlled substance or controlled substance analogue abuse or addiction within three (3) years from the date of application.” (c)(11): the applicant must confirm (A) no conviction for DUI of an intoxicant in this or any other state two or more times within ten years of application/renewal, and (B) none of those convictions occurred within five years of application/renewal. (c)(6)–(7), (12), (16), (18): no felony conviction and no disqualifying misdemeanour conviction under (c)(11), (c)(16), or (c)(18) (antitrust/unfair-trade exceptions), no current indictment/information for such an offence, no adjudication as a mental defective / judicial commitment / hospitalisation in a mental institution under Title 33 / conservator by reason of mental defect / judicial determination of disability by reason of mental illness, developmental disability, or other mental incapacity, no finding within seven years of posing an immediate substantial likelihood of serious harm because of mental illness under Title 33, Chapter 6, Part 5, no misdemeanour crime of domestic violence as defined in 18 U.S.C. §921, and no stalking conviction. (j): the department shall not deny an application if (1) any arrest or other records for any indictment, charge, or warrant have been judicially or administratively expunged; (2) the applicant's conviction has been set aside by a court of competent jurisdiction; (3) the applicant, rendered infamous or deprived of citizenship rights by judgment of any state or federal court, has had full rights of citizenship duly restored under Title 40, Chapter 29, or other federal or state law — provided (j)(3) does not apply to any person convicted of a felony crime of violence, an attempt to commit a felony crime of violence, a felony drug offense, or a felony offense involving use of a deadly weapon; or (4) a mental-defective/commitment firearm disability has been removed by court order under Title 16 and a copy provided to the department by TBI or by the applicant. Practical consequence: expungement under §40-32-107/108 (see §12.4) removes the (j)(1) bar for the expunged arrest/charge, but (j)(3)'s felony-drug-offence carve-out means citizenship restoration alone does not cure a felony drug conviction for permit purposes.

Permitless carry, prohibited carrying, and handgun while under the influence — §39-17-1307, §39-17-1321 (search extracts from Justia 2025, prior turn — [Verify against full text])

§39-17-1307(g) — Justia §39-17-1307: it is an exception to §39-17-1307(a) that a person is carrying, openly or concealed, a handgun and (1)(A) is at least 21 years of age, or (1)(B) is at least 18 and is an honorably discharged/retired veteran, honorably discharged reserve/guard member who completed basic training, or active-duty/current guard/reserve member who completed basic training; (2) lawfully possesses the handgun; and (3) is in a place where lawfully present. This exception does not override §39-17-1307(h) or §39-17-1321. §39-17-1307(h)(1): a person commits an offence who carries, with intent to go armed, a firearm and (A) has been convicted of stalking under §39-17-315; (B) has been convicted of DUI of an intoxicant in this or any other state two or more times within the prior ten years or one time within the prior five years; (C) has been adjudicated as a mental defective, judicially committed to or hospitalised in a mental institution under Title 33, or had a court appoint a conservator by reason of mental defect; or (D) is otherwise prohibited from possessing a firearm by 18 U.S.C. §922(g) as it existed on January 1, 2021. (h)(2): Class B misdemeanour. The 1 January 2021 freeze date in (h)(1)(D) is a live Verify question: whether post-2021 federal developments, including Hemani and the 2026 ATF rule, affect a Tennessee (h)(1)(D) charge has not been resolved in any source opened for Part 12 — do not assume either way. §39-17-1321 — Justia §39-17-1321 (history: Acts 1994, ch. 943, §10; 1997, ch. 476, §4; 2010, ch. 1009, §2; 2012, ch. 848, §22; 2019, ch. 479, §16, effective 1 January 2020 inserting §39-17-1366): (a) “Notwithstanding whether a person has a permit issued pursuant to §39-17-1315 or §39-17-1351 or §39-17-1366, it is an offense for a person to possess a handgun while under the influence of alcohol or any controlled substance or controlled substance analogue.” (b): it is also an offence to possess a firearm if the person is both within an establishment open to the public where liquor, wine, or other alcoholic beverages (§57-3-101(a)) or beer (§57-6-102) are served for consumption on the premises, and consuming any such alcoholic beverage. (c)(1): violation is a Class A misdemeanour. (c)(2): in addition, if the (a) violation occurs in a (b)(1) establishment and the person holds a permit under §39-17-1351 or §39-17-1366, the permit shall be suspended under §39-17-1352 for three years. The §39-17-1308 defence to §39-17-1307 prosecutions may not be asserted as a defence to §39-17-1321, per Attorney General Opinion 98-151 (12 August 1998) as cited in secondary summaries — that opinion was not opened for Part 12 and is labelled as an opinion, not law, if cited.

Attorney General opinions on “unlawful user” and DUI permits — opinions, not law

  • Opinion 23-004 (28 Feb 2023) — AG Op. 23-004: an enhanced handgun carry permit holder who satisfies §39-17-1351(c)(11) does not commit an offence under §39-17-1307(h)(1)(B) when carrying with intent to go armed, and §39-17-1308(a)(2) provides a defence to §39-17-1307 if possession/carrying was by a person authorised under §39-17-1351. Reasoning: §§39-17-1351(c)(11) and 39-17-1307(h)(1)(B) mirror one another. [Opinion, not law.]
  • Opinion 14-086 (2014) — AG Op. 14-086: “unlawful user” in §39-17-1351(c)(10) is not specifically defined in Tennessee Code and Tennessee appellate courts had not then construed it in that context; where there is no directly relevant state law, it is appropriate to look to related federal law, citing Thomas v. Oldfield, 279 S.W.3d 259 (Tenn. 2009). The opinion then turns to 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3). Full text beyond the search extract was not read for Part 12. [Opinion, not law.]
  • Opinion 08-022 (2008) — AG Op. 08-022: §39-17-1351(c)(10) states two conjunctive requirements — affirming not being an unlawful user of or addicted to alcohol or any controlled substance, and affirming not having been a rehabilitation patient/hospitalised for abuse or addiction within the stated periods. Its subdivision numbering reflects the statute as it stood in 2008 and must be checked against current (c)(10) before quoting pinpoints. [Opinion, not law.]

Federal prohibition — 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) (opened via U.S. Code, House of Representatives)

18 U.S.C. §922 (House USC) (accessed 2026-10-25): “(g) It shall be unlawful for any person— … (3) who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance (as defined in section 102 of the Controlled Substances Act (21 U.S.C. 802)); … to ship or transport in interstate or foreign commerce, or possess in or affecting commerce, any firearm or ammunition; or to receive any firearm or ammunition which has been shipped or transported in interstate or foreign commerce.” The current ATF Form 4473 “unlawful user” question and instructions were not opened for Part 12 [next step]. From Run 1 federal work (flagged Verify there, carried here): the ATF interim final rule effective 22 January 2026 rewrote 27 C.F.R. §478.11 to require regular use continuing into the present, and Schedule III alone does not remove medical users from §922(g)(3) text, with no ATF safe harbour located — neither point is Tennessee law and neither is presented as such.

Interaction with low-THC oil and HDCP use — UNAVAILABLE (no Tennessee authority located)

No Tennessee statute, AG opinion, or Department of Safety guidance was located on 2026-10-25 stating that lawful low-THC oil possession under §39-17-402(16)(F), or lawful HDCP use under Title 57, Chapter 7, is or is not “unlawful use” for §922(g)(3) or §39-17-1351(c)(10) purposes. The analysis that can be stated without overstating it: Low-THC oil that is excluded from Tennessee's definition of “marijuana” is not a Tennessee controlled-substance offence to possess on those terms — but federal “controlled substance” status turns on the federal CSA schedules, and marijuana remains Schedule I federally for Tennessee users because Tennessee has no state-licensed medical programme. A Tennessee low-THC oil possessor therefore cannot rely on the federal Schedule III move for state-licensed medical marijuana. HDCPs that are lawful under Tennessee law but contain THC above the federal hemp definition after 11 December 2026 present a separate federal-status question that no Tennessee source located for Part 12 resolves for firearms purposes. What Hemani does not do for Tennessee users: it does not create a Tennessee medical-marijuana firearms safe harbour, does not repeal §922(g)(3) text, and does not change §39-17-1351(c)(10), §39-17-1307(h)(1)(D), or §39-17-1321. Whether the Department of Safety, TBI, or a Tennessee court has applied Hemani to a permit denial or a §39-17-1307(h)(1)(D) charge remains [Verify]. Secondary 2026 development [Verify, not verified as enacted]: Vote Smart's Tennessee key-votes summary (Vote Smart TN, accessed 2026-10-25) lists SB 1603, “Requires Legislative Approval Before Rescheduling or Removing Marijuana as a Controlled Substance,” as passed in the Senate 26 Mar 2026 (21–12) and in the House 13 Apr 2026 (73–22). Governor action and public-chapter status were not confirmed for Part 12; do not present it as enacted law. If enacted, it would be relevant to any future Tennessee Schedule III adoption — but it does not change the current position that Tennessee has no state-licensed medical programme.

United States v. Hemani — VOTE-COUNT CONFLICT RESOLVED from the slip opinion

Slip opinion opened in full (syllabus + lineup), United States v. Hemani, No. 24-1234, argued 2 March 2026, decided 18 June 2026 — Hemani slip opinion (SCOTUS) (cited as 608 U.S. ___ (2026)): Facts (syllabus): “Ali Hemani is a dual citizen of the United States and Pakistan who was born in Texas. He has spent most of his life living in the Dallas area with his parents and working a stable job. Suspecting Mr. Hemani and his family members of terrorism-related activities, the government conducted a search of the family home in 2022. Throughout the process, Mr. Hemani proved cooperative: he surrendered a gun he kept in the house, pointed agents to some marijuana on the property, and consented to an interview during which he told law enforcement agents that he used marijuana about every other day. More than six months after the search, and relying solely on Mr. Hemani's admitted use of marijuana, the government prosecuted Mr. Hemani under 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) for knowingly possessing a gun in his home while being an unlawful user of a controlled substance.” The district court granted his motion to dismiss; the Fifth Circuit affirmed; the Supreme Court affirmed. Holding (syllabus): “Held: The government's prosecution of Mr. Hemani under §922(g)(3)'s unlawful user provision is inconsistent with the Second Amendment.” Vote-count resolution: the syllabus lineup reads: “GORSUCH, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which ROBERTS, C. J., and THOMAS, SOTOMAYOR, KAVANAUGH, BARRETT, and JACKSON, JJ., joined. THOMAS, J., filed a concurring opinion. JACKSON, J., filed a concurring opinion, in which SOTOMAYOR, J., joined. ALITO, J., filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, in which KAGAN, J., joined.” Correct statement: the opinion of the Court was joined by seven Justices; all nine Justices agreed on the judgment (affirmance), with Alito and Kagan concurring in the judgment only. The prior turn's secondary conflict is therefore resolved as follows: “7–2” is wrong as a description of the judgment (it was 9–0 on the result, 7 on the majority opinion's reasoning); “unanimous” is imprecise unless qualified as unanimous on the judgment. Reasoning (syllabus, (b)): the government's habitual-drunkard analogy “fails on every metric”: (1) different people — historical laws targeted persons “practically incapacitated and incapable of managing their affairs,” while §922(g)(3) on the government's account “automatically disarms anyone who regularly uses any amount of any controlled substance… without requiring a showing that a particular individual is regularly incapacitated, incapable of conducting his affairs, or a threat to himself or others”; (2) different purposes — vagrancy laws promoted productivity and suppressed vices, civil-commitment laws protected drunkards from themselves and families from financial devastation, and surety laws protected the community from scandals “against good morals,” not from a category of unusually dangerous persons; (3) different procedures — historical laws “usually provided some form of process before an individual lost any of his liberties” (conviction, probate-court proceeding, or justice-of-the-peace proceeding), while §922(g)(3) “automatically divests an individual of his constitutional right to bear arms the moment he becomes an unlawful user and until he ends his drug use — all without any pre-deprivation process.” (4) The syllabus also notes reasons to doubt §922(g)(3) serves its claimed purpose, including that “the government recently moved some marijuana products from Schedule I to Schedule III, 91 Fed. Reg. 22714” and that DOJ directed prosecutors to curtail enforcement against marijuana users. Narrowness (syllabus, (c)) — quote verbatim: “The Court's decision is narrow. It does not address efforts to ban addicts or those presently intoxicated from possessing a firearm; other prophylactic laws Congress might adopt after determining that users of a particular drug pose a special risk of misusing firearms; §922(g)(1)'s provision disarming individuals convicted of felonies; or whether the government could bring a prosecution under §922(g)(3) accompanied by individualized proof that the defendant's drug use renders him a danger to himself or others, or proof that a certain drug always renders its users dangerous.” Tennessee application (restated): Hemani limits only a status-only §922(g)(3) prosecution on its facts (regular marijuana user, in-home possession, no danger showing). It does not authorise any Tennessee conduct, does not affect Tennessee's permit affirmation in §39-17-1351(c)(10), and — because Tennessee has no state-licensed medical programme — the federal Schedule III point noted in the syllabus does not create a Tennessee user class.

12.7 Pet and veterinary products — no Tennessee veterinary-board rule located; law and veterinary guidance kept separate

Tennessee law: no pet-specific statute; the general hemp/HDCP framework is all that applies

No Tennessee statute specifically regulating pet CBD/hemp products was located in searches on 2026-10-25, and no Tennessee Board of Veterinary Medical Examiners guidance, rule, or policy statement on CBD/hemp pet products was located in searches on 2026-10-25. Both are search results as of that date, not proved negatives. The Board's site, Chapter 1730 rules, and any Department of Agriculture animal-feed/supplement rules remain next steps (see gaps list). The only Tennessee law that can be applied so far is the Run 1 framework: hemp is excluded from “marijuana” (§39-17-402(16)(C); §39-17-415(c)); CBD, CBG, CBN, CBC, etc. alone are excluded from the Title 57, Chapter 7 definition of “hemp-derived cannabinoid” (§57-7-102(3)(C)(i)–(viii)); a product that also contains a regulated cannabinoid above 0.1% becomes an HDCP; HDCPs are subject to the 21+ sale rule, testing, labelling, and venue rules in Title 57, Chapter 7. Whether a pet product marketed as a “supplement,” “treat,” or “feed” falls inside or outside the HDCP definition turns on its cannabinoid content and its intended use (ingested orally, inhaled, or absorbed through the skin, §57-7-102(4)), not on the species of the intended consumer — but no Tennessee agency guidance applying that definition to pet products was located, so that application is flagged [Verify], not stated as an agency position. Veterinary practice law (general, not cannabis-specific): Tennessee's veterinary licensing chapter (Title 63, Chapter 12) provides for discipline for, among other things, dispensing/prescribing/distributing a controlled substance or other drug “not in the course of the accepted practice of veterinary medicine” (§63-12-124, as reproduced secondarily at USLegal Tennessee veterinarians and in Justia 2017 text at Justia 2017 §63-12-124). No provision of that chapter mentioning cannabis, hemp, or CBD was located. Do not cite the general discipline provision as a cannabis rule.

Federal veterinary position — secondary summaries of FDA/AVMA positions, not Tennessee law

No FDA-approved veterinary cannabinoid drug: secondary summaries of the FDA position (dvm360, accessed 2026-10-25, dvm360; JD Supra, accessed 2026-10-25, JD Supra CBD) agree that FDA has approved one cannabis-derived human drug (Epidiolex) and three cannabis-related human drugs (Marinol, Syndros, Cesamet), has not approved cannabis for any use in animals, and cannot ensure the safety or effectiveness of cannabis products marketed for animals. FDA Center for Veterinary Medicine asked veterinarians in January 2025 to share experience with cannabis-derived products in animals, including adverse events and drug interactions (same secondary sources). FDA CVM guidance itself was not opened for Part 12 — [Verify on fda.gov before publishing the FDA position as a direct quotation]. Human FDA-approved products under §39-17-402(16)(D) do not transfer to veterinary use by that section alone. AVMA position (secondary): per the same JD Supra summary, AVMA states products for animals with therapeutic claims not evaluated and approved by FDA are unapproved animal drugs, considered “unsafe” under the FD&C Act because safety/efficacy have not been shown, and their use “can put pets at risk and possibly create legal risks for veterinarians who administer, prescribe, dispense or recommend them.” A veterinary-practice article (Today's Veterinary Practice, accessed 2026-10-25, Today's Veterinary Practice) reports AVMA encourages veterinarians to communicate with clients about treatment options including FDA-approved animal drugs or off-label uses of FDA-approved human drugs, and that experts advise avoiding the terms “prescribe” or “dispense” for hemp-derived CBD products, documenting discussions (including drug interactions, side-effect monitoring, and toxicity/overdose potential), and requesting a certificate of analysis to confirm the product is hemp and not marijuana; it also reports assessments that “as many as 70% of hemp-based products available may not contain the concentration of chemical components indicated on the label.” All of this is professional-practice guidance, not Tennessee law.

Market evidence only, not law: Nashville-area retail listings (Green Treez Company, accessed 2026-10-25, prior turn) sell hemp CBD pet spray oil (500 mg, broad-spectrum, marketed as veterinarian-formulated) and horse relief pellets (1,500 mg full-spectrum CBD, 1 lb pellets, 7.5 g pellets equalling roughly 25 mg CBD as marketed), with nationwide shipping statements and state shipping restrictions for delta-8 and THCa products. Those listings prove products are marketed in Tennessee, not that they are lawful, approved, or veterinarian-recommended under Tennessee law. A secondary guide (Cannimal, accessed 2026-10-25, prior turn) classifies Tennessee as “Legal • Gray area • Allowed but veterinary rules unclear” — a secondary classification, not a Tennessee rule. A secondary veterinary-authored guide (Peak Therapeutics, updated 3 August 2026, accessed 2026-10-25, prior turn) advises veterinarian formulation, batch-specific Certificate of Analysis from an ISO 17025-accredited laboratory, full-spectrum hemp from a stated source, extraction method, weight-based dosing, and talking with the owner's own veterinarian first.

THC toxicity in pets — veterinary/medical guidance, not law

ASPCA position (opened via ASPCA pages in search results, accessed 2026-10-25) — ASPCA, Pets and Marijuana: signs pets commonly show after ingesting marijuana or marijuana products include “unsteadiness on the feet, depression, dilated eyes, dribbling urine, sensitivity to sound and touch, slow heart rate and even low body temperature.” With higher-concentration products or large quantities, “more serious concerns are possible. Your pet may show signs of low blood pressure, agitation, seizures or even aspirate. In rare cases, death has occurred.” ASPCA advice: treat marijuana/THC products as medication, keep them far out of paws' reach (especially edibles), and if exposure is suspected, “contact your veterinarian or call Animal Poison Control Center (APCC) at 888-426-4435 immediately.” Corroborating veterinary summaries (secondary): AKC Canine Health Foundation poison-prevention library (AKC CHF, accessed 2026-10-25) lists the same core signs (urinary incontinence, disorientation, ataxia/wobbly gait, lethargy, exaggerated responses to stimuli, low heart rate), notes ingestion of edibles/baked goods is the most common risk, notes recreational drugs including marijuana entered ASPCA APCC's top-ten pet toxins list for the first time in 2023, and states treatment “relies on supportive care such as fluids, anti-nausea medications, and anti-anxiety medications until the toxin effects wear off,” with human urine drug screens noted as not very accurate in dogs. A veterinary-hospital summary (Killarney Animal Hospital, accessed 2026-10-25) adds vomiting, salivation, low blood pressure, sometimes fast heart rate, onset 30–90 minutes, symptoms usually lasting up to 72 hours (THC detectable up to five days), and treatment including induced vomiting if very recent and safe, activated charcoal (often every eight hours for the first 24 hours), IV fluids, and monitoring of temperature, blood glucose, and blood pressure; “Death is rare.” Dog sensitivity claim (secondary, commercial veterinary source, prior turn): a Peak Therapeutics history page states dogs have notably high CB1 receptor density in the cerebellum and brainstem, making them both highly responsive to cannabinoids and particularly sensitive to THC. Labelled as a veterinary/medical claim from a commercial source, not Tennessee law. No Tennessee-specific THC toxicity threshold, poisoning-reporting rule, or veterinary malpractice rule for cannabinoids was located. No Tennessee rule requires a veterinarian or owner to report pet THC exposure to any agency. Do not present the ASPCA/veterinary signs or treatment summaries as legal duties.

12.8 Low-THC oil or CBD in schools — no Tennessee school-administration statute for low-THC oil/CBD located

General school medication law — §49-50-1602 (opened, Justia 2025)

Justia §49-50-1602 (title includes “Assistance in self-administration of medications — Administration of glucagons and anti-seizure medications by volunteers — Possession and self-administration of asthma-reliever inhalers — Diabetes care”): (a)(1): “a local board of education or a governing board for a nonpublic school may permit an employee or a person under contract to the board to assist in self-administration of medications, under the following conditions: (A) The student must be competent to self-administer nonprescription or prescription medication with assistance; (B) The student's condition, for which the medication is authorized or prescribed, must be stable; (C) The self-administration of the medication must be properly documented; (D) Guidelines, not inconsistent with this section, for the assistance in self-administration of nonprescription or prescription medications by personnel in the school setting, developed by the departments of health and education and approved by the board of nursing, must be followed; (E) The student's parent or guardian must give permission in writing for school personnel to assist with self-administration of medications. The written permission shall be kept in the student's school records; and (F) Assistance with self-administration shall primarily include storage and timely distribution of medication.” (a)(2): “Health care procedures including administration of medications to students during the school day or at related events shall be performed by appropriately licensed health care professionals in accordance with applicable guidelines of their respective regulatory boards and in conformity with policies and rules of local boards of education or governing boards of nonpublic schools,” with written parent/guardian permission kept in school records. (a)(3): liability protection for reasonable, prudent assistance/performance pursuant to the Departments' guidelines and applicable board policies. (a)(4): the Departments of Education and Health jointly compile an annual report of self-administered medications and health care procedures to the governor and general assembly by 31 October each year. Self-carry exemptions are specific and do not list CBD/low-THC oil: (c) permits possession and self-administration of a prescribed, metered dosage asthma-reliever inhaler by an asthmatic student on written parent/guardian authorisation plus a prescriber's written statement (name/purpose, dosage, times, duration), kept on file with the school nurse/administrator, renewable each school year, revocable for misuse or sharing. (d)(7): on written parent/guardian request and inclusion in the medical management plan and individual health plan (IHP), a student with diabetes may perform blood glucose checks, administer insulin, treat hypoglycaemia/hyperglycaemia, and possess all necessary diabetes supplies including sharps. (e): a student with anaphylaxis may possess and self-administer prescription anaphylaxis medication on school property or at a school-related event/activity if prescribed for that student (prescription label), self-administered per prescription/written prescriber instructions, and the parent provides written authorisation and a liability release (lines 155–171 as opened; balance of (e) not read). (b): trained volunteer school personnel (trained by a registered nurse employed/contracted by the LEA, training repeated annually, competencies documented) may administer glucagon in emergencies and daily insulin based on the student's IHP; if a school nurse is available on site, the nurse provides the service. The section title also references anti-seizure medications by volunteers; that subsection was not read in full for Part 12 [Verify before quoting it]. None of §49-50-1602 as opened mentions CBD, low-THC oil, hemp, or cannabis. Do not extend the asthma-inhaler, diabetes, anaphylaxis, glucagon, or anti-seizure provisions to low-THC oil by analogy.

State Board policy and Department guidelines

State Board of Education Policy 4.205, “Administration of Medication in a School Setting” (adopted/revised 26 January 2018, per the State Board policies index, State Board policies index, accessed 2026-10-25) exists and is the policy district policies cite alongside the Departments' guidelines. The 2018 attachment (Policy 4.205 attachment (2018)) shows volunteer administration of anti-seizure medications (including diazepam gel) in emergencies based on the student's IHP, with RN training per the Departments' Guidelines, CPR training for volunteers, and a witness requirement. Policy 4.205 itself was not opened in full for Part 12, and no provision of it mentioning CBD, hemp, low-THC oil, or cannabis was located [Verify]. Department of Education / Department of Health “Guidelines for Healthcare in a School Setting” (Guidelines for Health Care in School Setting, prior turn) summarises §49-50-1602(a) consistently with the statute text above (competence, stable condition, documentation, Departments' guidelines approved by the Board of Nursing, written parent/guardian permission in school records, assistance primarily storage and timely distribution; training by a registered nurse employed/contracted by the school system, repeated annually, competencies in the personnel file). No Department of Education or Department of Health guidance mentioning CBD, hemp, low-THC oil, or cannabis was located for Part 12. District practice (secondary, illustrative): district policies accessed 2026-10-25 state medication must be delivered to the clinic/main office in person by the parent/guardian or adult designee, unless exempt by Tennessee law to be self-carried and authorised by the prescribing provider (epi-pens, inhalers, diabetic supplies, pancreatic enzymes given as examples); prescription medication in a properly labelled pharmacy bottle; nonprescription medication in an original unopened container; medication counted with the medication designee/school nurse and recorded with signatures. These are local policies, not state law, and they do not address CBD/low-THC oil.

Student possession and discipline — general rule only; no low-THC oil protection located

No Tennessee statute was located authorising a student to possess low-THC oil or CBD on school grounds, authorising a school nurse to store, dispense, or administer it, or protecting a student from discipline for possessing it. Conversely, no Tennessee statute was located that expressly bans low-THC oil possession by a student who meets the §39-17-402(16)(F) documentation conditions. The practical position is therefore: possession meeting the §39-17-402(16)(F) exclusion is not “marijuana” possession under Title 39, Chapter 17, Part 4, but school administration is a separate question governed by §49-50-1602, Policy 4.205, the Departments' guidelines, and local board policy, none of which was found to address low-THC oil/CBD. A school may still treat an unauthorised substance under its local discipline policy; that is a local-policy question, not a state-law answer Part 12 can give. Zero-tolerance expulsion, §49-6-3401(g) (opened, Justia 2025) — Justia §49-6-3401 (history ends 2024, ch. 915, §1): (g)(2) a student is in violation of a zero-tolerance offence and “shall be expelled for a period of not less than one (1) calendar year, except that the director of schools may modify this expulsion on a case-by-case basis” if the student (A) brings to school or is in unauthorised possession on school property of a firearm (18 U.S.C. §921); (B) commits aggravated assault or an assault resulting in bodily injury on a teacher, principal, administrator, other LEA employee, or school resource officer; (C) “Is in unlawful possession of any drug, including any controlled substance, as defined in §§ 39-17-402 — 39-17-415, controlled substance analogue, as defined by § 39-17-454, or legend drug, as defined by § 53-10-101, on school grounds or at a school-sponsored event”; or (D) threatens mass violence (subject to (g)(5) threat assessment). (g)(3): assignment to an alternative school is not prohibited. (g)(4): all other offences' discipline is set by local board policy. (g)(6): “expelled” means removal from the regular school programme at the location of the violation or removal from school attendance altogether, as determined by the school official; a “zero tolerance offense” requires expulsion for at least one calendar year, modifiable only case-by-case by the director of schools or charter-school head. Application note: because (g)(2)(C) turns on “unlawful possession” of a drug/controlled substance, a product that is not a controlled substance under §§39-17-402–415 (e.g. qualifying low-THC oil, or hemp/CBD that is not a controlled substance) does not fall within (g)(2)(C) on its terms — but whether a school treats a particular CBD/HDCP product as falling within it is a fact- and product-specific question Part 12 cannot resolve without a product analysis, and an HDCP possessed by a student under 21 would separately violate §57-7-103(b)(4) (Run 1). State this carefully, not as a guarantee. Pending bill, not law: TN HB0044 (114th General Assembly), as summarised secondarily (BillTrack50, accessed 2026-10-25, prior turn), would allow a student to possess and self-administer rescue seizure medication at school or a school-related activity/event, amending Title 49, Chapter 50, Part 16, with prescription, written parent/guardian authorisation, original sealed pharmacy packaging, liability waiver, and suspension/revocation for misuse or sharing; last action shown: assigned to House Education Administration Subcommittee, 28 January 2025. It is not shown as enacted, and it is a rescue seizure medication bill, not a CBD/low-THC oil bill. Do not present it as a school CBD law. Dated local incident, secondary only, not current law: a WVLT news report (accessed 2026-10-25, prior turn; search metadata places the underlying events in approximately 2017) reports an East Tennessee school district barring a father from administering hemp oil to his son on school grounds (son with involuntary twitching from tardive dyskinesia; “Charlotte's Web” oil from Colorado, less than 0.3% THC, three times a day; district awaiting an answer from the Campbell County District Attorney and requiring a doctor's note, which the father disputed on the ground the oil was not a controlled substance). This is a dated local news account, not Department guidance, not a court decision, and not current policy. If included at all, present it only as a dated illustration of pre-2026 uncertainty, with date and secondary status explicit.

Drug-free school zones — §39-17-432 (opened, Justia 2025)

Justia §39-17-432 (history: Acts 1995, ch. 515, §1; 1998, ch. 1049, §16; 2005, ch. 295, §§1–4; 2020, ch. 803, §§1–9; 2022, ch. 927, §1): (a): intent is to create drug-free zones to provide vulnerable persons an environment to learn, play, and enjoy themselves without the distractions and dangers incident to illegal drug activities, with enhanced sentences as a deterrent. (b)(1): “A violation of §39-17-417, or a conspiracy to violate the section, may be punished one (1) classification higher than is provided in §39-17-417(b)-(i) if the violation or the conspiracy to violate the section occurs: (A) On the grounds or facilities of any school; or (B) Within five hundred feet (500′) of or within the area bounded by a divided federal highway, whichever is less, the real property that comprises a public or private elementary school, middle school, secondary school, preschool, child care agency, public library, recreational center, or park.” Current figure is 500 feet, not 1,000 feet — older secondary summaries and the pre-2020 version described 1,000 feet; the 2020 amendment (ch. 803) is the change to verify in the history if Run 2 narrates it. (b)(2): additional fines on conviction — Class E felony not more than $10,000; Class D $20,000; Class C $40,000; Class B $60,000; Class A $100,000. (b)(3): a person convicted under (b) who is within the prohibited zone of a preschool, childcare center, public library, recreational center, or park “shall not be subject to additional incarceration as a result of [subsection (b)] but may be subject to the additional fines.” (c)(1)–(3): notwithstanding any other law or sentence to the contrary, a defendant sentenced under (b) may be required to serve at least the minimum sentence for the appropriate range; there is a rebuttable presumption the defendant is not required to serve at least the minimum, overcome if the court finds the defendant's conduct exposed vulnerable persons to the distractions and dangers incident to illegal drug activity; if required to serve at least the minimum, sentence-reduction credits must not permit release before full service of the minimum. (d)–(f): Title 40, Chapter 35, Part 5 release eligibility/parole and Title 41, Chapter 1, Part 5 gubernatorial/parole-board release do not authorise release before service of the entire minimum where (c) requires it, but the judge may sentence to any authorised term above the minimum. (h): a court that imposed a sentence for an offence under this section occurring prior to 1 September 2020 may, on motion of the defendant, the district attorney general, or the court's own motion, resentence under (a)–(g) after an evidentiary hearing; the defendant bears the burden to show a shorter confinement period would apply if the offence had occurred on or after 1 September 2020; the court shall not resentence if the new sentence would exceed the original or if resentencing would not be in the interests of justice (considering criminal record including subsequent convictions, behaviour while incarcerated, plea circumstances, and any other relevant factors); indigent defendants get appointed counsel under §40-14-202(c); no motion if a prior (h) motion was denied on the merits, if resentencing would not reduce the overall sentence or lead to earlier release, or if the defendant previously applied for executive clemency on or after 2 December 2021 for the same offence and was denied; (h) does not require a reduction. Scope limit — do not overstate: §39-17-432(b) by its terms applies to violations of §39-17-417 (manufacture/delivery/sale/possession with intent) and conspiracy to violate §39-17-417, not to simple possession under §39-17-418 as a standalone offence. Do not state that simple possession in a school zone is automatically enhanced under §39-17-432. Secondary summaries (Right on Crime; Jeffrey Jackson Law, Nashville, 11 September 2026, accessed 2026-10-25, prior turn) describe the enhancement as applying regardless of knowledge or intent, citing State v. Linville (2022), and describe Nashville District Attorney resentencing waves removing enhancements — secondary, [Verify against Linville and the current (b) text before stating any knowledge/intent rule]. Do not conflate: the HDCP retail school buffer in §57-7-106(b)(3) — 1,000 feet, 31 December 2023 grandfather (Run 1) — measurement method (wall-to-wall vs property-line) Verify on Lexis, do not state as settled — is a different regime from the §39-17-432 drug-free zone (500 feet, criminal enhancement) and must not be conflated with it.

12.9 Cross-checks against the Run 1 baseline (no contradictions found)

  1. Age 21+ for HDCPs (§57-7-103) vs. low-THC oil possession by a minor with documentation (§39-17-402(16)(F)): distinct categories, kept distinct in §§12.2 and 12.8 above.
  2. 1,000-foot HDCP retail buffer (§57-7-106(b)(3)) vs. 500-foot §39-17-432 drug-free zone: distinct regimes, kept distinct in §12.8.
  3. §57-7-111 property-owner/employer rights vs. any claimed tenant/employee protection: no tenant or employee protection for marijuana/HDCP use was located; §§12.5 and 12.6 state the negative.
  4. Schedule I federal status for Tennessee marijuana users vs. Schedule III for state-licensed medical users elsewhere: Tennessee has no state-licensed medical programme, so the Schedule III move and Hemani's Schedule III aside create no Tennessee user class (§12.6).
  5. “Delta-8 banned” shorthand was corrected in Run 1 and is not reintroduced: delta-8/delta-10/HHC/THCv are included in §57-7-102(3)(B) subject to the synthetic-cannabinoid test; Part 12 adds only that smoking hemp (including hemp flower intended for smoking) is age-21+ under Part 15, while HDCPs are governed by Title 57, Chapter 7.
  6. The task brief's suggested citation “§39-17-106” for low-THC oil was not located in Run 1 and is not used here; the low-THC exception is §39-17-402(16)(E)–(F) and §68-7-101.
  7. Cleveland, Ohio smoke-shop rules and Franklin County, Ohio tobacco licensing remain excluded.
